Unknotting nonorientable surfaces
Anthony Conway, Patrick Orson, Mark Powell

Abstract
. Given a nonorientable, locally flatly embedded surface in the 4 4 -sphere of nonorientable genus h h , Massey showed that the normal Euler number lies in { − 2 ​ h , − 2 ​ h + 4 , … , 2 ​ h − 4 , 2 ​ h } \{-2h,-2h+4,\ldots,2h-4,2h\} . We prove that every such surface with knot group of order two is topologically unknotted, provided that the normal Euler number is not one of the extremal values in Massey’s range. When h h is 1 1 , 2 2 , or 3 3 , the same holds even with extremal normal Euler number; the h = 1 h=1 case is due to Lawson. We also study nonorientable embedded surfaces in the 4-ball with boundary a knot K K in the 3-sphere, again where the surface complement has fundamental group of order two and nonorientable genus h h . We prove that any two such surfaces with the same normal Euler number become topologically isotopic, rel. boundary, after adding a single tube to each. If the determinant of K K is trivial, we show that any two such surfaces are isotopic, rel. boundary, again provided that they have non-extremal normal Euler number, or that h h is 1 1 , 2 2 , or 3 3 .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

57K40, 57N35, 57R67.

1.
Introduction

There is an involution of
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
given by complex conjugation
[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]\mapsto[\overline{z}_{0}:\overline{z}_{1}:\overline{z}_{2}]
. Remarkably, the quotient of
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
by this involution is the 4-sphere
[
49
,
42
,
1
]
.
The fixed point set of the involution is the standard
\mathbb{R}P^{2}
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
, and the image of this fixed set under the quotient map is called the
standard positive embedding
of
\mathbb{R}P^{2}
in
S^{4}
. The image of the standard positive embedding under a reflection of
S^{4}
is the
standard negative embedding
. The embeddings are distinguished by their normal Euler number (Definition
2.2
), which is
-2
for the positive embedding and
2
for the negative one.
Additional details on the standard embeddings
\mathbb{R}P^{2}\subseteq S^{4}
were given by Lawson
[
43
,
44
]
; see also Example
2.3
for an alternative construction.

The
nonorientable genus
of a compact, nonorientable surface
F
with at most one boundary component is the dimension of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
Taking connected sums of
h
standard embeddings of
\mathbb{R}P^{2}
realises every normal Euler number in the range
\{-2h,-2h+4,\ldots,2h-4,2h\}
. In fact, Massey
[
48
]
showed that for every locally flat
embedding
F\subseteq S^{4}
of a closed, nonorientable surface of nonorientable genus
h
, the normal Euler number
e(F)
must lie in this range.
Such a surface is said to be topologically
unknotted
if it is topologically isotopic to a connected sum of standardly embedded projective planes.

Every unknotted surface
F\subseteq S^{4}
has knot group
\pi_{1}(S^{4}\setminus F)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
By definition a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
in
S^{4}
will refer to a locally flatly embedded surface
F
, as above, with knot group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We consider the following question, which already appeared, for example, in
[
37
, p. 181]
,
[
36
, p. 55]
, and
[
8
, p. 7]
.

Question 1.1
.

Is every
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface in
S^{4}
topologically unknotted?

Lawson
[
44
]
proved that
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-projective planes are indeed unknotted.
Finashin, Kreck, and Viro
[
19
]
proved that once the nonorientable genus
h
and normal Euler number
e
are fixed, there are only finitely many topological isotopy types of smoothly embedded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces.
Kreck
[
40
]
showed that if a smoothly embedded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface has
(h,e)\in\{(10,\pm 16),(2,0)\}
, then it is topologically unknotted.
These are all the prior instances of progress on Question
1.1
.

We say
e(F)
is
extremal
if
|e(F)|=2h
and
non-extremal
otherwise.
Here is our main result.

Theorem A
.

Let
F\subseteq S^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface of nonorientable genus
h
and normal Euler number
e
.
If
h\leq 3
or if
e
is non-extremal, then
F
is unknotted.

We know of no example where the answer to Question
1.1
is negative.
After this article appeared as a preprint, Pencovitch generalised the calculations from our appendix, and in so doing proved that Theorem
A
also holds when
e
is extremal and
h=4,5

[
58
]
.
The extremal cases for
h\geq 6
remain as interesting open questions.
In fact, Pencovitch showed that the last step in our proof does not go through when
e
is extremal and
h=6,7
, so a new proof method would be needed for these cases.

Remark 1.2
.

One can ask a version of Question
1.1
where one considers smoothly embedded surfaces up to smooth isotopy. In terms of positive answers to such a question, Bleiler and Scharlemann
[
7
]
showed that if a smoothly embedded
\mathbb{R}P^{2}\subseteq S^{4}
has three critical points, then it is smoothly unknotted.

However, in general, this fully smooth version of Question
1.1
is known to have a negative answer.
Finashin, Kreck, and Viro produced an infinite family of smoothly distinct, smoothly embedded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces in
S^{4}
with nonorientable genus
h=10
and normal Euler number
e=\pm 16

[
19
]
.
They achieved this by combining annulus surgery with Donaldson’s theorem to construct infinitely many
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces
\{F_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
with non-diffeomorphic double branched covers.
Kreck later proved that all the
F_{i}

are topologically unknotted
[
40
]
.
Finashin used a similar strategy to produce an infinite family of smoothly embedded, pairwise non-diffeomorphic
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces with
(h,e)=(k,\pm(2k-4))
, for every
k\geq 6

[
18
, Theorem B]
.
More recently, for each
k=7,8,9,10
, Havens produced analogous smooth infinite families of irreducible
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces
[
30
, Theorem 1.3.1]
.
Theorem
A
implies that Finashin’s and Havens’ examples are all topologically unknotted.
After this paper appeared
as a preprint,
Miyazawa
[
54
]
posted a preprint proving the existence of a smooth infinite family of pairwise not smoothly isotopic
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-projective planes (relying on Lawson’s result
[
44
]
for topological isotopy), and Matić-Öztürk-Reyes-Stipsicz-Urzúa used Theorem
A
to give an example of a pair of topologically isotopic, but non-diffeomorphic smoothly embedded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces with
(h,e)=(5,6)

[
50
]
.

1.1.
Surfaces with boundary

Theorem
A
is deduced as a consequence of a uniqueness result for properly, locally flatly embedded, compact, nonorientable surfaces
F\subseteq D^{4}
with boundary a fixed knot in
S^{3}
.
Such an
F
is called a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
in
D^{4}
if it has knot group
\pi_{1}(D^{4}\setminus F)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

In order to state our main result in this setting, we start by recalling a fact about the normal Euler number of nonorientable surfaces in
D^{4}
.
First, if
F\subseteq D^{4}
is a nonorientable surface, then as shown in
[
27
, Theorem 2 and Corollary 5]
and
[
25
, Theorem 6]
, we have the equality
\sigma(K)=\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))+\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}e(F)
, where
\Sigma_{2}(F)
denotes the double branched cover of
F
and
\sigma(K)
denotes the signature of
K
.
If
F
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface of nonorientable genus
h
, then Yasuhara
[
69
, Corollary 1.1.1]
showed that

e(F)\in\{-2h+2\sigma(K),-2h+4+2\sigma(K),\ldots,2h-4+2\sigma(K),2h+2\sigma(K)\}.

We say that
e(F)
is
extremal
if
|e(F)-2\sigma(K)|=2h
.

Theorem B
.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
and the same boundary
K
. Assume that
|\det(K)|=1
.
If either
h\leq 3
or
e
is non-extremal, then
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

We deduce Theorem
A
from Theorem
B
by puncturing the closed surfaces in question i.e. a given closed
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface and the standard unknotted surface with the same nonorientable genus and normal Euler number, and applying Theorem
B
with
K
the unknot.
Theorem
B
is proved in Section
11
.

As we explain in Section
1.5
, our proof of Theorem
B
uses Kreck’s modified surgery
[
41
]
, and owes an intellectual debt to Kreck’s article
[
40
]
, while going further than that article.

Remark 1.3
.

Compared to the work in the smooth category discussed in Remark
1.2
, there are fewer results for smooth surfaces with nonempty boundary. Gompf
[
26
]
exhibited a smooth infinite family of pairwise non smoothly isotopic punctured Klein bottles in
D^{4}
with knot group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and Euler number
0
(and appealed to Kreck
[
40
]
to prove that these
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces are topologically isotopic) and, as we will discuss in more detail in Section
1.4
below, Lipshitz-Sarkar
[
46
]
recently also found examples of exotic surfaces in the
4
-ball with
h=3
.

1.2.
One internal stabilisation is enough

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
, and the same boundary
K
.
Baykur and Sunukjian
[
5
, Theorem 1]
proved that
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
become isotopic after some finite, but
a priori
arbitrarily large, number of internal stabilisations. Here, an
internal stabilisation
of a connected, nonorientable surface
F\subseteq M
in a 4-manifold is obtained by adding a tube. That is, performing ambient surgery on
F
, along an embedded copy of
D^{1}\times D^{2}\subseteq M
with
S^{0}\times D^{2}\subseteq F
and
(\mathring{D}^{1}\times D^{2})\cap F=\emptyset
. This increases the nonorientable genus by two. The fact that homotopy implies isotopy for 1-complexes in 4-manifolds means that, for
F
a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, every tube choice for internal stabilisation is a trivial tube. Therefore adding a tube is the same as taking the connected sum
(D^{4},F)\#(S^{4},T^{2})
, where
T^{2}\subseteq S^{4}
is the standard unknotted torus. Equivalently, one could take the connected sum with an unknotted Klein bottle in
S^{4}
that has trivial normal Euler number; see e.g.
[
5
, Lemma 4]
.

We refine Baykur and Sunukjian’s result, in the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces, to show that in fact one internal stabilisation suffices.

Theorem C
.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
, and the same boundary
K
.
Then
F_{0}\#T^{2}
and
F_{1}\#T^{2}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

Theorem
C
is proved in Section
11
.
Note that stabilising in this way automatically puts us into the case of non-extremal normal Euler number, so the outcome of a single stabilisation when
F
is closed, or when
\partial F=K
with
|\det{K}|=1
, is already covered by Theorems
A
and
B
respectively.
On the other hand, in Theorem
C
there is no assumption on
\det(K)
.
Theorem
C
can also be compared with
[
2
]
, which considers a type of external stabilisation.

1.3.
Involutions of connected sums of projective planes

We describe an application of our results to the study of involutions.
The result of Massey, Kuiper, and Arnol’d
[
49
,
42
,
1
]
, mentioned at the start of the introduction, can be applied to each of the connected summands of
\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P^{2}}
, to show that the conjugation-induced action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P^{2}}
has orbit set
S^{4}
and fixed set the unknotted
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface with nonorientable genus
h
and normal Euler number
e
, where
a+b=h
and
2(a-b)=-e
.
We refer to this action as the
standard involution.

We apply Theorem
A
to obtain a criterion for an involution on
\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P^{2}}
to be topologically conjugate to the standard involution.

Corollary D
.

Let
\tau
be an involution on
Z:=\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P}^{2}
that acts locally linearly and semifreely with fixed point set a nonorientable
1
1

1

In fact, as
Z
is nonspin, a result of Nagami
[
55
, Theorem 1.1]
implies that
\widetilde{F}
is necessarily nonorientable.

surface
\widetilde{F}\subseteq Z
of nonorientable genus
a+b
.
Assume that
Z\setminus\widetilde{F}
is simply-connected and that the pair
(h,e):=(a+b,2(b-a))
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
A
.
Then the involution
\tau
is topologically conjugate to the standard involution on
Z
.

Proof.

For brevity,
we write
Z_{\widetilde{F}}
for the exterior of
\widetilde{F}\subseteq Z
,
X:=Z/\tau
for the orbit space of the involution,
p\colon Z\to X
for the quotient map and
F:=p(\widetilde{F})\subseteq X
for the branch locus.
We also write
X_{F}:=X\setminus\nu F
so that, since the action is semifree, the restriction
p|\colon Z_{\widetilde{F}}\to X_{F}
is a
2
-fold covering map.
Note that
F
and
\widetilde{F}
both have nonorientable genus
h
.

We claim that
X
is homeomorphic to
S^{4}
.
Since
Z_{\widetilde{F}}
is simply-connected and
p|\colon Z_{\widetilde{F}}\to X_{F}
is a
2
-fold covering map, the long exact sequence of a fibration implies that
\pi_{1}(X_{F})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
A Seifert-van Kampen argument then shows that

\pi_{1}(X)=\pi_{1}(X_{F})/\langle\mu_{F}\rangle
is a quotient of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where
\mu_{F}\subseteq X_{F}
denotes a meridian to
F\subseteq X
.
Since the Euler characteristic of a closed
3
-manifold vanishes, a calculation using the additivity of the Euler characteristic yields

\displaystyle 2\chi(X)

\displaystyle=2(\chi(X_{F})+\chi(F))=\chi(Z_{\widetilde{F}})+2\chi(F)

\displaystyle=(\chi(Z)-\chi(\widetilde{F}))+2\chi(\widetilde{F})=\chi(Z)+\chi(\widetilde{F})=(2+h)+(2-h)=4.

We argue that
X
is simply-connected, i.e. that
\mu_{F}
is nontrivial in
\pi_{1}(X_{F})
.
If
\mu_{F}
were trivial in
\pi_{1}(X_{F})
, it would bound an immersed disc, which when glued to the meridional disc for
F
, would produce an immersed sphere in

X
that is geometrically dual to
F
in
X
. This sphere and
F
then represent independent and homologically essential
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficient homology classes.
This would imply, computing with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
coefficients, that
b_{2}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X)\geq 2
and therefore
\chi(X)\geq 1+2+1=4
, contradicting the calculation above.

Since
X
is a closed simply-connected
4
-manifold with
\chi(X)=2
, the 4-dimensional Poincaré conjecture implies that
X\cong S^{4}

[
21
]
.
This establishes the claim.

The claim implies that
Z
is homeomorphic to the 2-fold cover of
S^{4}
branched along the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F\subseteq S^{4}
.
Thus,
[
24
, Theorem 1]
implies that the normal Euler number of
F
is

e(F)=-2\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))=-2\sigma(Z)=-2(a-b)=e.

We apply Theorem
A
to deduce that there a homeomorphism of pairs
\varphi\colon(S^{4},F)\cong(S^{4},U_{h,e})
, where
U_{h,e}
is the unknotted embedding with normal Euler number
e
of the closed nonorientable surface of genus
h
.
This homeomorphism can be lifted to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant homeomorphism of the universal covers of the exteriors and extended to
a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant homeomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}
of the branched covers.
In other words,
\widetilde{\varphi}
conjugates the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\Sigma_{2}(F)
with the standard one on
\Sigma_{2}(U_{h,e})\cong\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P}^{2}
.
∎

1.4.
Surfaces obtained by band moves

Given a knot
K
, we describe a natural source of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces in the
4
-ball with boundary
K
.
Assume that
h
band moves, at least one of which is nonorientable, transform
K
into a knot
J
that bounds a genus
g
orientable surface
\Sigma\subseteq D^{4}
whose exterior
N_{\Sigma}=D^{4}\setminus\nu\Sigma
has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}
; we call
\Sigma
a
\mathbb{Z}
-surface.

We obtain a nonorientable
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
of nonorientable genus
h+2g

by using the
\mathbb{Z}
-surface
\Sigma
to cap off the nonorientable cobordism
C\subseteq S^{3}\times[0,1]
arising from the band moves.
To see that this is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface use the decomposition,
X_{F}=X_{C}\cup_{X_{J}}-N_{\Sigma}
, where
X_{C}
denotes the exterior of
C
and
X_{J}
denotes the exterior of
J
.
Since
\pi_{1}(X_{J})\to\pi_{1}(X_{C})
is surjective,
\pi_{1}(X_{F})
must be a quotient of
\pi_{1}(N_{\Sigma})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Then since
F
is nonorientable, this forces
\pi_{1}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Lipshitz and Sarkar recently used this construction to produce examples of exotic nonorientable surfaces
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}

[
46
]
.
In one of their examples, they perform 3 band moves on the knot
K=12^{n}_{309}
, to arrive at a specific knot
J\subseteq S^{3}
that Hayden-Sundberg
[
31
]
had shown bounds two smoothly distinct
\mathbb{Z}
-discs.
Lipshitz and Sarkar showed that the construction above results in two smoothly embedded
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of nonorientable genus
h=3
and
Euler number
-6
, that are not smoothly isotopic.
Since
\det(K)=1
and
h=3
, Theorem
B
shows that these two surfaces are topologically ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

An alternative route to this conclusion was already available, using the fact that any two
\mathbb{Z}
-discs with the same boundary are ambiently isotopic
[
13
]
.
In future applications, one might wish to cap off by a
\mathbb{Z}
-surface of higher genus than a slice disc. While isotopy uniqueness of such surfaces was also considered previously by two of the authors
[
14
]
, we suggest that Theorem
B
will likely be more easily applicable here, as it avoids some of the more delicate issues involving intersection pairings from that study.

1.5.
Outline of the proof of Theorem
B

Our proof of Theorem
B
uses Kreck’s modified surgery theory
[
41
]
, and as mentioned above our approach was inspired by
[
40
]
.
We review modified surgery theory in Sections
7
and
8
, but for this introduction we introduce some aspects of this theory and explain how it applies (in principle) to proving that two spin
4
-manifolds
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
, with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and (possibly empty) boundary, are homeomorphic.
While we delay detailed definitions to Sections
7
and
8
, we hope that the broad picture will give the reader some idea of what our proof of Theorem
B
entails.

The choice of a spin structure on the
M_{i}
and of an identification
\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
determine
2
-connected maps
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
where the
B:=\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
There is a fibration
\xi\colon B\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
, and in Kreck’s terminology
(B,\xi)
is called the
normal
1
-type
of the
M_{i}
, while the
\overline{\nu}_{i}
are called
normal
1
-smoothings
if they lift the stable normal bundles
M_{i}\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
of the
M_{i}
.

In order to show that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are homeomorphic, the first step in modified surgery is to fix a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
that preserves the normal
1
-smoothings, then find a
5
-dimensional cobordism
(W;M_{0},M_{1})
rel. boundary together with a normal
1
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon W\to B
that extends the
\overline{\nu}_{i}
.
The bordism
W
can be obtained by proving that
(M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{0}\cup-\overline{\nu}_{1})
vanishes in the bordism group
\Omega_{4}(B,\xi)
.

To such a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
, modified surgery associates an obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
in a monoid
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
If this obstruction takes a particularly simple form (i.e. is
elementary
), then
(W,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism.
Since
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a
good
group with trivial Whitehead torsion the
5
-dimensional
s
-cobordism theorem
[
20
, Theorem 7.1A]
then implies that the homeomorphism
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
extends to a homeomorphism
M_{0}\cong M_{1}
.
We refer to
[
6
, Definition 12.12]
for the definition of a good group and to
[
6
, Chapter 19]
for a survey of good groups, including the fact that finite groups are good.

Having described the modified surgery programme to decide whether two spin
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are homeomorphic, we restrict our attention to the case where
M_{0}=X_{F_{0}}
and
M_{1}=X_{F_{1}}
are exteriors of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
.

Definition 1.4
.

Suppose
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
have the same connected boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
and write
S(\nu F_{i})=\partial\overline{\nu}F_{i}\setminus\nu K\subseteq D^{4}
for the sphere bundle of the normal bundle to
F_{i}
, or equivalently the boundary of the closed tubular neighbourhood to
F_{i}
, minus
\nu K
.
Suppose
f\colon S(\nu F_{0})\to S(\nu F_{1})
is a homeomorphism. We say
f
is
\nu
-extendable
if it extends to a homeomorphism
\overline{\nu}F_{0}\cong\overline{\nu}F_{1}
of the closed tubular neighbourhoods that sends
F_{0}
to
F_{1}
.

Suppose the homeomorphism
f
restricts to the identity map
S(\nu K)\to S(\nu K)
. Then we say
f
is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary
if there exists moreover an extension
\overline{\nu}F_{0}\cong\overline{\nu}F_{1}
that restricts to the identity map on
\overline{\nu}K
.

A homeomorphism
S(\nu F_{0})\to S(\nu F_{1})
that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary is a necessary condition for
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
to be ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.
Such a homeomorphism exists if and only if
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
have the same nonorientable genera and relative normal Euler numbers.
Note also that if
f
is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary, then it extends both to a homeomorphism
\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
, which restricts to the identity on the knot exterior, and extends to a homeomorphism
\overline{\nu}F_{0}\to\overline{\nu}F_{1}
.

Since, for
i=0,1
, the exterior
X_{F_{i}}
is spin (it is a codimension zero submanifold of
S^{4}
which is itself spin) and has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, it comes with a normal
1
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon X_{F_{i}}\to B
.
The strategy underlying the proof of Theorem
B
is now as follows.

(1)

We prove that
(X_{F_{0}},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(X_{F_{1}},\overline{\nu}_{1})
are
(B,\xi)
-cobordant.
The key step is in Section
6
, where we use
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-valued quadratic forms due to Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
and Kirby-Taylor
[
38
]
to prove that there exists a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
such that the closed
4
-manifold
M:=X_{F_{0}}\cup_{f}-X_{F_{1}}
is spin, has
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and such that
f
restricts to a homeomorphism
S(\nu F_{0})\cong S(\nu F_{1})
that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary.
Here we use that
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
have the same nonorientable genus
h
and the same normal Euler number
e
.
It is then straightforward to show that
M
has zero signature, and an application of the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence
to calculate
\Omega_{4}(B,\xi)\cong\Omega_{4}^{\operatorname{TOPSpin}}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}
then implies
M
is null-bordant over
(B,\xi)
.

(2)

We prove that the modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary.
Use
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}
to denote the
\mathbb{Z}
-intersection form of the universal cover of
X_{F_{i}}
and
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
for the induced nondegenerate form on
\operatorname{coker}(H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}))
.
The arguments from
[
40
]
, reformulated in the framework from
[
15
]
, show that this obstruction lies in a subset
\ell_{5}(v_{0},v_{1})\subseteq\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
whose definition involves the quadratic form
v_{i}:=(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta_{i})
.
Here
T
stands for the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\langle T\mid T^{2}=1\rangle
and
\theta_{i}:=\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
denotes the quadratic form on
\operatorname{coker}(H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}
whose underlying even form is
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
.
If
|\det(K)|=1
and if either
h\leq 8
or
e
is non-extremal, then Proposition
5.11
shows that
\theta_{0}\cong\theta_{1}
and therefore
v:=v_{0}\cong v_{1}
.
Using Lemma
9.12
, the obstruction in
\ell_{5}(v,v)
further reduces to an obstruction in
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
, where
v_{-}\cong 2\theta_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{h}\times\mathbb{Z}^{h}\to\mathbb{Z}
is obtained by restricting
v
to the
-1
eigenspace.
Work of Crowley-Sixt
[
15
, Section 6.3]
implies that
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
is isomorphic to the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\partial v_{-})
of isometries of the boundary quadratic linking form of
v_{-}
modulo the action of isometries
\operatorname{Aut}(v_{-})
of
v_{-}
.
We appeal to a theorem of Nikulin
[
56
]
to deduce that this orbit space consists of a single orbit when
e
is non-extremal, and therefore every element of
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
, and hence of
\ell_{5}(v,v)
, has an elementary representative.
Here the key point is that when
e
is non-extremal and
h\geq 3
, Proposition
5.11
shows that the quadratic form
\theta_{i}
splits off a hyperbolic summand (even though the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-intersection form does not).
In the appendix we present detailed explicit computations which show, in the extremal cases with
h\leq 3
, that the relevant subset of the
\ell
-monoid,
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
, is trivial, and hence in these cases the modified surgery obstruction is also elementary.
As mentioned above, Pencovitch generalised these calculations to include
h=4,5
and showed that
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
is nontrivial in the extremal case for
h=6,7

[
58
]
.

(3)

Modified surgery theory, and more specifically
[
41
, Theorem 4]
, then ensures that
W
is bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism and, as mentioned above, the
s
-cobordism theorem
[
20
, Theorem 7.1A]
then implies that the homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
extends to a homeomorphism
\Phi\colon X_{F_{0}}\to X_{F_{1}}
.
The
\nu
-extendability condition means that we can extend
\Phi
to a rel. boundary homeomorphism
(D^{4},F_{0})\cong(D^{4},F_{1})
.
It follows from the Alexander trick that orientation-preserving homeomorphisms of

D^{4}
that restrict to the identity on
S^{3}
are rel. boundary isotopic to the identity rel. boundary. We conclude that
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

Remark 1.5
.

The key ingredients in establishing that
v_{0}\cong v_{1}
are the fact that for
i=0,1
, the quadratic form
v_{i}
is determined by the intersection form
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{i})}
of the
2
-fold branched cover, and that if we let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
be the unique integers such that
h=a+b
and
\sigma(Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{i})})=a-b
, then the classification of nonsingular symmetric bilinear forms implies that

(1)

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{i})}\cong(1)^{\oplus a}\oplus(-1)^{\oplus b},

provided
|\det(K)|=1
, and either
h\leq 8
or
e
is non-extremal.

Two questions are of interest if one is to make further progress on Question
1.1
.
The first question is to determine whether (
1
) holds for every extremal
e
.
If
F\subseteq S^{4}
is smoothly embedded, then this follows from Donaldson’s theorem
[
16
]
, whereas a negative answer in the locally flat setting would lead to a negative answer to Question
1.1
.
The second question is whether the subset
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
of
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
is trivial for the forms
v_{-}
that arise in the extremal cases when
h\geq 4
; see Proposition
7.44
. As illustrated in the appendix, the complexity of the computations grows rapidly with
h
, making further computations by hand increasingly challenging.
If (
1
) holds and if
\ell_{5}(v_{-},v_{-})
is trivial for every
h
, then an affirmative answer to Question
1.1
would follow.

The first of these two questions can be made more explicit for
h=9
.

Question 1.6
.

Is there a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F\subseteq S^{4}
with
(h,e)=(9,\pm 18)
whose
2
-fold branched cover has intersection form
E_{8}\oplus(1)
?

As explained above, a positive answer to this question would give an example of a knotted
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface in
S^{4}
and therefore to a negative answer to Question
1.1
.
As mentioned above, Donaldson’s theorem implies that the answer to Question
1.6
is negative if
F
is smoothly embedded.

1.6.
Isotopy of embeddings

In our analysis thus far, we considered submanifolds of
S^{4}
or
D^{4}
up to ambient isotopy. We can instead consider the closely related problem of ambient isotopy of embeddings.
Let
F
be a nonorientable surface with zero or one boundary components, let
N
denote
S^{4}
with zero or one copies of
\mathring{D}^{4}
removed respectively, and let
g_{0},g_{1}\colon F\hookrightarrow N
be proper locally flat embeddings with the same normal Euler number and, in the case
\partial F\cong S^{1}
and
N=D^{4}
, with
g_{0}|_{\partial F}=g_{1}|_{\partial F}
. Suppose that
g_{i}(F)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, for
i=0,1
.
We now consider whether there is an ambient isotopy rel. boundary between
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
.

Given a proper locally flat embedding
g\colon F\hookrightarrow N
,
Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
defined a quadratic refinement
q_{GM}\colon H_{1}(g(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{4}
of the intersection pairing
Q_{g(F)}\colon H_{1}(g(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\times H_{1}(g(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, in terms of the embedding
g
, which we recall in Definition
6.12
.

Theorem E
.

Suppose that for
i=0,1
, the images
F_{i}:=g_{i}(F)

satisfy the hypotheses of one of Theorems
A
,
B
, or
C

(
in the latter case, we assume that
g_{i}(F)
are the stabilised surfaces
)
.
The embeddings
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary if and only if the homeomorphism

\psi:=g_{1}\circ(g_{0})^{-1}\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}

induces an isometry of the Guillou-Marin forms.

Theorem
E
is proved in Section
11
.

Remark 1.7
.

Suppose
g\colon F\hookrightarrow S^{4}
is a smooth embedding such that
g(F)
is a connected sum of standardly embedded projective planes and that
\varphi\colon F\to F
is a self-diffeomorphism.
Hirose
[
33
]
proved that
\varphi
is induced by a self-diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon S^{4}\to S^{4}
sending the image
g(F)
to itself if and only if
g\circ\varphi\circ g^{-1}
induces an isometry of the Guillou-Marin form on
g(F)
.
In the case
N=S^{4}
, it is possible to deduce the case of Theorem
E
corresponding to Theorem
A
by combining Theorem
A
with an application of Hirose’s theorem.
However, since this method would not have applied automatically in the cases when
N=D^{4}
, we gave the independent proof above, taking advantage of the flexibility in our method of proof (Section
6
in particular) to choose our preferred Guillou-Marin form preserving homeomorphism.

Another view on Theorem
E
is that it recovers a topological version of Hirose’s theorem, by applying it in the special case that
g_{0}(F)\subseteq S^{4}
is a connected sum of standardly embedded projective planes and
g_{1}=g_{0}\circ\varphi
for some homeomorphism
\varphi\colon F\to F
.
From this point of view, we have obtained extensions of Hirose’s theorem to surfaces with boundary in the cases where
N=D^{4}
covered by Theorems
B
and
C
, but again only in the topological category. It would be interesting to know whether the smooth analogues also hold, but this cannot be addressed with the inherently topological methods of this paper.

Organisation

In Section
2
, we review the normal Euler number of an embedded surface.
In Section
3
, we review hermitian and quadratic forms.
In Section
4
, we collect some facts about the algebraic topology of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors. Section
5
is concerned with intersection forms.
In Section
6
, we prove that if
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
have the same normal Euler number then it is possible to form the union
M:=X_{F_{0}}\cup-X_{F_{1}}
along a homeomorphism of the boundaries so that
M
is a spin
4
-manifold and
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In Section
7
, we review quasi-formations and the definition of the monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
.
In Section
8
, we recall the set-up of Kreck’s modified surgery theory.
In Section
9
, we formulate a criterion for a class
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
to be elementary.
In Section
10
, we analyse the surgery obstruction and prove that it is elementary.

Conventions

We work in the topological category with locally flat embeddings unless otherwise stated.
From now on, all manifolds are assumed to be compact, connected, and based; if a manifold has a nonempty boundary, then the basepoint is assumed to lie in the boundary.

Let
F
denote a nonorientable, compact surface with connected, nonempty boundary and nonorientable genus
h
.
The
standard cell structure
for
F
consists of a 0-cell
e^{0}
and a 1-cell
e^{1}_{\partial}
to construct
\partial F
, then
h
1-cells
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{h}
attached to the 0-cell, followed by a 2-cell attached via
e^{1}_{\partial}\cdot\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{h}\gamma_{h}
.
Note that
F
deformation retracts onto the subcomplex
e^{0}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{h}\gamma_{i}\simeq\bigvee^{h}S^{1}
.

For a space
X
, we write
H_{n}(X)
for the
n
-th singular homology group of
X
with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients. Any other coefficient system will be included in the notation.
We write
\subseteq
for inclusions of subsets and
\subset
to indicate proper subsets.
The notation
\Sigma_{2}(-)
is used exclusively for
2
-fold branched covers, typically of a knot in
S^{3}
or a surface in
D^{4}
.

If
R
is a ring with involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
and
M
is a left
R
-module then

M^{*}
denotes the dual of
M
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,R)
,
which is given a left
R
-module structure using the involution on
R
in the usual way.

Open Access

For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a CC-BY Creative Commons attribution license to this version, and will do the same to any author-accepted manuscript arising.

2.
The normal Euler number

In this section, we discuss the normal Euler number of a properly embedded surface.
The normal Euler number, an integer, is an isotopy invariant of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces, which can distinguish between those with the same nonorientable genus.

We start by recalling the general definition of Euler numbers with twisted coefficients.
Suppose
(X,Y)
is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair, with both
X
and
Y
compact and connected.
Let
\xi=(\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E\xrightarrow{\pi}X)
be a rank
n
vector bundle.
The example to keep in mind is that of the normal bundle of a properly embedded surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
.
Write
D(\xi)
and
S(\xi)
for the disc and sphere bundles of
\xi
.
Consider the obstruction-theoretic problem of finding a section
s
for the sphere bundle
S(\xi)
extending a given section
s^{\prime}
on the restriction of
S(\xi)
to
Y
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Id}_{X}}

The primary obstruction to this problem is the
relative Euler class
, which takes values in the local coefficient system given by the homotopy groups of the fibres
\epsilon(\xi,s^{\prime})\in H^{n}(X,Y;\{\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x})\})
.
Write
x_{0}\in Y
for a basepoint and
\pi_{1}(X):=\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
.
The monodromy around a loop either preserves or reverses an orientation on
S(\xi)_{x_{0}}
, and this determines a homomorphism
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x_{0}}))
.
This homomorphism is used
to form a twisted coefficient system for
X
, giving groups
H^{n}(X,Y;\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x_{0}}))
, canonically isomorphic to
H^{n}(X,Y;\{\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x})\})
; see
[
67
, page 288, Theorem 4.9]
.

A cohomology class
w\in H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
determines a homomorphism
\pi_{1}(X)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z})
by assigning, to a loop
\gamma
, multiplication by
w([\gamma])\in\{\pm 1\}
. This in turn determines a twisted coefficient system
\mathbb{Z}^{w}
. We may identify
\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)}
with
S(\xi)_{x_{0}}
as
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
-modules by choosing an isomorphism of groups
\mathbb{Z}\cong\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x_{0}})
.
Via either such choice of identification, we see that the monodromy-induced map
\rho
and the homomorphism determined by
w_{1}(\xi)
agree.
Thus, once such a choice is specified, we consider the relative Euler class
\epsilon(\xi,s^{\prime})
to lie in
H^{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})
.

Now suppose that
X
is a compact
n
-manifold with (possibly empty) boundary
Y
.
There is an isomorphism
H_{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(TX)})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and a choice of generator
[X]
is called a
twisted
(
relative
)
fundamental class
.
Suppose the total space
E
of
\xi
is orientable, and fix a fundamental class for this total space.
A choice of a twisted fundamental class
[X]
determines local orientations for
X
.
Together with the orientation on the total space of
\xi
, we infer an orientation on the fibres of
\xi
and hence an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\cong\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x_{0}})
.
Thus we obtain an Euler class
\epsilon(\xi,s^{\prime})\in H^{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})
.
Noting that
w_{1}(TX)+w_{1}(\xi)=w_{1}(E)=0
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued cap product

-\cap-\colon H_{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(TX)})\times H^{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\to H_{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(TX)+w_{1}(\xi)})=H_{0}(X)=\mathbb{Z}.

We define the
relative Euler number

e(\xi,s^{\prime})
of
\xi
to be the integer given by the cap product

e(\xi,s^{\prime}):=[X]\cap\epsilon(\xi,s^{\prime})\in H_{0}(X)=\mathbb{Z}.

A priori
this depends on a choice of
[X]
and a choice of orientation on
\xi
. A change in the choice of
[X]
switches the sign of
[X]
and also switches the identification
\mathbb{Z}\cong\pi_{n-1}(S(\xi)_{x_{0}}),
thus overall these cancel in the computation of
e(\xi,s^{\prime})
, and the relative Euler number is independent of the choice of
[X]
. Tracking a change in the choice of orientation on the total space of
\xi
through the construction above, shows this will ultimately change
e(\xi,s^{\prime})
to
-e(\xi,s^{\prime})
.

Remark 2.1
.

In practice, (relative) Euler numbers may be computed by looking at how generic sections of the bundle intersect the 0-section in the total space. Suppose
\xi
is a vector bundle over a compact, connected
n
-manifold with boundary
(X,Y)
, and that the total space of
\xi
is oriented. Fix a nonvanishing section
s^{\prime}\colon Y\to E|_{Y}
.
Let
s\colon X\to E
be any section that restricts to a section isotopic to
s^{\prime}
on
Y
and meets the image of the 0-section
s_{0}(X)
transversely. At the preimage in
X
of each intersection
x\in s_{0}(X)\pitchfork s(X)
, arbitrarily choose a local orientation for
X
(this corresponds to choosing one of the two twisted fundamental classes
[X]\in H_{n}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(TX)})
). This induces an orientation on
T_{x}s_{0}(X)\oplus T_{x}s(X)
, and we assign a sign to
x
by comparing this induced orientation to the ambient orientation on
T_{x}E
. The signed count of these intersections is independent of the local orientation choices because the choice appears in the orientation of both
T_{x}s_{0}(X)
and
T_{x}s(X)
.

It is known that the Euler number
e(\xi,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}
is equal to the signed count of these intersections.
This follows because the relative Euler number is equal to the algebraic self-intersection number of the
0
-section
s_{0}(X)
in the total space.
In the level of generality we are working, this is a somewhat intricate exercise in elementary algebraic topology and we will not perform it here.
When
Y=\emptyset
, and for oriented base space and bundle, this procedure is carried out in
[
10
, Proposition 12.8]
, for example. By working with twisted Poincaré duality, and relative to the subspace
Y
, the method for the closed, oriented case can be adapted to the situation we are considering, where both the base space and bundle are nonorientable (and thus the total space of the bundle is orientable).

Definition 2.2
.

The
normal Euler number

e(F)\in\mathbb{Z}
of an embedded closed surface
F\subseteq S^{4}
is the Euler number of its normal bundle
\nu F
, whose total space is oriented using the fixed, standard orientation on
S^{4}
.

The
normal Euler number

e(F)\in\mathbb{Z}
of a properly embedded compact surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
with connected nonempty boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
is the relative Euler number
e(\xi,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}
of its normal bundle
\nu F
, whose total space is oriented using the fixed, standard orientation on
D^{4}
, and where
s^{\prime}\colon K\to\nu K
denotes a nonvanishing section (which, in this case, is equivalent to a longitude of
K
) corresponding to the Seifert framing of
K
.

Example 2.3
.

In the introduction, we defined the standard embeddings
\mathbb{R}P^{2}\subseteq S^{4}
using the involution of
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
given by complex conjugation.
An alternative construction for the positive/negative standard embedding
\mathbb{R}P^{2}\subseteq S^{4}
, up to ambient isotopy, is as follows. Take an unknotted Möbius band in
S^{3}\subseteq S^{4}
, with one positive/negative half-twist, push its interior into the northern hemisphere of
S^{4}
, then cap it off with the standardly embedded
2
-disc
D
in the other hemisphere. With this description one can compute that the normal Euler number of the standard positive embedding is
-2
and the normal Euler number of the standard negative embedding is
+2
.
To compute this, first extend a nowhere zero section of the normal bundle of the pushed-in Möbius band to a generic section of the normal bundle of
D
. This gives a generic section of the embedded
\mathbb{R}P^{2}
, where geometric intersections with the 0-section happen only on
D
. One can compute this intersection number between
D
and its generic section by considering linking between their intersections with
S^{3}
.

For example, there is a nowhere zero section of the pushed-in Möbius band that restricts on the boundary unknot to be a pushoff in the outwards facing normal direction to
\mathcal{M}\subseteq S^{3}
. For the positive case, this is shown in Figure
1
. This pushoff links +2 with the unknot, so that the generic section over
D
in the southern hemisphere has intersection number
-2
with the 0-section. Here the minus sign arises because the southern hemisphere uses the opposite orientation to the standard one.

Figure 1.
Left: The unknotted, positively twisted Möbius band
\mathcal{M}\subseteq S^{3}
, before its interior is pushed into the northern hemisphere of
S^{4}
. Right: The red curve is the pushoff in the outwards-facing normal direction of
\mathcal{M}\subseteq S^{3}
. The pushoff direction is indicated by arrows. The pushoff has linking number +2 with the boundary of the Möbius band. The pushoff is the restriction of a non-vanishing section of the normal bundle of the pushed-in Möbius band.

It also follows from this description that the exteriors of these standard embeddings admit a handle decomposition with a single 0-handle and a single 1-handle, and therefore have fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
This is the easiest way to see that all unknotted nonorientable surfaces, regardless of their nonorientable genus, are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces.

Massey proved the following theorem, as mentioned in the introduction. In particular it implies that the normal Euler number of a closed surface in
S^{4}
is always even.

Theorem 2.4

(Massey
[
48
]
)
.

Let
F\subseteq S^{4}
be an embedded, closed, nonorientable surface of nonorientable genus
h
. The normal Euler number of
F
lies in the set
\{2h-4,2h,2h+4,\dots,4-2h\}
. For each
h\geq 1
, this set is in bijective correspondence with the isotopy classes of the standard embeddings.

Technically speaking, Massey proved Theorem
2.4
in the smooth category, but his proof extends to the topological category.
For example, one of the crucial points in the proof is the relation between the Euler number
e(F)
and
\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))
, the signature of the
2
-fold branched cover.
This relation also holds in the topological category
[
24
, Theorem 1]
.
Alternatively, since, as noted in
[
51
, Corollary after Theorem 5.2]
, Massey’s theorem follows from a congruence of Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
, one can alternatively note that the Guillou-Marin congruence holds in the topological category; see Theorem
6.18
.

In fact, Massey’s theorem can be generalised to surfaces with boundary, once one takes into account the signature
\sigma(K)
of the knot
K=\partial F
.
The outcome reads as follows.

Theorem 2.5
.

If
F\subseteq D^{4}
is a nonorientable surface with boundary a knot
K
,
then

(2)

\sigma(K)=\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))+\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}e(F),

and the Euler number lies in the following range, where
h
denotes the nonorientable genus of
F
:

e(F)\in\{-2h+2\sigma(K),-2h+4+2\sigma(K),\ldots,2h-4+2\sigma(K),2h+2\sigma(K)\}.

Proof.

The equality in (
2
) can be found in
[
27
, Theorem 2 and Corollary 5]
; see also
[
25
, Theorem 6]
.
The range of
e(F)
is due to Yasuhara
[
69
, Corollary 1.1.1]
.
∎

3.
Hermitian and quadratic forms

In Subsection
3.1
we recall terminology regarding hermitian and quadratic forms.
In Subsection
3.2
, we collect some important facts about
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.

3.1.
Quadratic forms

We begin by recalling some notation and terminology regarding hermitian and quadratic forms, which we will use throughout the article.
References include
[
59
]
and
[
15
, Subsection 3.2]
.

Let
R
be a ring with involution and let
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.
Given an
R
-module
P
, consider the involution

\varepsilon\top\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,P^{*})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,P^{*}),\phi\mapsto(x\mapsto(y\mapsto\varepsilon\overline{\phi(y)(x)})).

The quadratic and symmetric
Q
-groups
are then respectively defined as

Q_{\varepsilon}(P):=\operatorname{coker}(1-\varepsilon\top)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ Q^{\varepsilon}(P):=\operatorname{ker}(1-\varepsilon\top).

We will often abbreviate
Q_{\pm}(P):=Q_{\pm 1}(P)
.
We also note that for
P=R
, it is customary to identify
Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
with the quotient
R/\{r-\varepsilon\overline{r}\mid r\in R\}.

Definition 3.1
.

(i)

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
is a pair
(P,\psi)
, where
P
is a stably free
R
-module and
\psi\in Q_{\varepsilon}(P)
.

(ii)

An
\varepsilon
-hermitian form
is a pair
(P,\lambda)
, where
P
is a stably free
R
-module and
\lambda\in Q^{\varepsilon}(P)
.

(iii)

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic form is
nonsingular
if its
symmetrisation

(1+\varepsilon\top)\psi
is an isomorphism.

(iv)

An
isometry

h\colon(P,\psi)\to(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
between quadratic forms consists of an isomorphism
h\colon P\to P^{\prime}
such that
h^{*}\psi^{\prime}h=\psi\in Q_{\varepsilon}(P)
. Isometries of hermitian forms are defined similarly.

Remark 3.2
.

As is customary in surgery theory, we will go back and forth between thinking of quadratic forms (resp. hermitian forms) as (equivalence classes of)
R
-linear maps
P\to P^{*}
on the one hand and pairings
P\times P\to Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
(resp. pairings
P\times P\to R
) on the other.
We will also frequently use the fact that the data of a quadratic form
(P,\psi)
is equivalent to the data of a triple
(P,\lambda,\mu)
where
\lambda\colon P\times P\to R
is a hermitian form and
\mu\colon P\to Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
is a quadratic refinement of
\lambda
, a notion whose definition we recall next; see e.g.
[
47
, Remark 8.78]
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
q\colon R\to Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
be the quotient map and let
s\colon Q_{\varepsilon}(R)\to R
be a section of
q
.
A function
\mu\colon P\to Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
is a
quadratic refinement
of a hermitian form
\lambda\colon P\times P\to R
if

(i)

\mu(x+y)-\mu(x)-\mu(y)=q(\lambda(x,y))
for every
x,y\in P
,

(ii)

\lambda(x,x)=s(\mu(x))+\varepsilon\overline{s(\mu(x))}
for every
x\in P
,

(iii)

\mu(rx)=r\mu(x)\overline{r}\in Q_{\varepsilon}(R)
for every
x\in P
and
r\in R
.

Note that the right hand side of (ii) does not depend on the choice of section
s
.

Definition 3.4
.

For a stably free
R
-module
L
, the
standard hyperbolic
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
is

H_{\varepsilon}(L)=\left(L\oplus L^{*},\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right]\right).

A quadratic form
(P,\psi)
is called
hyperbolic
if it is isometric to
H_{\varepsilon}(L)
for some
L
.

Definition 3.5
.

A
sublagrangian
of an
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
(P,\psi)
is a direct summand
j\colon L\hookrightarrow P
that satisfies
j^{*}\psi j=0\in Q_{\varepsilon}(L)
.
A sublagrangian
j\colon L\hookrightarrow P
satisfies
j(L)\subseteq L^{\perp}
, where the module
L^{\perp}=\ker(j^{*}(1+\varepsilon\top)\psi)
is called the
annihilator
of
j\colon L\hookrightarrow P
.
A sublagrangian
j\colon L\hookrightarrow P
is a
lagrangian
if it satisfies
L=L^{\perp}
.

Remark 3.6
.

When
P
is only assumed to be projective, this definition requires the additional assumption that
j^{*}(1+\varepsilon\top)\psi
is surjective; cf.
[
59
, p. 65]
.
This is relevant to the study of projective
L
-theory, see e.g.
[
65
, page 260]
and the references within.
As we are working in the setting that
P
is stably free, this condition follows automatically.

We conclude this subsection by discussing the relationship between quadratic and hermitian forms.
Given an
R
-module
P
, the map
1+\varepsilon\top
induces an
R
-linear map

(3)

1+\varepsilon\top\colon Q_{\varepsilon}(P)\to Q^{\varepsilon}(P).

Lemma 3.7
.

When
\varepsilon=1
and
P
is projective, the symmetrisation map (
3
) is injective.

Proof.

Assume first that
P
is moreover free. Then an element in the kernel of (
3
) is represented by a skew-hermitian matrix
A
over
R
. A skew-hermitian matrix may be written
A=B^{T}-\overline{B}=(1-\top)B^{T}
, where
B
is the upper triangle of
A
. Thus
A
determines the 0 element of
Q_{+}(P)
. It follows that in this case, the map (
3
) is injective.

Now drop the assumption the
P
is free. As
P
is projective, there exists an
R
-module
Q
such that
P\oplus Q
is free. Suppose
\psi\colon P\to P^{*}
represents an element of
\ker(1+\top\colon Q_{+}(P)\to Q^{+}(P))
. Then
\psi\oplus 0
represents an element of
\ker(1+\top\colon Q_{+}(P\oplus Q)\to Q^{+}(P\oplus Q))
. As
P\oplus Q
is free, by the argument in the first paragraph,
\psi\oplus 0=(1-\top)\eta
for some
\eta\colon P\oplus Q\to(P\oplus Q)^{*}\cong P^{*}\oplus Q^{*}
. Writing
i\colon P\hookrightarrow P\oplus Q
for the inclusion, we have

\psi=i^{*}(\psi\oplus 0)i=i^{*}(1-\top)\eta i=i^{*}(1-\top)\eta i=(1-\top)(i^{*}\eta i),

and so we have that
\psi
determines the 0 element of
Q_{+}(P)
.
∎

We obtain the following consequences.

Corollary 3.8
.

If a
(+1)
-hermitian form
(P,\lambda)
admits a quadratic refinement, then it admits a unique quadratic refinement. Moreover, if
L\subseteq P
is a direct summand and
\lambda
vanishes on
L
, then
L
is a sublagrangian for that unique quadratic form with symmetrisation
(P,\lambda)
.

Proof.

The first claim is immediate from Lemma
3.7
, as
P
is stably free, therefore projective (indeed the claim holds for forms defined more generally on projective modules).
Indeed, if two quadratic forms
(P,\psi_{1}),(P,\psi_{2})
admit
(P,\lambda)\in Q^{+}(P)
as a common symmetrisation, then Lemma
3.7
implies that
(P,\psi_{1})=(P,\psi_{2})\in Q_{+}(P)
and therefore the corresponding quadratic refinements agree; here we use Remark
3.2
to go back and forth between quadratic forms and quadratic refinements.

Write
(P,\psi)
for the unique quadratic form such that
(1+\top)\psi=\lambda
.
The second claim comes from the fact that as
L
is a direct summand of
P
, it is also projective. The vanishing of
\lambda
on
L
is equivalent to saying
0=j^{*}(1+\top)\psi j=(1+\top)j^{*}\psi j\in Q^{+}(L)
.
As
L
is projective, Lemma
3.7
shows
0=j^{*}\psi j\in Q_{+}(L)
, and so
L
is a sublagrangian for
(P,\psi)
.
∎

3.2.
Modules over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]

Since we will be soon describing the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module structure of various homology groups, we start by recalling some facts about
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.
From now on we write
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\langle T\mid T^{2}=1\rangle
.

Lemma 3.9
.

For a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
P
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

P
is free,

(2)

P
is stably free,

(3)

P
is projective.

Proof.

For finitely generated modules over any ring, free implies stably free, which in turn implies projective.
A theorem of Jacobinski
[
34
]
ensures that stably free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules are moreover free; see also
[
35
]
.
For any finite group
G
,
every finitely generated (f.g.) projective
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module is stably free if and only if the reduced projective class group
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[G])
is trivial
[
66
, Lemma 2.1, Definition 2.3, and Example 2.4]
.
Rim proved that for a prime
p
, the group
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{p}])
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[\xi_{p}])
, where
\xi_{p}
denotes the
p
-th primitive root of unity
[
63
]
.
A result of Milnor then implies that
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[\xi_{p}])
is isomorphic to the ideal class group of
\mathbb{Z}[\xi_{p}]

[
53
, Section 1, Corollary 1.11]
.
This latter group is trivial for
p=2
since
\mathbb{Z}[\xi_{2}]=\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus a f.g.
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module is projective if and only if it is stably free.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Every f.g.
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
H
that is torsion-free as an abelian group decomposes as
P\oplus H_{+1}\oplus H_{-1}
, where
P
is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-free,
H_{+1}
has the

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
+1
, and
H_{-1}
has the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
-1
.

Proof.

Reiner
[
62
]
proved that every f.g.
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
H
that is torsion-free as an abelian group decomposes as
P\oplus H_{+1}\oplus H_{-1}
, where
H_{\pm 1}
are as in the statement of the lemma and
P
is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-projective.
Summands of f.g. modules are f.g. and Lemma
3.9
therefore implies that
P
is in fact
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-free.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

In what follows,
\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}
denotes
\mathbb{Z}
with the
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}],\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
-bimodule structure where both left and right module structures are determined by stipulating that
T
induces multiplication by
\pm 1
.
One verifies for example the useful isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}(\mathbb{Z}_{\pm},\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}
.

4.
The algebraic topology of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors

In this section we collect some facts about the algebraic topology of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors, which we will need in later proofs.
Subsections
4.1
and
4.2
are concerned with the homology of the boundary of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors.
In Subsections
4.3
and
4.4
we study the homology of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors and the corresponding branched covers.

Before getting started we record a result that we will use frequently.
If
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\langle T\mid T^{2}=1\rangle
acts on a space
X
, its rational homology groups split into eigenspaces for the automorphism induced by
T
.
The eigenvalues are necessarily
\pm 1
and we write
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))\subseteq H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})
for the corresponding eigenspaces.
Note that
H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})=\mathcal{E}_{+}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))
because every
x\in H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})
decomposes as
x=\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{2}$}(x+Tx)+\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{2}$}(x-Tx)
with
\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{2}$}(x\pm Tx)\in\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})).

Lemma 4.1
.

Fix
i\geq 0
.
If
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts on a space
X
, then
\mathcal{E}_{+}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))\cong H_{i}(X/\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Q})
and therefore

H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{i}(X/\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Q})\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})).

Proof.

Consider the set
H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
of fixed points of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action on
H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})
.
A transfer map argument
[
9
, Chapter III, Theorem 7.2]
shows that fixed set of the induced action on the rational homology is isomorphic to the rational homology of the orbit space, i.e.
H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cong H_{i}(X/\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Q})
.
We therefore obtain the following sequence of isomorphisms:

\mathcal{E}_{+}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))=H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cong H_{i}(X/\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{Q}).

The final sentence now follows from the decomposition
H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q})=\mathcal{E}_{+}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Q}))
.
∎

4.1.
The homology of circle bundles over nonorientable surfaces

Given a properly embedded nonorientable surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
with boundary a knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
, the boundary of the exterior
X_{F}:=D^{4}\setminus\nu F
decomposes as
\partial X_{F}=X_{K}\cup-\mathring{Y}
where
X_{K}
is the exterior of
K
and
\mathring{Y}
is an oriented
3
-manifold that has the structure of a circle bundle over
F
(see Notation
1
for details).
We calculate the homology of
\mathring{Y}
as well as the homology of circle bundles over closed nonorientable surfaces.

We first introduce some notation we will use throughout this section.

Notation 1
.

Let
F
be a closed, nonorientable surface of nonorientable genus
h
and let
\xi
be a rank 2 vector bundle over
F
with oriented total space and Euler number
e
.
Decompose
{F}=D^{2}\cup_{S^{1}}\mathring{{F}}
and write
\mathring{\xi}
for the restriction to
\mathring{F}
.
Write
Y
and
\mathring{Y}
for the respective total spaces of the associated
S^{1}
-bundles
S(\xi)
and
S(\mathring{\xi})
, and let
p\colon Y\to F
and
\mathring{p}\colon\mathring{Y}\to\mathring{F}
denote the projection maps.
Let
\mathring{s}
be a section of
\mathring{\xi}
, which exists because
\mathring{\xi}
is rank 2 and
\mathring{F}
is homotopy equivalent to a 1-complex.

Proposition 4.2
.

We have a commuting diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{p}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathring{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{s}_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{H_{1}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{0}

where the vertical maps are inclusion induced, the top row is split by
\mathring{s}_{*}
, and the bottom is split if and only if
e
is even.
Moreover, we have the following homology calculations.

•

If
e
is even,
there exists a unique map
\sigma\colon H_{1}(F)\to H_{1}(Y)
that splits the bottom row and satisfies
\iota_{Y}\circ\mathring{s}_{*}=\sigma\circ\iota_{F}
.
In particular, there are isomorphisms

H_{1}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(F)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\mathring{F}).

In both cases the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-summand is generated by an
S^{1}
-fibre of
S(\xi)
.

•

If
e
is odd, then

H_{1}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\mathring{F}).

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-summand is generated by an
S^{1}
-fibre of
S(\xi)
whereas the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
summand fits into the nonsplit short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to{\underbrace{H_{1}(Y)}_{\cong\,\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}}}\xrightarrow{p_{*}}{\underbrace{H_{1}(F)}_{\cong\,\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}}}\to 0

in which the left hand side
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is generated by an
S^{1}
-fibre of
S(\xi)
.

Proof.

Consider the total space
N
of the disc bundle
D(\xi)
over
F
.
We note that
H_{1}(N,Y)=0
and
H_{2}(N,Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generated by a
D^{2}
-fibre of
D(\xi)
.
Indeed since
\partial N=Y
and
N
deformation retracts onto
F
, Poincaré duality implies that

H_{i}(N,Y)\cong H^{4-i}(N)\cong H^{4-i}(F)

which vanishes for
i=1
and is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for
i=2
.
In the punctured case, write
\mathring{N}
for the total space of
D(\mathring{\xi})
, so that excision gives
H_{i}(\mathring{N},\mathring{Y})\cong H_{i}(N,Y)
for all
i\geq 0.

Since
N
(resp.
\mathring{N}
) deformation retracts via the bundle projection onto
F
(resp.
\mathring{F}
), we have
H_{1}(N)\cong H_{1}(F)
(resp.
H_{1}(\mathring{N})\cong H_{1}(\mathring{F})
).
Consider the following commutative diagram in which the rows (resp. columns) arise from the exact sequences of the pairs
(\mathring{N},\mathring{Y})
and
(N,Y)
(resp.
(F,\mathring{F})
and
(Y,\mathring{Y})
):

\textstyle{H_{2}(Y,\mathring{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H_{2}(F,\mathring{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{p}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathring{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{s}_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{H_{1}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{0.}

Here the map
\mathring{s}_{*}
splits
\mathring{p}
and is induced by the choice of section
\mathring{s}\colon\mathring{F}\to\mathring{Y}
.
It remains to prove the statements related to the splitting
\sigma
.

By excision,
H_{2}(F,\mathring{F})\cong H_{2}(D^{2},S^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
generated by a choice of relative fundamental class
[D^{2}]
.
Using our standard cell structure for
\mathring{F}
, with
1
-cells
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{h}
, we see that
H_{1}(\mathring{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}
. We choose a basis with first basis element
[\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\dots\gamma_{h}]
to obtain a decomposition
H_{1}(\mathring{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
, so that
\partial([D^{2}])=(2,\mathbf{0})
.
Using the splitting of the top sequence we write
H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\mathring{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
. Again by excision, we know that
H_{2}(Y,\mathring{Y})\cong H_{2}(S^{1}\times D^{2},S^{1}\times S^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, generated by
[\{1\}\times D^{2}]
.

We claim that
\partial([\{1\}\times D^{2}])=(\overline{e},2,\mathbf{0})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
, where
\overline{e}
is the Euler number
e
of
\xi
reduced modulo 2.
The last two entries are necessitated by commutativity of the top right square.
To see that the first entry is the Euler number mod 2, recall that the Euler number is the obstruction to extending the section
\mathring{s}\colon\mathring{F}\to\mathring{Y}\subseteq Y=S(\xi)\subseteq D(\xi)
to a nonvanishing section on all of
F
.
Indeed, if we extend, the Euler number equals the number of transverse intersections of any section over all of
F
with the zero section.
This means that the boundary of
\{1\}\times D^{2}\subseteq S^{1}\times D^{2}\cong Y\setminus\mathring{Y}
maps to
\pm e
copies of the circle fibre.
This concludes the proof of the claim.

Assume
e
is even.
A chase in the following diagram shows that
\sigma
is well-defined:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(0,2,\mathbf{0})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(2,\mathbf{0})}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{p}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathring{s}_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{H_{1}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{0.}

In more detail, the map
\sigma
is defined to make the right hand square commute; i.e.
\sigma(x)=\iota_{Y}\circ\mathring{s}_{*}(\mathring{x})
where
\iota_{F}(\mathring{x})=x
.
To check that
\sigma
is indeed a splitting, given
x\in H_{1}(F)
and
\mathring{x}\in H_{1}(\mathring{F})
such that
\iota_{F}(\mathring{x})=x
. Then

p\circ\sigma(x)=p\circ\sigma\circ\iota_{F}(\mathring{x})=p\circ\iota_{Y}\circ\mathring{s}_{*}(\mathring{x})=\iota_{F}\circ\mathring{p}\circ\mathring{s}(\mathring{x})=\iota_{F}(\mathring{x})=x.

The uniqueness of
\sigma
follows similarly: if
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfies
\iota_{Y}\circ\mathring{s}_{*}=\sigma^{\prime}\circ\iota_{F}
, then for any
x\in H_{1}(F)
and any
\mathring{x}\in H_{1}(\mathring{F})
with
\iota_{F}(\mathring{x})=x
, we have
\sigma(x)=\iota_{Y}\circ\mathring{s}_{*}(\mathring{x})=\sigma^{\prime}(\iota_{F}(\mathring{x}))=\sigma^{\prime}(x)
.

If
e
is odd, then we deduce that
H_{1}(Y)=\operatorname{Im}(\iota_{Y})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}/\langle(1,2,0)\rangle
, which is itself isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
.
The proposition follows.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

The nontrivial integral homology groups of
\mathring{Y}
are

H_{0}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h},\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{2}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},

and the nontrivial integral homology groups of
Y
are

H_{0}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(Y)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $e$ is odd}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $e$ is even,}\end{cases}\quad H_{2}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},\quad H_{3}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

Since
\mathring{Y}
is connected
H_{0}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Proposition
4.2
proves that
H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h}
.
Since
\mathring{Y}
is a
3
-manifold with torus boundary, we have
H_{3}(\mathring{Y})=0
and
0=\chi(\mathring{Y})=1-h+b_{2}(\mathring{Y})
.
On the other hand, Poincaré duality and the universal coefficient theorem imply that
H_{2}(\mathring{Y})
is free.
Combining these facts shows that
H_{2}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
, as claimed.
An analogous argument gives the closed case.
∎

Construction 4.4
.

Assume the Euler number
e
of the bundle
\xi
is even, so that Proposition
4.2
gives

H_{1}(Y)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(F)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ H_{1}(\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\mathring{F}),

where in both cases the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-summand is generated by an
S^{1}
-fibre of
S(\xi)
.
Projecting to this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-summand leads to surjections
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(\mathring{Y})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-covers
Y^{\varphi}
and
\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}
corresponding to the kernels of these epimorphisms.
From now on when we write
H_{i}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
and
H_{i}(\mathring{Y};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
, we will mean the homology groups of these covers, viewed as
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.

Lemma 4.5
.

When the Euler number
e
of
\xi
is even, it is twice that of the rank 2 bundle
\xi^{\varphi}
associated with
Y^{\varphi}
.

Proof.

Consider
N:=N_{e,h}
, the
D^{2}
-bundle over
F
with Euler number
e
.
This bundle satisfies
\partial N=Y
.
Note that
N\setminus\nu F\cong Y\times[0,1]\simeq Y
, so that
\varphi
determines a
2
-fold branched cover
p\colon N_{2}(F)\to N
, branched over
F\subseteq N
, and with
Y^{\varphi}=\partial N_{2}(F)
.
Orient
N_{2}(F)
so that
p
is degree 1.
Write
\widetilde{F}\subseteq N_{2}(F)
for the branch set of this branched cover.
Write
e^{\prime}
for the Euler number of
Y^{\varphi}
. This agrees with the normal Euler number of
\widetilde{F}\subseteq N_{2}(F)
.
The spaces
N_{2}(F)
and
N
have the structure of tubular neighbourhoods over
\widetilde{F}
and
F
respectively, and we choose these tubular neighbourhoods so that the double branched covering map
p
is a fibre preserving
D^{2}
-bundle map of the normal bundles
p\colon\nu\widetilde{F}\to\nu F
.
Implicitly using the homeomorphism
p\colon\widetilde{F}\to F
in the base, there is a change of local coefficients homomorphism

p_{\#}\colon H^{2}(\widetilde{F};\{\pi_{1}(S(\xi)_{x})\})\to H^{2}(F;\{\pi_{1}(S(\xi)_{x})\})
that sends
e(\nu\widetilde{F})
to
e(\nu F)
; cf.
[
48
, Lemma 1]
.
As
p
restricts to a double cover on the
S^{1}
fibres, this map is multiplication by
\pm 2
. The sign ambiguity of an Euler number depends only on the choice of orientation on the ambient
4
-manifolds (see Section
2
). The stipulation earlier that
p\colon N_{2}(F)\to N
is a degree 1 map, fixes the sign to be positive.
Thus,
2e^{\prime}=2e(\widetilde{F})=e(F)=e
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The Euler number
e
of a
D^{2}
-bundle
N
over
F
is, by definition, equal to both the Euler number of the associated sphere bundle, and the normal Euler number
e(F)
of
F\subseteq N
, the 0-section of the
D^{2}
-bundle in the total space. Now assume
e
is even and write
\widetilde{F}\subseteq N_{2}(F)
for the branch set of the
2
-fold branched cover described in the proof of Lemma
4.5
. It follows that Lemma
4.5
is equivalent to the statement that the normal Euler numbers satisfy
e(F)=2e(\widetilde{F})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume the Euler number
e
of the bundle
\xi
is even.
The nontrivial homology groups of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cover
\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}
are

H_{0}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\mathring{F}),\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}.

If we write
e=2x
, then the nontrivial homology groups of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cover
Y^{\varphi}
are

H_{0}(Y^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(Y^{\varphi})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $x$ is odd}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $x$ is even,}\end{cases}\quad H_{2}(Y^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},\quad H_{3}(Y^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The definition of the surjection
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}
implies that
Y^{\varphi}
is again a circle bundle with base a surface of nonorientable genus
h
, and is the sphere bundle of a rank two vector bundle
\xi^{\varphi}
over
F
.
By Lemma
4.5
, the Euler number
e^{\prime}
of the rank 2 bundle
\xi^{\varphi}
associated with
Y^{\varphi}
is half that of
\xi
. In other words,
e^{\prime}=x
.
We now have that
Y^{\varphi}
is an Euler number
x
circle bundle with base a surface of nonorientable genus
h
, so the result follows in this closed case from Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
.
For the statement about
\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}
, the reasoning is analogous, but simpler, as the homology computed in the punctured cases of Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
are the same, no matter the parity of the Euler number.
∎

4.2.
The homology of the boundary of a surface exterior

We fix the following notation for the remainder of this section.

Notation 2
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface of nonorientable genus
h
and normal Euler number
e
.
Write
X_{F}
for the exterior of
F
and
K:=\partial F
for its boundary.

Proposition 4.8
.

The nontrivial homology groups of
\partial X_{F}
are

H_{0}(\partial X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(\partial X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},\quad H_{2}(\partial X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{3}(\partial X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

Since
H_{1}(X_{K})=\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{i}(X_{K})=0
for
i>1
, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for the decomposition
\partial X_{F}=\mathring{Y}\cup X_{K}
shows that the homology of
\partial X_{F}
is independent of the knot
K
.
In particular we can assume without loss of generality that
K
is the unknot.
This implies that the Mayer-Vietoris calculation for
\partial X_{F}=\mathring{Y}\cup X_{K}

calculates the homology of the circle bundle
Y=\mathring{Y}\cup(S^{1}\times D^{2})
.
Since
e
is even (recall Theorem
2.5
) the result now follows from Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Let
\widetilde{X}_{F}
denote the universal cover of
X_{F}
and let
\Sigma_{2}(K)
denote the
2
-fold branched cover of a knot
K
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Write the normal Euler number of
F
as
e=2x
.
The nontrivial homology groups of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cover
\partial\widetilde{X}_{F}
are

\displaystyle H_{0}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1},\quad H_{3}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z},\text{ and}

\displaystyle H_{1}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}\oplus H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $x$ is odd}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}\oplus H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))&\quad\text{ if\penalty\ $x$ is even.}\end{cases}

Additionally the inclusion
\mathring{Y}\hookrightarrow\partial X_{F}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-isomorphism

H_{2}(\mathring{Y},\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\xrightarrow{}H_{2}(\partial X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]).

Proof.

Use
X_{J}^{2}\to X_{J}
to denote the
2
-fold cover of the exterior of a knot
J
.
Additionally using
\Sigma_{2}(J)
to denote the
2
-fold branched cover of
J
, a Mayer-Vietoris argument shows that
H_{1}(X_{J}^{2})=H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(J))\oplus\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{2}(X_{J}^{2})=0
.

Note that
\partial\widetilde{X}_{F}=\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}\cup X_{K}^{2}
.
This has the same homology as
Y^{\varphi}
from Proposition
4.7
, except in degree one.
In our setting, if we assume that
|\det(K)|=1
, we see that
X_{K}^{2}
has the same homology as
X_{U}^{2}=S^{1}\times D^{2}
where
U\subseteq S^{3}
denotes the unknot.
In particular, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for
\partial\widetilde{X}_{F}=\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}\cup X_{K}^{2}
calculates the homology of
Y^{\varphi}=\mathring{Y}\cup(S^{1}\times D^{2})
.
The result then follows from Proposition
4.7
.
In general, when
|\det(K)|\neq 1
, the Mayer-Vietoris computation shows that one obtains an additional summand of
H_{1}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})
isomorphic to
H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))
. To see this, the relevant part of the sequence is

H_{1}(\partial X^{2}_{K})\to H_{1}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\oplus H_{1}(X^{2}_{K})\to H_{1}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to 0.

Since
H_{1}(X^{2}_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))
and the map
H_{1}(\partial X^{2}_{K})\to H_{1}(X^{2}_{K})\to H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))
, given by inclusion then projection, is the zero map, it follows that
H_{1}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong H_{1}(Y^{\varphi})\oplus H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))
, as asserted.
∎

4.3.
The homology of exteriors of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces

We continue with the conventions from Notation
2
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The nontrivial integral homology groups of
X_{F}
are

H_{0}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad H_{1}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{2}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}.

Proof.

Since
X_{F}
is connected we have
H_{0}(X_{F})=\mathbb{Z}
and since
X_{F}
is a
4
-manifold with boundary, we know that
H_{4}(X_{F})=0
.
The fact that
X_{F}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface implies that
H_{1}(X_{F})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and that the inclusion induced map
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F})\to\pi_{1}(X_{F})
is surjective.
We deduce that
H_{1}(X_{F},\partial X_{F})=0
.
Poincaré duality and the universal coefficient theorem then imply that
H_{3}(X_{F})=0
and that
H_{2}(X_{F})
is free.
Since
\mathring{Y}
is a
3
-manifold with torus boundary, it has vanishing Euler characteristic.
We deduce that

1=\chi(D^{4})=\chi(X_{F})+\chi(\overline{\nu}F)-\chi(\mathring{Y})=(1+b_{2}(X_{F}))+(1-h)-0

from which it follows that
b_{2}(X_{F})=h-1
.
Thus
H_{2}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
.
∎

We record a lemma that will be helpful when calculating the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-homology of
X_{F}
.
Recall that
\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}
denotes
\mathbb{Z}
considered as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module where
T
acts as multiplication by
\pm 1
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For
k\geq 1
, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}(\mathbb{Z}_{\pm},\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=0
.

Proof.

Consider the following projective resolution for
\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}
:

(4)

\ldots\xrightarrow{1\pm T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xrightarrow{1\mp T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xrightarrow{1\pm T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xrightarrow{1\mp T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\to\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}\to 0.

Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}(-,\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
and removing the rightmost term, we obtain the chain complex

\cdots\xleftarrow{1\pm T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xleftarrow{1\mp T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xleftarrow{1\pm T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\xleftarrow{1\mp T}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\leftarrow 0.

Taking the homology of this chain complex gives the result.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

The nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-homology groups of
X_{F}
are

H_{0}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{+}\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h-1}.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{X}_{F}
is connected, we certainly have
H_{0}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and since
\widetilde{X}_{F}
has nonempty boundary we have
H_{4}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=0
.
Since
\widetilde{X}_{F}
is simply-connected, the fact that
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F})\to\pi_{1}(X_{F})
is surjective implies that
H_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{F},\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})=0
.
Duality and the universal coefficient theorem now imply that
H_{3}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=0
.
Our task is therefore to calculate
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
We first calculate
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
as an abelian group.
Duality and the universal coefficient theorem imply that
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
is free abelian.
We deduce that
1+b_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=\chi(\widetilde{X}_{F})=2\chi(X_{F})=2(1+b_{2}(X_{F}))
and it follows that as an abelian group, we have

H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2b_{2}(X_{F})}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2(h-1)}.

We determine the
