\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module structure of
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.

Next, we deduce from a result of Wall
[
64
, Lemma 5.2]
that the double
D:=X_{F}\cup_{\operatorname{Id}}-X_{F}
has
H_{2}(D;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{-}\oplus F
with
F
free.

Claim 1
.

We have that

H_{2}(D;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\oplus H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])^{*}
.

Proof.

Using excision we have
H_{2}(D,X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=H_{2}(X_{F},\partial X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
Duality implies that
H_{2}(X_{F},\partial X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong H^{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
We now apply the universal coefficient spectral sequence (or UCSS for short) to
H^{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
We refer to
[
45
, Theorem 2.3]
for details about this spectral sequence but note that in this instance it takes the form

E_{2}^{p,q}=\operatorname{Ext}^{q}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}(H_{p}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]),\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\Rightarrow H^{*}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])

with differentials of degree
(1-r,r).

Using Lemma
4.11
and the UCSS, we deduce that
H^{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is isomorphic to
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])^{*}
.
The long exact sequence of the pair
(D,X_{F})
now gives

0\to H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\to H_{2}(D;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\to H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])^{*}\to 0.

The fold map
D\to X_{F}
is a retract and so its induced map on
H_{2}(-;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
splits this short exact sequence.
This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Using the fact that
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2(h-1)}
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module and torsion free as an abelian group, Lemma
3.10
implies
that as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
decomposes as
P\oplus P_{+1}\oplus P_{-1}
.
Here,
P
is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-free,
P_{+1}
has the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
+1
, and
P_{-1}
has the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
-1
.
Since Lemma
4.1
gives
\mathcal{E}_{+}(H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F};\mathbb{Q}))\cong H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{h-1}

and we know that
H_{2}(D;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{2}\oplus F
(with
F
free) decomposes as
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\oplus H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])^{*}

(thanks to Claim
1
), this forces

H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h-1}.

Here we also used that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}(\mathbb{Z}_{\pm},\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}_{\pm}
; recall Remark
3.11
.
This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.4.
The homology of branched covers of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces

We continue with the conventions from Notation
2
, and additionally we write
\widetilde{F}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}(F)
for the branch set in the
2
-fold branched cover.
The next proposition relates the homology of the branched and unbranched covers, and follows
[
40
, p. 65]
.

Proposition 4.13
.

There is an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules

(5)

0\to H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{j}H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 0.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
term has
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action given by extending the trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action linearly.

Proof.

This proposition follows from a Mayer-Vietoris argument for
\Sigma_{2}(F)
, as we now describe.
We have a decomposition
\Sigma_{2}(F)=\widetilde{X}_{F}\cup_{\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}}\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
.
The gluing takes place along
\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}\cong\partial\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
.
We assert that the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for
\Sigma_{2}(F)=\widetilde{X}_{F}\cup_{\mathring{Y}^{\varphi}}\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
reduces to

(6)

0\to H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\to H_{1}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\xrightarrow{\psi}H_{1}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\to 0.

For the leftmost side of the sequence, we used
H_{3}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong H^{1}(\Sigma_{2}(F),\partial\Sigma_{2}(F))=0
, where the latter holds because
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is simply-connected and
\Sigma_{2}(K)=\partial\Sigma_{2}(F)
is connected.
For the rightmost zero, we used that
H_{1}(\partial\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\cong H_{1}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus H_{1}(\widetilde{F})
(recall Proposition
4.7
) maps surjectively onto
H_{1}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\cong H_{1}(\widetilde{F})
, and we used that
H_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=0
.
The assertion now follows from the fact that
H_{2}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\cong H_{2}(\widetilde{F})=0
.

Next we explain how to go from the exact sequence (
6
) to the short exact sequence (
5
) in the statement of the proposition.
To study the rightmost side of the sequence, we consider the following portion of the exact sequence of the pair
(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F},\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})
:

\cdots\to H_{2}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F},\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{1}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\xrightarrow{\psi}H_{1}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\to H_{1}(\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F},\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to\cdots

Here
\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
is what we called
\mathring{N}
in the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
In that proposition, using excision, we saw that
H_{i}(\mathring{N},\mathring{Y})
vanishes for
i=1
and that for
i=2
we have that
H_{2}(\mathring{N},\mathring{Y})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, generated by the
D^{2}
-fibre of
\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
.
It follows that
\psi
is surjective with
\ker(\psi)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generated by the meridian to
\widetilde{F}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}(F)
.

We have now established the exact sequence in (
5
). We conclude by explaining the last assertion of the proposition: the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
term in (
5
) has the trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action.
We have already argued that this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is generated by the meridian to
\widetilde{F}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}(F)
.
By definition of the branched cover, this meridian is
(1+T)\widetilde{\mu}
, where
\widetilde{\mu}\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{F}
is a path lifting a meridian
\mu\subseteq X_{F}
to
F\subseteq D^{4}
.
Since
T(1+T)\widetilde{\mu}=(1+T)\widetilde{\mu}
, the generator is unchanged under the action of
T
, so we obtain the required statement.
∎

Next, we use the exact sequence of Proposition
4.13
to deduce the homology of the
2
-fold cover of
D^{4}
branched along a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F
.

Proposition 4.14
.

The branched cover
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is simply-connected and its nontrivial homology groups are

H_{0}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}.

Proof.

The branched cover
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is simply-connected because
\widetilde{X}_{F}
is simply-connected.
The fact that
H_{0}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}
follows because
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is connected, and
H_{4}(\Sigma_{2}(F))=0
because
\Sigma_{2}(F)
has nonempty boundary.
Since
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is simply-connected,
H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(F))=0
and
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
is free. It therefore suffices to prove that
b_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))=h
. Recall from Proposition
4.10
that
H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h-1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{-}
and therefore
b_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=2(h-1)+1=2h-1
.
Recall also from Proposition
4.7
that
H_{2}(\partial\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F})\cong H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})
is free abelian of rank
h-1
. The exact sequence in (
5
) now implies that
b_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))=(2h-1)-(h-1)=h
, as claimed.
∎

Recall that the homology groups of
\Sigma_{2}(F)
are endowed with the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module by the action of the deck transformation.
We compute the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module structure of
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
.

Proposition 4.15
.

There is an isomorphism
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}_{-}
of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Proposition
4.12
.
Namely, we first use fixed points to study
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})
and then use Lemma
3.10
to conclude.

Claim 2
.

There is an isomorphism
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}^{h}_{-}
of
\mathbb{Q}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.

Proof.

We showed in Proposition
4.14
that
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}^{h}
as a vector space over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Lemma
4.1
then implies that

H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{2}(D^{4};\mathbb{Q})\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q}))\cong\mathcal{E}_{-}(H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})).

It follows that
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}_{-}^{h},
as claimed.
∎

We now conclude the proof of Proposition
4.15
.
Using the fact that
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module and torsion free as an abelian group,
Lemma
3.10
implies that as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
decomposes as
P\oplus P_{+1}\oplus P_{-1}
.
Here,
P
is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-free,
P_{+1}
has the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
+1
, and
P_{-1}
has the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action where
T
operates by
-1
.
Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
,
Claim
2
implies that
P=0
and
P_{+1}=0
. Therefore
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}
, as required.
∎

5.
Intersection forms

In this section, we describe some facts about the various types of intersection forms associated with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface.
We continue with the notation from Section
4.2
, which we recall briefly.

Notation 3
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface of nonorientable genus
h
and normal Euler number
e
.
Write
X_{F}
for the exterior of
F
and
K:=\partial F\subseteq S^{3}
for its boundary.

5.1.
The
\mathbb{Z}
-intersection form of the branched cover

Proposition 5.1
.

The intersection form
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nondegenerate. If
|\det(K)|=1
, then
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is moreover nonsingular.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma_{2}(K)=\partial\Sigma_{2}(F)
is a rational homology 3-sphere, we know that
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(K))=0
.
Thus
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\to H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F),\Sigma_{2}(K))
is injective, and we deduce that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nondegenerate.
If in addition
|\det(K)|=1
, then
H_{1}(\Sigma_{2}(K))=0
and it follows that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nonsingular.
∎

Recall that we say
e:=e(F)
is
extremal
if
e\in\{-2h+2\sigma(K),2h+2\sigma(K)\}
, i.e.
e
is one of the two extremal values in Theorem
2.5
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume that
|\det(K)|=1
.
The intersection form
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is definite if and only if the normal Euler number
e
is extremal.

Proof.

A nonsingular symmetric bilinear form is definite if and only if its rank equals the absolute value of its signature.
By Proposition
5.1
, we know that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nonsingular.
Thus
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is definite if and only if
\operatorname{rk}(H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))=h
equals
|\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))|=|\sigma(K)-\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}e(F)|
.
By Theorem
2.5
this is equivalent to
e
being extremal.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

The intersection form
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is odd.

Proof.

Edmonds
[
17
, Corollary 4]
proved that the branched cover
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is not spin.
We note that Edmonds states his proof in the smooth category, but that the proof may be adapted to work for a properly embedded surface in a
4
-manifold, replacing his discussions of smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
actions by locally linear
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
actions.
Since
\Sigma_{2}(F)
is simply-connected, this implies that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is odd.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Assume that
|\det(K)|=1

and write
\sigma:=\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))=\sigma(K)-\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}e
.
If
h\leq 8
or
e
is non-extremal, then

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}\cong(1)^{a}\oplus(-1)^{b}\penalty\

where
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are nonnegative integers such that
h=a+b
and
\sigma=a-b
, which exist because
h\equiv\sigma\mod{2}
and
|\sigma|\leq h
.

Proof.

We know from Propositions
5.1
,
5.3
, and
4.14
that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nonsingular, odd and has rank
h
.
Assume that
h\leq 8
and that
e
is extremal.
By Proposition
5.2
,
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is definite.
Since
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is odd and definite, the classification of (low rank) definite, nonsingular symmetric bilinear forms implies that

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}\cong\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)(1)^{\oplus h}

[
52
, Chapter II, Lemma 6.2 and Remark 1]
.
We now assume that
e
is non-extremal so that by Proposition
5.2
,
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is indefinite.
Since
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is odd and indefinite, the classification of odd, indefinite, nonsingular, symmetric, bilinear forms
[
52
, Theorem II.4.3]
in terms of their rank and signature implies that

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}\cong(1)^{\oplus a}\oplus(-1)^{\oplus b},

where
a+b=h
is the rank of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
and
a-b=\sigma
is the signature of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
.
∎

5.2.
The
\mathbb{Z}
-intersection form of the universal cover

This section is concerned with
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
, the
\mathbb{Z}
-intersection form of the universal cover of
X_{F}
.
In fact, we are particularly interested in the radical
\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
and the form induced on the quotient
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
.
Here, recall that
\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
consists of those
x\in H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
such that
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x,y)=0
for all
y\in H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}).

Proposition 5.5
.

The inclusion induced map
i_{*}\colon H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
is injective.
In particular, this implies that, as an abelian group, the radical of
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is

\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})=\operatorname{Im}(i_{*}\colon H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}))\cong H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}.

Additionally, the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module structure on
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
induced by deck transformations is extended linearly from
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, acting by multiplication by
-1
.

Proof.

The adjoint of
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is given by the composition

H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{j_{*}}H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F},\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{PD},\cong}H^{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev},\cong}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}),\mathbb{Z}).

Thus
the radical of
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is equal to
\ker(j_{*})=\operatorname{Im}(i_{*}\colon H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}))
.
Using Poincaré duality and the universal coefficient theorem, we see that
H_{3}(\widetilde{X}_{F},\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong H^{1}(\widetilde{X}_{F})=0
.
The long exact sequence of the pair
(\widetilde{X}_{F},\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})
implies that

i_{*}\colon H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})
is injective.
As
i_{*}
is injective, this image is isomorphic to
H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})
, which by Proposition
4.9
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}
.

Finally, the exact sequence

0\to H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{j}H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 0

from Proposition
4.13
shows that we can identify
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
with a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-submodule of
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
, of index
2
as an abelian subgroup.
By Proposition
4.15
we have
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

In fact, as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module, we have that
\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h-1}_{+}
. We omit the proof since we shall not require this information later.

Write
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
for the nondegenerate form induced by
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
on
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
.
The next proposition refines Proposition
4.13
to include information on the intersection forms. The statement was given (without proof) on
[
40
, p. 65]
.

Proposition 5.7
.

There is an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules

(7)

0\to\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{j}H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 0.

The inclusion-induced map
j\colon H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
induces an isometry
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\cong Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}|_{\operatorname{Im}(j)}
, and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
term has
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-action given by extending the trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action linearly.

In particular the form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
splits as

Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}\cong(0)^{(h-1)}\oplus Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}|_{\operatorname{Im}(j)},

where
\operatorname{Im}(j)
is free abelian of rank
h
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.13
established the exact sequence

0\to H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\xrightarrow{j}H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to 0.

We also proved in Proposition
4.9
that the inclusion
\mathring{Y}\subseteq\partial X_{F}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-isomorphism
H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})
.
It now follows from Proposition
5.5
that

\operatorname{Im}(H_{2}(\mathring{Y}^{\varphi})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}))=\operatorname{Im}(H_{2}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{F})\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F}))=\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}).

This gives rise to the exact sequence (
7
).
The sentence concerning the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action was already proved in Proposition
4.13
and since
j
is inclusion induced, it gives rise to an isometry
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\cong Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}|_{\operatorname{Im}(j)}
, as asserted.
The fact that
\operatorname{Im}(j)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}
follows from (
7
) together with Proposition
4.14
, according to which
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}
.

We conclude by proving the splitting of the intersection form.
Note that
H_{2}(\Sigma(F))
is free abelian, and therefore as a subgroup of this, so is
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
. It follows that there is a splitting
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})\oplus H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
, and the form also splits since one summand is the radical.
∎

Next we describe
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
in terms of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
.
Recall that a properly embedded surface
F\subseteq X
in a
4
-manifold
X
is
characteristic
if
[F]\in H_{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is Poincaré dual to
w_{2}(X)\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Lemma 5.8
.

If
G\subseteq D^{4}
is a properly embedded nonorientable surface, then the preimage
\widetilde{G}\subset\Sigma_{2}(G)
of
G
under the branched covering map is characteristic.
The same assertion holds for closed nonorientable surfaces in
S^{4}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.3
, the intersection form of
\Sigma_{2}(G)
is odd, and so
\Sigma_{2}(G)
is not spin.
It is a folklore result that a properly embedded surface in a
compact, oriented
4
-manifold is characteristic if and only if the exterior of the surface is spin; see e.g.
[
22
, Proposition 7.18]
for a proof.
Since
G\subseteq D^{4}
, and
D^{4}
is spin, the exterior
X_{G}
is spin, and thus so is the universal cover
\widetilde{X}_{G}
. But
\widetilde{X}_{G}
is the exterior of
\widetilde{G}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}(G)
, so the lemma is proved.
∎

Example 5.9
.

As an example, if
G\subseteq S^{4}
is unknotted then
\Sigma_{2}(G)\cong\#^{a}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\#^{b}\overline{\mathbb{C}P}^{2}
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
[\widetilde{G}]\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(G);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is the sum of the
a+b
standard classes
[\mathbb{C}P^{1}]
, one from each connect summand.
It is not difficult to compute that this
[\widetilde{G}]
is characteristic by verifying that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(G)}(x,x)=Q_{\Sigma_{2}(G)}(x,[\widetilde{G}])
mod
2
for every
x\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(G);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

We return to our fixed
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F
.

Proposition 5.10
.

The inclusion
j\colon\widetilde{X}_{F}\hookrightarrow\Sigma_{2}(F)
induces an isometric injection

j\colon(H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}),{Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}})\hookrightarrow(H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F)),Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}).

Moreover

\operatorname{Im}(j)=\{x\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\,\,|\,\,Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,x)\equiv 0\mod 2\}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}.

Proof.

The first assertion was already proved in Proposition
5.7
, so we now prove the statement relating the image of
j
to mod
2
intersections in
\Sigma_{2}(F)
.
We know from Proposition
5.7
that
\mathbb{Z}^{h}\cong\operatorname{Im}(j)=\ker(\partial)
.
As a consequence, the assertion reduces to proving that
\partial(x)\equiv Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,x)\pmod{2}
for every
x\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
.
The definition of the connecting homomorphism in the Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence for
\Sigma_{2}(F)=\widetilde{X}_{F}\cup\overline{\nu}\widetilde{F}
implies that
\partial(x)=Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,[\widetilde{F}])\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
By Lemma
5.8
, the class
[\widetilde{F}]\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is Poincaré dual to
w_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
, and is thus a characteristic vector for
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Thus
\partial(x)\equiv Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,[\widetilde{F}])\equiv Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,x)\pmod{2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

The next proposition applies Proposition
5.10
to calculate
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
and its corresponding quadratic form
\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
.
The answer will depend on the following quadratic form, defined for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
:

X_{n}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)\left[\begin{pmatrix}2&2&2&\ldots&2\\
0&1&1&\cdots&1\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&1\\
0&\ldots&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n}).

The symmetrisation of this form is definite, which is relevant to the next proposition.

Recall that
h
is the rank of
H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))
and
\sigma
is the signature of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
.
It follows that
|\sigma|\leq h
and that
h\equiv\sigma\mod{2}
when
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
is nonsingular.
Thus
h-|\sigma|
is nonnegative and even, and if
\sigma=0
then
h
is even.

Proposition 5.11
.

Assume that
|\det(K)|=1
and write
\sigma:=\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F))
.
If
h\leq 8
or if
e
is non-extremal, then

\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\cong\begin{cases}2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{h}{2}-1}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma=0,\\
X_{\sigma}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma\neq 0.\end{cases}

Proof.

We calculate
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
, as this entirely determines the quadratic form
\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
.
We will use Proposition
5.10
, which shows that the inclusion
\widetilde{X}_{F}\subseteq\Sigma_{2}(F)
induces an isometry between the form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
and the restriction of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
to

(8)

\operatorname{Im}(j)=\{x\in H_{2}(\Sigma_{2}(F))\mid Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}(x,x)=0\ \operatorname{mod}\ 2\}.

Assume
e
is non-extremal.
Using Proposition
5.4
, and recalling that
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are defined to be the unique integers such that
h=a+b
and
\sigma=a-b
, the classification of odd indefinite nonsingular symmetric bilinear forms
[
52
, Theorem II.4.3]
, which says that such forms are isometric if and only if they have the same rank and signature, gives

(9)

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}\cong(1)^{\oplus a}\oplus(-1)^{\oplus b}\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)I_{|\sigma|}\oplus H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma\neq 0,\\
(1)\oplus(-1)\oplus H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\frac{h}{2}-1}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma=0.\end{cases}

Indeed these forms have signature
\sigma
, rank
h
, and are indefinite and odd. Such symmetric bilinear forms are unique up to isometry.
When
e
is extremal and
h\leq 8
we proved in Proposition
5.4
that
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}\cong\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)(1)^{\oplus h}
and, since
|\sigma|=h\neq 0
, the isomorphisms in (
9
) hold trivially.

We now calculate
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}|_{\operatorname{Im}(j)\times\operatorname{Im}(j)}
.
The hyperbolic summands of
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
lead to (the same number of) hyperbolic summands in
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}|_{\operatorname{Im}(j)\times\operatorname{Im}(j)}
. Using (
8
), this is because hyperbolics are even and therefore all squares are zero mod
2
.

We first assume that
\sigma=0
, and analyse the restriction of
B:=(1)\oplus(-1)
to
\operatorname{Im}(j)
.
Use
e_{1},e_{2}
to denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. By (
8
),

\operatorname{Im}(j)=\{v_{1}e_{1}+v_{2}e_{2}\mid v_{1}+v_{2}\equiv 0\mod{2}\}.

Whence observe that
\operatorname{Im}(j)
admits
2e_{1},e_{1}+e_{2}
as a basis.
Evaluating
B
on these basis vectors yields
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&2\\
2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cong 2H^{+}(\mathbb{Z}).

Passing to quadratic forms yields the result in this case.

We conclude with the case
\sigma\neq 0
.
We need to analyse the restriction of
B^{\prime}:=\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)I_{|\sigma|}
to
\operatorname{Im}(j)
.
Use
e_{1},\ldots,e_{|\sigma|}
to denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{|\sigma|}
. By (
8
),

\operatorname{Im}(j)=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{|\sigma|}v_{i}e_{i}\,\Big|\,\sum_{i=1}^{|\sigma|}v_{i}\equiv 0\mod{2}\Big\}.

We can therefore compute that
\operatorname{Im}(j)
admits
2e_{1},e_{1}+e_{2},e_{1}+e_{3},\ldots,e_{1}+e_{|\sigma|}
as a basis.
Evaluating
B^{\prime}
on these basis vectors yields the matrix
X_{\sigma}+X_{\sigma}^{T}
.
Passing to the quadratic forms yields the result in this case.
∎

5.3.
The
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-intersection form of the exterior

We describe the nondegenerate Hermitian form
\lambda_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
induced by the equivariant intersection
\lambda_{X_{F}}\colon H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\times H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
on the quotient
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})
.

Proposition 5.12
.

The radical of the equivariant intersection form
\lambda_{X_{F}}
of
X_{F}
equals that of the standard intersection form of
\widetilde{X}_{F}
under the isomorphism
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})\cong H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
. On the quotient
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}
we have
\lambda_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}=(1-T)Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
.

Proof.

The same reasoning as in Proposition
5.5
, but now using that
H_{3}(X_{F},\partial X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong H^{1}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=0
, shows that the radical of
\lambda_{X_{F}}
is the image of
H_{2}(\partial X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
and this is precisely
\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
.
Use Proposition
5.5
again
to observe that

(10)

H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})=H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})=H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{h}_{-}.

The last part of the assertion now follows from a direct calculation using the definition of
\lambda_{X_{F}}
. Indeed, for any pair of elements
x,y\in H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})
, we have

\lambda_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}(x,y)=Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x,y)+Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x,Ty)T=Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x,y)+Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x,-y)T=(1-T)Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}(x,y).

This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

6.
A spin union of surface exteriors

We will show that if two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces in
D^{4}
with the same connected, nonempty boundary have the same nonorientable genus and normal Euler number, then it is possible to glue their exteriors together so that the union is both spin and has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Notation 4
.

In this section, it will be necessary to be particularly careful with tubular neighbourhoods.
We will use the notation
\nu_{A\subseteq B}
for the normal vector bundle of a submanifold
A\subseteq B
(as opposed to
\nu A\subseteq B
, which is an open tubular neighbourhood).
We will assume that every surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
comes equipped with a choice of tubular neighbourhood, i.e. a choice of continuous map
\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}\to D^{4}
from the total space of the normal vector bundle of
F
whose restriction to the unit disc bundle is a homeomorphism to its image:

\alpha\colon(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}),S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))\xrightarrow{\cong}(\alpha(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})),\alpha(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))),

and which when composed with the zero section gives the original embedding of
F
.
We refer to the disc bundle
\alpha(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
as a
tubular neighbourhood
of
F\subseteq D^{4}
.

Moreover, for each knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
, we choose a tubular neighbourhood and assume that, if a surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
has connected nonempty boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
, then the tubular neighbourhood of
F
extends the given one of
K
. In this way, if two such surfaces have the same connected boundary
K
, their tubular neighbourhoods will agree precisely upon restriction to the boundary.
Throughout this section it will also be helpful to recall that
\partial X_{F}\cong X_{K}\cup_{\partial\overline{\nu}K}\alpha(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
.
Moreover, one should recall that if a manifold is orientable (resp. spin), then so is every codimension 0 submanifold, and so is the boundary. In particular, as
D^{4}
is spin so are
X_{F}
, the open tubular neighbourhood
\nu F
, and
\partial X_{F}
.

The
meridian
of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
refers to the homotopy class
T\in\pi_{1}(X_{F})
of the boundary of any
D^{2}
-fibre of the tubular neighbourhood
\alpha(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
, together with a basing path.
The meridian of
F

•

generates
\pi_{1}(X_{F})\cong H_{1}(X_{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
;

•

lies in the image of the inclusion induced map
\iota\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F})\to\pi_{1}(X_{F})
.

We will also refer to an element
\mu\in\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F})
as a meridian if it satisfies
\iota(\mu)=T
.
Given two nonorientable surfaces
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
, a homomorphism
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})\to\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{1}})
is said to
preserve meridians
if there exists a meridian
\mu_{0}\in\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
with
\varphi\circ\iota_{1}(\mu_{0})=T_{1}
.

Definition 6.1
.

A section
s\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
is
nice
if the composition

H_{1}(F)\xrightarrow{{s}_{*}}H_{1}(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{*}}H_{1}(\partial X_{F})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H_{1}(X_{F}),

is trivial, where
\iota\colon\partial X_{F}\to X_{F}
is the inclusion.

Recall from Definition
1.4
that a homeomorphism
f\colon S(\nu F_{0})\to S(\nu F_{1})
that restricts to the identity map on
S(\nu K)
is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary

if it extends to a homeomorphism
\overline{\nu}F_{0}\cong\overline{\nu}F_{1}
of the closed tubular neighbourhoods that sends
F_{0}
to
F_{1}
, and restricts to the identity map on
\nu K
.
The main result of this section is the following.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of nonorientable genus
h
, with the same connected nonempty boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
, and each with normal Euler number
e
. Fix a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\partial X_{F_{1}})
that extends to
X_{F_{1}}
.
Then there exists an orientation preserving homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
such that:

(1)

the spin structure
f^{*}\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
extends to
X_{F_{0}}
;

(2)

on
X_{K}\subseteq X_{F_{0}}
, the map
f
restricts to the identity map
\operatorname{Id}_{X_{K}}
;

(3)

on
\partial\overline{\nu}F_{0}\setminus\nu K=\alpha_{0}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})\subseteq X_{F_{0}})
, the map
f
restricts to a homeomorphism from

\alpha_{0}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}))
to
\alpha_{1}(S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}}))
, that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary;

(4)

the map
f_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})\to\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{1}})
preserves meridians;

(5)

there exist nice sections
s_{0}\colon F_{0}\to S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})
and
s_{1}\colon F_{1}\to S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}})
which are preserved by

f
, in the sense that
f(\alpha_{0}(s_{0}(F_{0})))=\alpha_{1}(s_{1}(F_{1}))\subseteq\alpha_{1}(S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}}))
.

Some preparation is required for the proof of this proposition, which is the goal of this section.
But, assuming the proposition, we record the main consequence.

Corollary 6.3
.

The union
X_{F_{0}}\cup_{f}-X_{F_{1}}
is spin and has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Proof of Corollary
6.3
.

It follows immediately from Proposition
6.2
that the union is spin.
To see that the fundamental group is as claimed, we apply the Seifert–van-Kampen theorem, computing the push-out
of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle T_{1}\mid{T_{1}}^{2}=1\rangle=\pi_{1}(X_{F_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(X_{F_{0}})=\langle T_{0}\mid{T_{0}}^{2}=1\rangle,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{0})_{*}}

where
\iota_{j}\colon\partial X_{F_{j}}\to X_{F_{j}}
denote the inclusion maps.
To understand this diagram we consider the decomposition
\partial X_{F_{0}}\cong X_{K}\cup_{\partial\overline{\nu}K}\alpha_{0}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}))
, which computes the fundamental group as the amalgamated free product

\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{K})\ast_{\pi_{1}(\partial\overline{\nu}K)}\pi_{1}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})).

We claim that an element of
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
maps trivially under
(\iota_{0})_{*}
if and only if it maps trivially under
(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}
.
Let
s_{0}\colon F_{0}\to S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})
be a nice section.
Recalling the standard cell structure for
F
from our standing conventions at the end of the introduction, we have closed curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{h}
on
F_{0}
.
Together with a meridian
\mu_{F_{0}}
, we have a generating set
\{\mu_{F_{0}},s_{0}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,s_{0}(\gamma_{h})\}
for
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
.
Since
s_{0}
is a nice section, and since
f
preserves nice sections, we have

(\iota_{0})_{*}(s_{0}(\gamma_{i}))=1\in\pi_{1}(X_{F_{0}})\quad\text{ and }\quad(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}(s_{0}(\gamma_{i}))=1\in\pi_{1}(X_{F_{1}}).

The meridian
\mu_{F_{0}}
lies in the image
\pi_{1}(\partial\overline{\nu}K)\to\pi_{1}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}))
.
Thus an element
g\in\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
can be expressed as a word in elements of
\pi_{1}(X_{K})
and the
s_{0}(\gamma_{i})
.
Consider the word
\widetilde{g}\in\pi_{1}(X_{K})
obtained by removing all the instances of
s_{0}(\gamma_{i})
from
g\in\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
.
Since
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
are nice sections and
f
preserves nice sections, the images
(\iota_{0})_{*}(g)\in\pi_{1}(X_{F_{0}})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}(g)\in\pi_{1}(X_{F_{1}})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are both computed by taking the image of
\widetilde{g}
under the composition
\pi_{1}(X_{K})\to H_{1}(X_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, given by the Hurewicz map and reduction modulo two.
Thus each element of
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
maps trivially under
(\iota_{0})_{*}
if and only if it maps trivially under
(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}
, as claimed.

As
f
is meridian preserving,
\mu_{F_{0}}\in\pi_{1}(\partial X_{F_{0}})
is such that
(\iota_{0})_{*}(\mu_{F_{0}})=T_{0}
and
(\iota_{1}\circ f)_{*}(\mu_{F_{0}})=T_{1}
.
Hence in the push-out we have

\pi_{1}(X_{F_{0}}\cup_{f}X_{F_{1}})\cong\langle T_{0},T_{1}\mid{T_{0}}^{2}=1,{T_{1}}^{2}=1,T_{0}=T_{1}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2},

as desired.
∎

The focus for the proof of Proposition
6.2
is on proving condition (
1
); conditions (
2
), (
3
), (
4
), and (
5
) will be achieved along the way. The idea for showing (
1
) is intuitively straightforward but the argument is a little long, so some more informal discussion is in order before we begin.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exterior
X_{F}
is spin, and there are exactly two choices of spin structure
because
H^{1}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. A spin structure on a manifold can be described by its restriction to the 1-skeleton. The inclusion of the sphere bundle of the normal bundle
\alpha(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))\subseteq\partial X_{F}
induces an isomorphism on homology with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficients, so in fact a spin structure on
\partial X_{F}
can be described by just by its restriction to the 1-skeleton of
\alpha(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
; this 1-skeleton consists of the meridian to
F
and a push-off of the 1-skeleton of
F
. The two spin structures on
X_{F}
differ on the meridian, in particular showing these are distinct spin structures on restriction to
\partial X_{F}
. There are
|H^{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})|=2^{h}
possible spin structures on the push-off of the 1-skeleton of
F
. As we shall see, exactly one of these is the restriction of the two spin structures on
\partial X_{F}
that extend to
X_{F}
.
Thus, intuitively, the task is to produce a meridian-preserving homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
that also preserves this distinguished spin structure on the surface push-off.
Having equal normal Euler numbers is what guarantees the last condition is possible.

We need the following definition.

Definition 6.4
.

Given a symmetric bilinear form
(P,\lambda)
on a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space
P
, a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement
is a function
q\colon P\to\mathbb{Z}_{4}
such that
q(x+y)=q(x)+q(y)+2\lambda(x,y)
, for all
x,y\in P
. We call the triple
(P,\lambda,q)
a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Even though
\lambda(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we can make sense of
2\lambda(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.
One quickly deduces from the condition on
q
that
q(0)=0
and that
2q(x)=2\lambda(x,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.

Here is a more detailed proof structure for Proposition
6.2
(
1
).

(1)

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a compact, properly embedded surface with boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
and write
\mathring{Y}
for the total space of the sphere bundle of the normal bundle, so that
\partial X_{F}\cong X_{K}\cup\mathring{Y}
.
We show that a choice of section
s\colon F\to\mathring{Y}
can be used to specify a 1:1 correspondence between the set of spin structures on
\partial X_{F}
and the set of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinements of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form on
s(F)
, up to isometry, together with the restricted spin structure on the meridian.
Here, the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-refinement
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})
associated to a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
is due to Kirby-Taylor
[
38
]
.

(2)

An embedded surface
F\subseteq D^{4}
has a canonical
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form of
F
, called the
Guillou-Marin quadratic refinement

[
28
]
, and denoted
q_{GM}
.
We prove (Corollary
6.17
) that a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial X_{F}
extends to
X_{F}
if and only if the Kirby-Taylor refinement on
s(F)\subseteq\partial X_{F}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{s}
coincides with the Guillou-Marin refinement on
F\subseteq D^{4}
.

(3)

Guillou-Marin proved (Corollary
6.19
)
that if two surfaces
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
with
\partial F_{0}=\partial F_{1}
have equal nonorientable genus and normal Euler number, then there exists a homeomorphism
F_{0}\cong F_{1}
inducing an isometry of Guillou-Marin
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic forms.

(4)

To complete the proof, we let
\mathfrak{s}
be a spin structure on
\partial X_{F_{1}}
that extends to
X_{F_{1}}
.
We show how to use Guillou-Marin’s homeomorphism
F_{0}\cong F_{1}
to construct a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
. We show that

q_{GM_{0}}=q_{GM_{1}}\circ f_{*}=q_{KT_{1}}(\mathfrak{s})\circ f_{*}=q_{KT_{0}}(f^{*}\mathfrak{s}).

The first equality uses (
3
), and the second uses (
2
). Applying the reverse implication of (
2
) to
\partial X_{F_{0}}
implies that

f^{*}\mathfrak{s}
extends over
X_{F_{0}}
.

6.1.
The Kirby-Taylor quadratic refinements

Given an abstract compact surface, we recall a construction of Kirby-Taylor
[
38
, Definition 3.5]
that relates a certain set of spin structures to the set of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinements of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form on the surface.

Let
\Sigma
be a compact, connected surface.
Let
\zeta=(\mathbb{R}\to E\to\Sigma)
be a line bundle with
w_{1}(\zeta)=w_{1}(\Sigma)
. Note that
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
admits a spin structure. To see this, consider an immersion
\iota\colon\Sigma\looparrowright\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Note that
\iota^{*}(T\mathbb{R}^{3})=T\Sigma\oplus\nu_{\iota(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}}
.
By the Whitney sum formula
w_{1}(\nu_{\iota(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}})=w_{1}(T\Sigma)=w_{1}(\zeta)
, so the normal bundle is isomorphic to
\zeta
. We then compute that
w_{1}(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta)=w_{1}(T\Sigma)+w_{1}(\zeta)=0
and

0=\iota^{*}(w_{2}(T\mathbb{R}^{3}))=w_{2}(\iota^{*}(T\mathbb{R}^{3}))=w_{2}(T\Sigma\oplus\nu_{\iota(\Sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}})=w_{2}(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta).

So
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
admits a spin structure as desired. Fix a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
.
Using the
0
-section, identify
\Sigma
with a subspace of
E
.
The reader will verify that there is a canonical bundle isomorphism
\zeta\cong\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}
.

2
2

2

For the reader comparing with
[
38
, p. 203]
, we note that they stipulate a choice of spin structure on
TE
at this point. We think this is a minor error in that article, and they should rather stipulate a choice of spin structure on
TE|_{\Sigma}
, the tangent bundle of the total space restricted to
\Sigma
, as this is what corresponds to a
\operatorname{Pin}^{-}
structure on
T\Sigma
; see the proof of Theorem
6.7
below. This is then equivalent to our stipulation of a spin structure on
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
, by the observation that
\zeta\cong\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}
and the splitting of
TE|_{\Sigma}\cong T\Sigma\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}
.

Let
\gamma\subseteq\Sigma
be an embedded circle.
Make a choice of orientation on
\gamma
. This choice determines a framing
T\gamma\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}
.
We now describe two ways of framing the rank three vector bundle
T\Sigma|_{\gamma}\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}|_{\gamma}
.
The framing
\mathcal{F}
is defined to be the one determined by the choice of spin structure on
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
, via the following sequence of identifications:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\colon T\Sigma|_{\gamma}\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}|_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\cong T\Sigma|_{\gamma}\oplus\zeta|_{\gamma}\cong(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta)|_{\gamma}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

The framing
\mathcal{G}_{f}
is obtained by combining the orientation-induced framing
T\gamma\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}
with some additional choice of a framing
f\colon\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the following identifications:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{f}\colon T\Sigma|_{\gamma}\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}|_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\cong T\gamma\oplus\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq E}|_{\gamma}\cong T\gamma\oplus\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Definition 6.5
.

The framing
f\colon\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}\xrightarrow{\cong}\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, above, is called
odd
(with respect to the chosen spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
) if
\mathcal{G}_{f}
determines the same orientation on
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}
as
\mathcal{F}
, but the framings
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
disagree up to homotopy.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface, let
\zeta=(\mathbb{R}\to E\to\Sigma)
be a line bundle over
\Sigma
with
w_{1}(\zeta)=w_{1}(\Sigma)
and choose a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
for
T\Sigma\oplus\zeta
.
Let
\gamma\subseteq E
be an embedded circle. Choose an orientation on
\gamma
and an odd framing of
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}
with respect to
\mathfrak{s}
.
With respect to these data, define
the Kirby-Taylor quadratic refinement of
\gamma
as

q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})(\gamma):=\#\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{right-handed half-twists made by }\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\subseteq\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}\\
\text{as it completes a full rotation around}\penalty\ \gamma\text{ in the positive direction}\end{array}\right\}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}.

Note that a count of right-handed half-twists only makes sense once an orientation on
\gamma
and a framing
f\colon\nu_{\gamma\subseteq E}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
have been chosen.

The Kirby-Taylor quadratic refinement depends neither on the choice of orientation on
\gamma
, nor on the particular choice of odd framing; see
[
38
, p. 203]
. The dependency on the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
is witnessed by the concept of an odd framing.

Theorem 6.7

(Kirby-Taylor
[
38
]
)
.

The triple
(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma},q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s}))
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The assignment described in Definition
6.6
determines a bijection of sets

q_{KT}\colon\operatorname{Spin}(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta)\xrightarrow{1:1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of}\\
\text{$(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma})$}\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

As Kirby-Taylor do not state the result in the way we have, we collect the components of their work which yield our statement. First,
[
38
, Theorem 3.2]
is the statement that there is a natural 1:1 correspondence

(11)

\operatorname{Pin}^{-}(T\Sigma)\xrightarrow{1:1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of}\\
\text{$(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma})$}\end{array}\right\},

where the reader is referred to that article for the definition of a
\operatorname{Pin}^{-}
structure.
The natural correspondence is described on
[
38
, p. 203]
, and begins by using
[
38
, Lemma 1.7]
in which there is described a natural bijection
\Psi_{4k+1}(\xi)\colon\operatorname{Pin}^{-}(\xi)\to\operatorname{Spin}(\xi\oplus(4k+1)\det(\xi))
, for any vector bundle
\xi
and integer
k\geq 0
. We use
\xi=T\Sigma
, and
k=0
.
We then note that as
w_{1}(T\Sigma)=w_{1}(\zeta)
and
T\Sigma
is orientable, we obtain
w_{1}(\det(T\Sigma))=w_{1}(\zeta)
and so there is an isomorphism
\det(T\Sigma)\cong\zeta
.
We thus obtain a bijection of sets

\operatorname{Pin}^{-}(T\Sigma)\to\operatorname{Spin}(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta)

(depending only on the choice of isomorphism
\det(T\Sigma)\cong\zeta
). The remainder of the construction in
[
38
, p. 203]
is the definition of
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})
we have given above and so the bijection of line (
11
) factors as

\operatorname{Pin}^{-}(T\Sigma)\xrightarrow{1:1}\operatorname{Spin}(T\Sigma\oplus\zeta)\xrightarrow{q_{KT}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of}\\
\text{$(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma})$}\end{array}\right\}.

It follows that
q_{KT}
is a bijection as claimed.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

We remark at this point that Kirby-Taylor do not explicitly allow for the possibility that the surface
\Sigma
has boundary. However, we do explicitly allow this possibility. To justify this, we note that their proofs that we cited in the proof of Theorem
6.7
remain valid (without modification) when
\Sigma
has boundary.

The following is a standard lemma and we omit the proof.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\zeta
be a spin vector bundle over a space
B
. Let
i\colon K\to B
be a map of spaces inducing an isomorphism
H_{1}(K;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong H_{1}(B;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Then the induced map on the sets of spin structures
\operatorname{Spin}(\zeta)\to\operatorname{Spin}(i^{*}\zeta)
is a bijection.

We use the previous theorem by Kirby-Taylor in the proof of the following.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
F
,
F_{1}
, and
F_{2}
be nonorientable surfaces, with connected
nonempty boundary.

(1)

Let
\xi=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathring{E}\to F)
be a
vector bundle with orientable total space.
Write
\mathring{Y}
for the total space of the associated
S^{1}
-bundle
S(\xi)
.
Fix a choice of section
s\colon F\to\mathring{Y}
and a choice of
S^{1}
-fibre
\mu\subseteq\mathring{Y}
.
There is a bijection of sets

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Psi_{s,\mu}\colon\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y})&\xrightarrow{1:1}&\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{\mu})\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of}\\
\text{$(H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s(F)})$}\end{array}\right\}\\
\mathfrak{s}&\mapsto&(\mathfrak{s}|_{\mu},q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})).\end{array}

To define
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})
, we take
\zeta:=\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}}
and use the spin structure on
\mathring{Y}
to induce a spin structure on
Ts(F)\oplus\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}}\cong T\mathring{Y}|_{s(F)}
.

(2)

Let
\Phi\colon\xi_{1}\cong\xi_{2}
be an isomorphism of nonorientable 2-plane vector bundles with orientable total space, covering a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon F_{1}\to F_{2}
.
Write
\mathring{Y}_{i}
for the total space of the associated
S^{1}
-bundles
S(\xi_{i})
. Fix a choice of section
s_{1}\colon F_{1}\to\mathring{Y}_{1}|_{F_{1}}
and a choice of
S^{1}
-fibre
\mu_{1}\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{1}
.
Write
s_{2}=\Phi\circ s_{1}\circ\varphi^{-1}
and
\mu_{2}:=\Phi(\mu_{1})
.
Then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}_{2}|_{\mu_{2}})\times\left\{\parbox{128.0374pt}{\centering\penalty\ $\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of\\
\penalty\ $(H_{1}(s_{2}(F_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s_{2}(F)})$\@add@centering}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y}_{1}|_{\mu_{1}})\times\left\{\parbox{128.0374pt}{\centering\penalty\ $\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of\\
\penalty\ $(H_{1}(s_{1}(F_{1});\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s_{1}(F)})$\@add@centering}\right\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{s_{2},\mu_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{s_{1},\mu_{1}}}

where the
\Psi_{s_{i},\mu_{i}}
are the bijections of sets from the first item, and

{\widehat{\Phi}}(\mathfrak{t},q):=((\Phi|_{\mu_{2}})^{*}\mathfrak{t},q\circ\Phi_{*}).

Proof.

We begin with the first item.
Take the standard cell structure for
F

(see the conventions at the end of the introduction),
with its basis of loops
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{h}
.
Let
A\subseteq\mathring{Y}
be a connected 1-complex comprising a single
0
-cell, the
S^{1}
-fibre loop
\mu
, and the collection of loops
s(\gamma_{i})
.
Applying Lemma
6.9
to the inclusion map of
A
, we obtain a bijection
\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y})\to\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{A})
.
Here we used Proposition
4.2
and the universal coefficient theorem to compute the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-coefficient homology of
\mathring{Y}
.
Now, as
A
is a bouquet of circles, there is a bijection

\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{A})\xrightarrow{1:1}\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{\mu})\times\prod_{i=1}^{h}\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{s(\gamma_{i})}).

Hence it now suffices to prove that the latter factor is in 1:1 correspondence with the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement factor from the statement of the proposition.

For this, first note that the collection of loops
s(\gamma_{i})
form the
1
-skeleton of
s(F)
and so, as
s(F)
is homotopy equivalent to this
1
-complex, restriction determines a bijection

\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{s(F)})\xrightarrow{1:1}\prod_{i=1}^{h}\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{s(\gamma_{i})}).

It is clear that there is a bijection between
\operatorname{Spin}(T\mathring{Y}|_{s(F)})
and
\operatorname{Spin}(Ts(F)\oplus\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}})
. We now wish to apply Theorem
6.7
using the surface
\Sigma=s(F)
and the line bundle
\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}}
. To see that this theorem is indeed applicable, observe that as
\mathring{Y}
is orientable, the product formula for Stiefel-Whitney classes implies that
w_{1}(Ts(F))=w_{1}(\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}})
.
Thus, by Theorem
6.7
, the Kirby-Taylor quadratic refinement determines a bijection of sets

\Psi_{s,\mu}\colon\operatorname{Spin}(Ts(F)\oplus\nu_{s(F)\subseteq\mathring{Y}})\xrightarrow{1:1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{4}$-quadratic refinements of}\\
\text{$(H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s(F)})$}\end{array}\right\},

and this completes the proof of the statement in the first item.

We will now prove the second claim, namely that
\Psi_{s_{1},\mu_{1}}\circ\Phi^{*}={\widehat{\Phi}}\circ\Psi_{s_{2},\mu_{2}}
.
In other words, for all
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y}_{2})
, we will show

((\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{s})|_{\mu_{1}},q_{KT_{1}}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{s}))=((\Phi|_{\mu_{2}})^{*}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\mu_{2}}),q_{KT_{2}}(\mathfrak{s})\circ\Phi_{*}).

As
\mu_{2}=\Phi(\mu_{1})
, by definition, equality in the first argument is clear.
Given
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y}_{2})
, we will now show the equality

(12)

q_{KT_{1}}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{s})=q_{KT_{2}}(\mathfrak{s})\circ\Phi_{*}\colon H_{1}(s_{1}(F_{1}))\to\mathbb{Z}_{4},

which will complete the proof of the second claim.
Let
\gamma_{1}\subseteq F_{1}
be an embedded circle and let
\gamma_{2}=\varphi(\gamma_{1})
be the corresponding loop in
F_{2}
.
This way
s_{1}(\gamma_{1})\subseteq s_{1}(F_{1})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{1}
is an embedded circle and, by definition of
s_{2}=\Phi\circ s_{1}\circ\varphi^{-1}
, we deduce that
\Phi(s_{1}(\gamma_{1}))=s_{2}(\gamma_{2})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{2}
is similarly an embedded circle.
Pick a framing
f_{2}\colon\nu_{s_{2}(\gamma_{2})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{2}}\cong s_{2}(\gamma_{2})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is odd with respect to the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
.
Since
\Phi
is a bundle isomorphism, one checks that the framing defined by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\colon\nu_{s_{1}(\gamma_{1})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{s_{2}(\gamma_{2})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}(\gamma_{2})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}(\gamma_{1})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{-1}\times\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is odd with respect to the spin structure
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{s}
.
Choose an orientation on
\gamma_{1}
and use the induced orientations on
s_{1}(\gamma_{1})
and
s_{2}(\gamma_{2})
. Now
q_{KT_{1}}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{s})(s_{1}(\gamma_{1}))
is calculated by counting right-handed half-twists of
\nu_{s_{1}(\gamma_{1})\subseteq s_{1}(F_{1})}\subseteq\nu_{s_{1}(\gamma_{1})\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{1}}
with respect to the odd framing
f_{1}
. On the other hand,
q_{KT_{2}}(\mathfrak{s})\circ\Phi_{*}(s_{1}(\gamma_{1}))=q_{KT_{2}}(\mathfrak{s})(\Phi(s_{1}(\gamma_{1})))
is calculated
by counting right-handed half-twists of
\nu_{\Phi(s_{1}(\gamma))\subseteq s_{2}(F_{2})}\subseteq\nu_{\Phi(s_{1}(\gamma))\subseteq\mathring{Y}_{2}}
with respect to the odd framing
f_{2}
.
These counts agree thanks to our choice of framings. This establishes (
12
) and therefore proves the second claim.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

A similar statement to Proposition
6.10
holds when the surfaces are closed. To modify the statement to the case of
F
closed, one must consider the punctured surface
\mathring{F}
in order to choose the initial section
s\colon\mathring{F}\to\mathring{Y}|_{\mathring{F}}
(similarly for
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
). The remainder of the statement holds verbatim and the proof proceeds entirely similarly.

6.2.
The Guillou-Marin quadratic refinement

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a properly embedded nonorientable surface with connected nonempty boundary.
We review the definition of the Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
quadratic refinement
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, refining the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued intersection form of
F
.

Let
\gamma
be an embedded circle in the interior of
F
and let
D\looparrowright D^{4}
be an immersed disc with

\partial D=\gamma
and such that
D
is
transverse to
F
along
\gamma
; we refer the reader to the diagram on
[
20
, p.15]
for the local model we are describing, where
D=B
,
\gamma=c
, and
F=A
, in their terminology.
Moreover, assume the interior of
D
meets
F
transversely, and away from
\gamma
.
Choose an orientation on
\gamma
, and thus a positive tangent direction.
There are two possible choices of framing
\nu_{D\subseteq D^{4}}\cong D\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, one for each orientation of the fibres.
Choose the framing with the property that, writing
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
, the frame

\text{(outwards-facing normal to $\gamma$ in $D$)}\times\text{(positive tangent to\penalty\ $\gamma$)}\times e_{1}\times e_{2}\in\operatorname{Fr}(T_{p}D^{4})

agrees with the ambient orientation of
D^{4}
at all points
p\in\gamma
.

Definition 6.12
.

Given an embedded loop
\gamma\subseteq F
, choose an orientation on
\gamma
and a disc
D
as above. We then denote by
W(D)
the number of right-handed half-twists completed by the sub-bundle
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq F}\subseteq\nu_{D\subseteq D^{4}}|_{\gamma}
in one complete rotation around
\gamma
.
Define
3
3

3

Guillou-Marin define
q_{GM}
for closed surfaces in closed
4
-manifolds but their definition extends to properly embedded surfaces in
4
-manifolds with boundary, see e.g.
[
25
]
.

q_{GM}(\gamma):=W(D)+2(D\cdot F)\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}.

6.3.
The exterior Guillou-Marin form, in the presence of a nice section

In this section we show it is possible to compute the Guillou-Marin quadratic refinement of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface using immersed discs whose interiors lie entirely in the complement of
F
.
Before doing this, we introduce a mechanism that will allow us to choose immersed discs so that their interiors, minus a boundary collar, miss the tubular neighbourhood.

Throughout this subsection, let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a properly embedded surface with connected nonempty boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
. Recall the standing convention that we have fixed a tubular neighbourhood
\alpha\colon(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}),S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))\xrightarrow{\cong}(\alpha(D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})),\partial X_{F}\setminus\mathring{X}_{K})
, where
\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}
denotes the normal bundle of
F
and
D(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
and
S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
respectively denote its disc and circle bundles.

Lemma 6.13
.

The bundle
S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
admits a nice section.

Proof.

Write
h
for the nonorientable genus of
F
, write
\bigvee_{i=1}^{h}\gamma_{i}\subseteq F
for the
1
-subcomplex that is a deformation retract of
F
, using the standard cell decomposition of
F
, and let
b_{i}:=[\gamma_{i}]\in H_{1}(F)
be the corresponding homology generators.
Note that
w_{1}(F)(b_{i})\neq 0
for all
i=1,\dots,h
.
Choose a section
s\colon F\to{S}(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})

and consider the composition

\mathbb{Z}\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{h}\rangle\cong H_{1}(F)\xrightarrow{{s}_{*}}H_{1}(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{*}}H_{1}(\partial X_{F})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H_{1}(X_{F}),

where
\iota\colon\partial X_{F}\to X_{F}
is the inclusion.
By Proposition
4.2
we may write this map as

(13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{h}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}\langle\mu\rangle\oplus\mathbb{Z}\langle s_{*}(b_{1}),\dots,s_{*}(b_{h})\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\circ\alpha_{*}}

Here,
\mu
is a generator of the first homology of a fibre of
S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
.
We now intend to define a new section
s^{\prime}\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
, which will be the promised nice section.
Because
F
is homotopy equivalent to a wedge of circles, it is sufficient to describe our new section individually on the 1-cells
\gamma_{i}
.
Recall that a nonorientable circle bundle
\xi
over a circle has exactly two sections up to isotopy.
The total space
E(\xi)
is a Klein bottle with homology
H_{1}(E(\xi))\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and with respect to some such choice of isomorphism, the two possible sections represent the classes
(1,0)
and
(1,1)
. In our case, the choices of
\gamma_{i}
and
\mu
fix such an isomorphism and so the image in
H_{1}(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
of the two possible sections of
S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})|_{\gamma_{i}}
are
s_{*}(b_{i})
and
s_{*}(b_{i})+\mu
.
For each
i
such that the composition in (
13
)
\iota_{*}\circ\alpha_{*}\circ s_{*}(b_{i})
is nontrivial, modify
s|_{\gamma_{i}}
to
s^{\prime}|_{\gamma_{i}}
by switching to the other section of this nontrivial circle bundle.
On all other
\gamma_{i}
, i.e. where
\iota_{*}\circ\alpha_{*}\circ s_{*}(b_{i})
is trivial, set
s|_{\gamma_{i}}=s^{\prime}|_{\gamma_{i}}
. This determines the new section
s^{\prime}\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
.

We now verify that
s^{\prime}
is nice.
Note that for
i
such that
\iota_{*}\circ\alpha_{*}\circ s_{*}(b_{i})
is nontrivial, we have that
s^{\prime}_{*}(b_{i})=s_{*}(b_{i})+\mu\in H_{1}(S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}))
.
Thus
\iota_{*}\circ\alpha_{*}\circ s^{\prime}_{*}(b_{i})
is now trivial for all
i
, and so
s^{\prime}
is a nice section, as desired.
∎

Now let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface and suppose that we have chosen a tubular neighbourhood
\alpha
for
F\subseteq D^{4}
, together with a nice section
s\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
.
For any embedded circle
\gamma\subseteq s(F)
, the facts that
s
is a nice section and that
F
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, imply
\alpha(\gamma)

is null-homotopic in
X_{F}
. So there exists a properly immersed disc
D^{\prime}\looparrowright X_{F}
that is transverse to
s(F)
along
\partial D^{\prime}=\alpha(\gamma)
.

Definition 6.14
.

Let
\alpha
be a tubular neighbourhood for
F\subseteq D^{4}
, together with a nice section
s\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
.
Let
D^{\prime}\looparrowright X_{F}
be a properly immersed disc that is transverse to
s(F)
along
\partial D^{\prime}=\alpha(\gamma)
.
for some embedded loop
\gamma\subseteq s(F)
.
Choose an orientation on
\gamma
and choose a framing
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X_{F}}|_{\alpha(\gamma)}\cong\alpha(\gamma)\times\mathbb{R}\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
such that the frame

\text{(outwards facing normal to $\alpha(\gamma)$ in\penalty\ $D^{\prime}$)}\times\text{(positive tangent to\penalty\ $\alpha(\gamma)$)}\times e_{1}\times e_{2}\in\operatorname{Fr}(T_{p}X_{F})

agrees with the ambient orientation of
X_{F}
at all points
p\in\alpha(\gamma)
.
Define
W(D^{\prime})
as the number of right-handed half-twists of
\nu_{\alpha(\gamma)\subseteq\alpha(s(F))}\subseteq\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X_{F}}|_{\alpha(\gamma)}
in one complete loop around
\gamma
in the positive direction.
Define

\widehat{q}_{GM}(\gamma):=W(D^{\prime}).

As there is a basis of
H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
by oriented embedded loops, this extends linearly to a function
\widehat{q}_{GM}\colon H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, which we call the
exterior
Guillou-Marin function.
The proof that this is well defined on
H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is similar to the one for the Guillou-Marin form; see Theorem
6.18
.

The next proposition relates
\widehat{q}_{GM}
to the Guillou-Marin form, and in particular shows that its isometry type does not depend on the choice of the tubular neighbourhood
\alpha
of
F
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, and fix a nice section
s\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
.
Then the exterior Guillou-Marin quadratic function is a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form
(H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s(F)})
.

Moreover, the isometry of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection forms
s_{*}\colon H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
induces an isometry between the Guillou-Marin quadratic form and the exterior Guillou-Marin quadratic form.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be a tubular neighbourhood for
F\subseteq D^{4}
, let
\delta\subseteq F
be an embedded loop, and let
D^{\prime}\looparrowright X_{F}
be a properly immersed disc that is transverse to
s(F)
along
\partial D^{\prime}=\alpha(s(\delta))
.
For each
p\in\delta
, write
I_{p}
for the radial line between
p
and
s(p)
in the fibre
\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}|_{p}
. The union
A:=\coprod_{p\in\delta}I_{p}
is an embedded annulus in
\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}}
.
The union
D:=D^{\prime}\cup_{\alpha(s(\delta))}\alpha(A)
may be used to compute
q_{GM}(\delta)
.
The interior of the disc
D
does not intersect
F
and therefore
q_{GM}(\delta)=W(D)
.
As
\alpha(A)
is an embedded annulus with trivial normal bundle in
D^{4}
, and the framing conventions for computing
W(D)
and
W(D^{\prime})
are the same, we also have
W(D)=W(D^{\prime})
. Thus,
q_{GM}(\delta)=\widehat{q}_{GM}(s(\delta))
. This shows both that
\widehat{q}_{GM}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form on
s(F)
and that
s_{*}
is indeed an isometry of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic forms.
∎

There is an isometry between the (exterior) Guillou-Marin form for
F
and the particular Kirby-Taylor forms that correspond to the spin structures on
\partial X_{F}
that extend to
X_{F}
:

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, and let
s\colon F\to S(\nu_{F\subseteq D^{4}})
be a nice section. Write
\mathring{Y}\subseteq\partial X_{F}
for the boundary of the tubular neighbourhood of
F
.
Choose a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
for
\mathring{Y}
that extends to a spin structure on
X_{F}
.
Then there is an equality of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic forms

((H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s(F)},\widehat{q}_{GM})=((H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{s(F)},q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})),

where the latter form
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})
is furnished by Proposition
6.10
.

Proof.

To ease notation, in this proof we will write
X:=X_{F}
and
\Sigma:=s(F)
, and we will henceforth identify
\Sigma
with
\alpha(\Sigma)
. Let
\gamma\subseteq\Sigma
be an oriented, embedded loop.
We unravel the definition of
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})(\gamma)
from Proposition
6.10
. In the notation of that section, set
\zeta=\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}
.
The spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\mathring{Y}
gives a framing of

T\mathring{Y}|_{\Sigma}\cong T\Sigma\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}.

This, together with the choice of orientation on
\gamma
, is used to define the concept of an odd framing for the 2-plane bundle

\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\oplus\nu_{\Sigma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}\cong\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}.

Then
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})(\gamma)
is the number of right-hand half twists modulo
4
made by
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\subseteq\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}
as it rotates around
\gamma
, with respect to a choice of odd framing on this ambient
2
-plane bundle.

We compare with the process for computing the exterior Guillou-Marin form.
Choose a properly immersed disc
D^{\prime}\looparrowright X_{F}
for computing
\widehat{q}_{GM}(\gamma)
.
Write

(14)

f\colon\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

for the framing coming from restricting the framing on
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}
determined by the convention described in Definition
6.14
.
Then
\widehat{q}_{GM}(\gamma)
is the number of right-hand half-twists modulo
4
in
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\subseteq\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}
with respect to
f
.
Since
D^{\prime}
is transverse to
s(F)
along
\gamma
, there is a bundle isomorphism
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}\cong\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}
.

As both
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})(\gamma)
and
\widehat{q}_{GM}(\gamma)
are counts of right-handed half twists of
\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\Sigma}\subseteq\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}
, to show they are equal it suffices to prove that
f
is an odd framing.
In other words, we must show that the following two framings induce the same orientation, but disagree as framings:

\mathcal{F}\colon T\mathring{Y}|_{\gamma}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{G}_{f}\colon T\mathring{Y}|_{\gamma}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

where
\mathcal{F}
is obtained by using the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
\mathring{Y}
and, recall,
\mathcal{G}_{f}
is defined as the sequence of bundle isomorphisms

T\mathring{Y}|_{\gamma}\cong T\gamma\oplus\nu_{\gamma\subseteq\mathring{Y}}\cong T\gamma\oplus\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}\cong\gamma\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Writing
n
for the outwards-facing normal to
\gamma
in
D^{\prime}
,
t
for the tangent to
\gamma
, and
\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle
for the frame specified by
f
, the frame
n\times t\times e_{1}\times e_{2}
agrees with the ambient orientation of
X
, by definition of
f
. Using the “outwards normal first” convention, the orientation on
X
induces an orientation on
\mathring{Y}
. Our frame includes
n
as the first vector already, so the frame
t\times e_{1}\times e_{2}
, (i.e. the frame specified by
\mathcal{G}_{f}
) induces this ambient orientation on
\mathring{Y}
. Now consider that
\mathcal{F}
comes from a spin structure on
\mathring{Y}
, and this spin structure extends to a spin structure on
X
, by hypothesis. It must be that the orientations underlying these spin structures agree with the orientations on
X
and
\mathring{Y}
. Hence the orientation underlying
\mathcal{F}
is the ambient orientation on
\mathring{Y}
, and the orientations coming from
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
agree, as claimed.

Next, we show that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
disagree, as framings.
Using the outwards-facing normal
n
, complete each of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
to a framing of

TX|_{\gamma}\cong\nu_{\gamma\subseteq D^{\prime}}\oplus T\mathring{Y}|_{\gamma}.

We refer to the completed framings as
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}^{\prime}
, respectively.
We now show that these two framings disagree, as this will imply that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
also disagree.

The framing
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
extends to a framing of
TX|_{D^{\prime}}
because the spin structure on
\mathring{Y}
used to define
\alpha
extends to
X
(this uses the “outwards-facing normal first” convention, again). The framing
\mathcal{G}_{f}^{\prime}
splits as a framing of
TD^{\prime}|_{\gamma}\cong\nu_{\gamma\subseteq D^{\prime}}\oplus T\gamma
and of
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}|_{\gamma}
. The latter of these framings is
f
and extends to
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}
, by definition; recall (
14
).
The former of these framings is given by
n\times t
. An ordered pair of framings of a
2
-plane bundle over a circle, which correspond to the same orientation, determines an element of
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SO}(2))
. Fix the (unique) framing that extends to a framing of
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}
as our reference framing; this framing determines the trivial class in
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SO}(2))
. On the other hand, the framing
n\times t
determines a generator of
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SO}(2))\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and so determines the Lie framing.
Therefore this latter framing does not extend to a framing of
\nu_{D^{\prime}\subseteq X}
.
Thus, overall,
\mathcal{G}_{f}^{\prime}
does not extend to
TX|_{D^{\prime}}
. This proves that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{f}
are different framings of
TX|_{\gamma}
.

But the framings
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{f}
were obtained from
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
by the addition of the same line bundle (given by the outwards facing normal), from which we deduce that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}_{f}
are different framings. This completes the proof that
f
is an odd framing, which completes the proof of Lemma
6.16
.
∎

Corollary 6.17
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface, and write
\mathring{Y}\subseteq\partial X_{F}
for the boundary of the tubular neighbourhood of
F
.
Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y})
be a spin structure and let
s
be a nice section.
Then
\mathfrak{s}
extends to
X_{F}
if and only if the quadratic refinement
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{s})
associated to the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
by Proposition
6.10
agrees with the exterior Guillou-Marin quadratic refinement.

Proof.

Write
S
for the set of spin structures on
\mathring{Y}
that extend to
X_{F}
.
Write
T
for the set of
\mathfrak{t}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\mathring{Y})
such that
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{t})(\gamma)=\widehat{q}_{GM}(\gamma)
for every
\gamma\in H_{1}(s(F);\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. The corollary is equivalent to the statement
S=T
.
Lemma
6.16
shows that if
\mathfrak{t}
extends to
X_{F}
then we have an identification between
\widehat{q}_{GM}
and
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{t})
, and so
S\subseteq T
.

Now note that by Proposition
6.10
(1), exactly two spin structures on
\mathring{Y}
correspond to the Guillou-Marin quadratic refinement, so we have
|T|=2
. The corollary will be proved if we can show
|S|=2
.
We know
|S|\leq|T|=2
because
S\subseteq T
.
As noted earlier there are two spin structures on
X_{F}
and these induce at most two spin structures on
\mathring{Y}
. We write
S=\{\mathfrak{s}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{2}\}
where
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
are the restrictions of the two distinct spin structures on
X_{F}
to
\mathring{Y}
.
These spin structures on
X_{F}
differ on their restriction to a meridian
\mu\subseteq X_{F}
, which is nontrivial in
H_{1}(\mathring{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
Thus by Proposition
6.10
(1) we have
\mathfrak{s}_{1}\neq\mathfrak{s}_{2}
and so
|S|=2
as required.
∎

A
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic form
(P,Q,q)
over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
has a Brown-Kervaire invariant
\beta(P,Q,q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{8}
; see e.g.
[
11
,
39
]
.
We will need to use the following theorem.

Theorem 6.18

(Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
, Kirby-Taylor
[
38
]
, Gilmer-Livingston
[
25
]
)
.

The Guillou-Marin function
q_{GM}
is well defined on homology and gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-intersection form
(H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2});Q_{F})
.
Moreover, if
F
has normal Euler number
e

(
which is necessarily even by Theorem
2.5
)
, then

\beta(H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F},q_{GM})\equiv-\frac{1}{2}e+4\operatorname{Arf}(\partial F)\pmod{8}.

Proof.

None of the sources given above state the theorem exactly as we do, so we now discuss how this version arises from the literature.
Guillou-Marin
[
28
]
proved the theorem for closed nonorientable manifolds, only in the smooth category. Gilmer-Livingston
[
25
, Theorem 7]
proved the theorem, as stated above, but again only in the smooth category. The arguments of Guillou-Marin that their form is well-defined and gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic enhancement extend, without changes, to the topological setting, and the case that the surface has non-empty boundary, using the existence of normal bundles
[
20
, Theorem 9.3A]
and topological transversality in dimension 4
[
20
, §9.5]
.

To extend the stated congruence to the topological category, begin by recalling that the Guillou-Marin formula for smooth
closed
surfaces was explicitly generalised in
[
38
, Corollary 9.3]
to the topological category. To state the generalisation for our purposes, let
\Sigma\subseteq S^{4}
be a closed surface and define a
characteristic structure
(
[
38
, Definition 6.1]
) to be a
\operatorname{Pin}^{-}
-structure on
S^{4}\setminus\Sigma
that does not extend over
\Sigma
. Such structures are in 1:1 correspondence with
H^{1}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
, so there is a unique such structure, in this case coming from the unique spin structure on
S^{4}
. This determines a
\operatorname{Pin}^{-}
structure on
\Sigma
in a certain way described in
[
38
, Lemma 6.2]
, and hence a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic refinement of
(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma})
, using (
11
). Proposition
6.15
and Lemma
6.16
combine to show that this Kirby-Taylor refinement agrees with the Guillou-Marin refinement (this proposition and lemma are technically for surfaces with boundary, but can be applied to
(S^{4},\Sigma)
, by first removing a ball pair
(D^{4},D^{2})
). Given the discussion above, the result
[
38
, Corollary 9.3]
reads as

(15)

2\cdot\beta(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{\Sigma},q_{GM})\equiv-e(\Sigma)\pmod{16},

which concludes the proof in the closed case.
4
4

4

In our statement of the congruence (
15
), we have corrected what we think is a sign error in Kirby-Taylor; our statement has the same signs as in Guillou-Marin and in Gilmer-Livingston. We think the sign error in
[
38
, Theorem 9.3]
arises from their computation in
[
38
, Theorem 6.3]
. We note that if the Kirby-Taylor sign is in fact correct, and (
15
) has the wrong sign, this is a global change and will not affect our only application of Theorem
6.18
, in Corollary
6.19
, below. Indeed, we use it to show that if two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces have the same knot as boundary and same normal Euler number then they have the same Brown invariant, and this deduction is valid regardless of which sign is correct overall in this congruence.

Finally, to extend to surfaces with boundary, the discussion of Gilmer-Livingston in
[
25
, §2.2.5]
applies equally well in the topological category to adjust the formula from the closed case to the case of a properly embedded surface with boundary
F\subseteq D^{4}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

If
F_{1},F_{2}\subseteq D^{4}
have the same nonorientable genus, connected, nonempty boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
, and normal Euler number, then there exists a homeomorphism
f\colon F_{1}\to F_{2}
, restricting to the identity map on
K
, and inducing an isometry between the Guillou-Marin forms

f_{*}\colon(H_{1}(F_{1};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{1}},q_{GM_{1}})\xrightarrow{\cong}(H_{1}(F_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{2}},q_{GM_{2}}).

Proof.

Since both
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
have the same nonorientable genus, there is an isometry of intersection forms
(H_{1}(F_{1};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{1}})\cong(H_{1}(F_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{2}})
.
As the surfaces have the same normal Euler number and connected boundary
K
, Theorem
6.18
implies that their Guillou-Marin forms have the same Brown-Kervaire invariant.
As mentioned in
[
28
, final remark of Section II]
, this implies there is an isometry of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-quadratic forms
(H_{1}(F_{1};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{1}},q_{GM_{1}})\cong(H_{1}(F_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{2}},q_{GM_{2}})
; this remark concerns abstract non-degenerate quadratic forms and therefore applies since
\partial F_{1}=\partial F_{2}
is connected.
Finally, it is shown in
[
23
, Theorem 6.1]

that every isometry
(H_{1}(F_{1};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{1}})\cong(H_{1}(F_{2};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),Q_{F_{2}})
of intersection forms of compact, nonorientable surfaces with connected nonempty boundary is induced by some homeomorphism between the surfaces that fixes the boundary pointwise.
∎

6.4.
The proof of Proposition
6.2

We begin with a lemma that will allow us to align choices of nice sections. We then move on to the proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Given a surface
\Sigma
with connected, nonempty boundary and a bundle
\mathring{\xi}=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathring{N}\to\Sigma)
, we write
\mathring{N}|_{\partial\Sigma}
for the total space of
\mathring{\xi}|_{\partial\Sigma}
.
Since
[\partial\Sigma]=0\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, it follows that
w_{1}(\mathring{\xi})([\partial\Sigma])=0
, and therefore
\mathring{\xi}|_{\partial\Sigma}
