is a trivial bundle.

Lemma 6.20
.

For
i=0,1
, let
F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}
be nonorientable surfaces with the same nonorientable genus, and the same connected boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
.
For brevity, write
\mathring{\xi_{i}}=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathring{N_{i}}\to F_{i})
for the respective normal bundles.
Suppose
s_{0},s_{1}\colon F_{i}\to\mathring{N_{i}}
are nonvanishing sections.

(1)

Given a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}
, restricting to the identity map on
K
, there exists a vector bundle isomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathring{\xi_{0}}\to\mathring{\xi_{1}}
covering
\varphi
and sending
s_{0}
to
s_{1}
.

(2)

Suppose moreover that
s_{0}|_{K}
and
s_{1}|_{K}
are isotopic as sections of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
. Then, after an isotopy of
s_{1}
supported in a collar of
K\subseteq F_{0}
, the isomorphism
\Phi
may be assumed to restrict to the identity on
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
.

Proof.

We prove the first item.
For
i=0,1
, the section
s_{i}
determines a (trivial) line bundle
\zeta_{i}=(\mathbb{R}\to E_{i}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}F_{i})
.
Namely
\zeta_{i}
is the subbundle of
\mathring{\xi}_{i}
with fibre over
x\in F_{i}
given by
\mathbb{R}\langle s_{i}(x)\rangle
.
Every
v\in E_{i}
can be written uniquely as
v=\lambda_{v}^{i}s_{i}(\pi_{i}(v))\in(E_{i})_{\pi_{i}(v)}
for some
\lambda_{v}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.
It follows that the assignment
v\mapsto\lambda_{v}^{0}s_{1}(\varphi(\pi_{0}(v)))
defines
a bundle isomorphism
\alpha\colon\zeta_{0}\cong\zeta_{1}
, that covers
\varphi
and
satisfies
\alpha\circ s_{0}=s_{1}\circ\varphi
.

We now extend
\alpha
to a bundle isomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathring{\xi}_{0}\cong\mathring{\xi}_{1}
, using orthogonal complements.
Choose Riemannian metrics on
\mathring{\xi}_{0}
and
\mathring{\xi}_{1}
which agree over
K
, and consider the perpendicular bundles
\zeta_{0}^{\perp}
and
\zeta_{1}^{\perp}
.
Given an
embedded loop
\gamma\subseteq F_{i}
,
the line bundle

\zeta_{i}^{\perp}|_{\gamma}
is orientable or nonorientable according to whether
w_{1}(\mathring{\xi}_{i})
is trivial on
\gamma
or not. Since
\varphi
is a homeomorphism, for
\gamma\subseteq F_{0}
we have that
w_{1}(F_{0})(\gamma)=w_{1}(F_{1})(\varphi(\gamma))
. Therefore since the total space of
\mathring{\xi}_{i}
is orientable, we have that
w_{1}(\mathring{\xi}_{0})(\gamma)=w_{1}(\mathring{\xi}_{1})(\varphi(\gamma))
.
Thus in both cases there is a bundle isomorphism
\zeta_{0}^{\perp}|_{\gamma}\cong\zeta_{1}^{\perp}|_{\varphi(\gamma)}
.
Because line bundles are classified by
w_{1}
,
this implies there exists a bundle isomorphism
\beta\colon\zeta_{0}^{\perp}\to\zeta_{1}^{\perp}
covering
\varphi
.
Combining
\alpha
and
\beta
, we obtain a bundle isomorphism

\Phi\colon\mathring{\xi}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\zeta_{0}\oplus\zeta_{0}^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\alpha\oplus\beta}\zeta_{1}\oplus\zeta_{1}^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathring{\xi}_{1},

which covers
\varphi

and satisfies
\Phi\circ s_{0}=s_{1}\circ\varphi
.

We prove the second item.
The normal bundles
\mathring{\xi}_{0}
and
\mathring{\xi}_{1}
both restrict to
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
over
K
, and recall that our choices of Riemannian metric for
\mathring{\xi}_{0}
and
\mathring{\xi}_{1}
were constructed to agree over
K
.
An isotopy of
s_{1}
over
K
that makes it agree with
s_{0}
thus determines an isotopy between the line bundles
\zeta_{1}|_{K}
and
\zeta_{0}|_{K}
, as subbundles of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
.
Also, because the metrics agree over the boundary, this isotopy induces an isotopy between
\zeta_{1}^{\perp}|_{K}
and
\zeta_{0}^{\perp}|_{K}
as subbundles of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
.
This means we obtain an isotopy from
\Phi|_{\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}}
to a bundle automorphism of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
that sends
\zeta_{0}|_{K}
to itself and
\zeta_{0}^{\perp}|_{K}
to itself.
Since
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}\cong\zeta_{0}|_{K}\oplus\zeta_{0}^{\perp}|_{K}
, it follows that this automorphism is in fact the identity.
Taper the isotopies of
\Phi|_{\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}}
and of
s_{1}
over a collar neighbourhood of
K
in
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
, to obtain the desired isotopy of
\Phi
to a new bundle isomorphism
\Phi^{\prime}\colon\mathring{\xi}_{0}\to\mathring{\xi}_{1}
that is the identity over
K
, and to obtain the claimed isotopy of
s_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 6.21
.

For
i=0,1
, let
F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}
be nonorientable surfaces with the same nonorientable genus, the same connected boundary
K\subseteq S^{3}
and each with normal Euler number
e
. Let
s_{i}\colon F_{i}\to\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}}
be nonvanishing sections. Then
s_{0}|_{K}
and
s_{1}|_{K}
are isotopic.

Proof.

Recall that, by convention in this section, the tubular neighbourhood of any surface with boundary a given
K\subseteq S^{3}
extends a given tubular neighbourhood for
K
. So there are assumed identifications
\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}}|_{K}=\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
for
i=0,1
. Isotopy classes of nonvanishing sections of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with framings of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}\cong S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we will identify the two, using the same notation for a section and the corresponding framing.
Write
\tau\colon K\to\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
for a nonvanishing section corresponding to the Seifert framing of
K
. The set of framings is in affine correspondence with
\mathbb{Z}
, so for
i=0,1
, the difference between framings determines an integer
d(\tau,s_{i}|_{K})\in\mathbb{Z}
. We claim that for
i=0,1
there is an equality
d(\tau,s_{i}|_{K})=e
. To see this, consider that if we add
-k_{i}
full turns to
\tau
to make the difference 0, and thus to make
\tau
isotopic to
s_{i}|_{K}
, this introduces
-k_{i}
signed intersections between a generic extension of
\tau
to
F_{i}
and the 0-section of
\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}}
. As
s_{i}|_{K}
extends to a nonvanishing section, i.e.
e(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}},s_{i}|_{K})
=0, this implies that
d(\tau,s_{i}|_{K})=k_{i}=e(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}},\tau)=:e
(using the interpretation of
e(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}},\tau)
as a signed intersection count from Remark
2.1
).

We thus have that

d(\tau,s_{1}|_{\nu_{K}})=d(\tau,s_{1}|_{\nu_{K}})
,
from which we deduce

d(s_{0}|_{\nu_{K}},s_{1}|_{\nu_{K}})=d(\tau,s_{0}|_{\nu_{K}})-d(\tau,s_{1}|_{\nu_{K}})=0.

So
s_{0}|_{\nu_{K}}
is isotopic to
s_{1}|_{\nu_{K}}
as claimed.
∎

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Since the surfaces
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
have the same normal Euler number and nonorientable genus, by Corollary
6.19
we can, and will, choose a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}
inducing an isometry of their Guillou-Marin forms and restricting to the identity map on
K
.
The strategy for the remainder of the proof is to extend
\varphi
to a homeomorphism
\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}\cong\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}}
, and then to
restrict to
S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})\cong S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}})
. Finally, extending by the identity on
X_{K}
leads to the sought-for homeomorphism
\partial X_{F_{0}}\cong\partial X_{F_{1}}
. Now, the details.

Recall that for
i=0,1
, we fixed a tubular neighbourhood

\alpha_{i}
for
\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}}
, so that
\alpha_{i}(S(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}}))=\partial X_{F_{i}}\setminus\mathring{X}_{K}
.
By Lemma
6.13
, we may pick nice sections
s_{i}\colon F_{i}\to S(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}})|_{F_{i}}
with respect to this tubular neighbourhood.
Thus we may apply Lemma
6.20
(
1
) to obtain a bundle isomorphism

\Phi\colon\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}\cong\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}}

covering
\varphi
. Because the normal Euler numbers of
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
agree, Lemma
6.21
shows
s_{0}|_{K}
and
s_{1}|_{K}
are isotopic as sections of
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
.
As
\varphi
restricts to the identity map on
K
, this means we may apply Lemma
6.20
(
2
).
This achieves that
\Phi
restricts to the identity map on
\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}}
, at the expense of changing
s_{1}
by an isotopy.
We will continue to call this modified section
s_{1}
, and we note that the new
s_{1}
is still a nice section as this property is preserved by isotopy.

Our chosen tubular neighbourhoods
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
satisfy
\alpha_{i}\colon S(\nu_{F_{i}\subseteq D^{4}})\hookrightarrow\partial X_{F_{i}}
for
i=0,1
, and are such that each embedding extends the given tubular neighbourhood of the knot
S(\nu_{K\subseteq S^{3}})\cong\partial X_{K}
.
We thus have a homeomorphism

g:=\alpha_{1}\circ\Phi\circ(\alpha_{0})^{-1}\colon\alpha_{0}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}))\xrightarrow{\cong}\alpha_{1}(S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})).

As
\Phi
restricts to the identity bundle map over
K
, this homeomorphism
g
restricts to the identity map on the tubular neighbourhood of the knot
K
. Thus we may extend
g
by the identity map
\operatorname{Id}\colon X_{K}\to X_{K}
, to define a homeomorphism

(16)

f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial X_{F_{1}}.

We claim
f
has the properties listed in the statement of the proposition.

Property (
2
), that
f|_{X_{K}}
is the identity, is clear from construction. Properties (
3
) and (
4
), that
f
restricts to a map that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary, and that meridians are preserved, follow because
f
was constructed from the restriction of a vector bundle isomorphism
\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}}\cong\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}}
.
Property (
5
), which was about
f
preserving nice sections, is also automatic from the construction because we defined
f
using choices of nice sections.

To finish the proof we prove that
f
has property (
1
).
Pick a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}(\partial X_{F_{1}})
that extends to the exterior
\operatorname{Spin}(X_{F_{1}})
.
Write
\mathfrak{t}:=\alpha_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{s}
for the induced spin structure on
S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}})
.
Choose an
S^{1}
-fibre
\mu_{0}\subseteq S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})
and write
\mu_{1}:=\Phi(\mu_{0})\subseteq S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}})
for the corresponding
S^{1}
-fibre of
S(\nu_{F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}})
.
Using the fact that
\Phi

satisfies
\Phi\circ s_{0}=s_{1}\circ\varphi
, the second item of Proposition
6.10
implies that

(17)

{\widehat{\Phi}}(\mathfrak{t}|_{\mu_{1}},q_{KT}(\mathfrak{t}))=((\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t})|_{\mu_{0}},q_{KT}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t})).

On the left-hand side, the quadratic refinement is, by definition
q_{KT}(\mathfrak{t})\circ\Phi_{*}
,
whereas on the right-hand side the quadratic refinement
q_{KT}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t})
is assigned by the construction in Proposition
6.10
.
Both of these quadratic refinements are defined on
H_{1}(s_{0}(F_{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

We claim that we have the following sequence of equalities of quadratic refinements:

(18)

\widehat{q}_{GM_{0}}=\widehat{q}_{GM_{1}}\circ\Phi_{*}=q_{KT_{1}}(\mathfrak{t})\circ\Phi_{*}=q_{KT_{0}}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t}).

The last equality follows from (
17
), as just explained.
To see the central equality in (
18
) we use Lemma
6.16
, which implies that
q_{KT_{1}}(\mathfrak{t})=\widehat{q}_{GM_{1}}
, the exterior Guillou-Marin form of
s_{1}(F_{1})
.

It remains to see that the isometry of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-forms
\Phi_{*}
induces an isometry between the exterior Guillou-Marin forms
\widehat{q}_{GM_{0}}
and
\widehat{q}_{GM_{1}}
.
As
\Phi
satisfies
\Phi\circ s_{0}=s_{1}\circ\varphi
,
the isometry
\Phi_{*}
factors as a sequence of isomorphisms

\Phi_{*}\colon H_{1}(s_{0}(F_{0});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{(s_{0})_{*}^{-1}}H_{1}(F_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{*}}H_{1}(F_{1};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\xrightarrow{(s_{1})}H_{1}(s_{1}(F_{1});\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

By Proposition
6.15
, the outer two isomorphisms induce isometries between the
\widehat{q}_{GM}
forms on
H_{1}(s_{i}(F_{i});\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and the
q_{GM}
forms on
H_{1}(F_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. One of the defining properties of the central isomorphism
\varphi_{*}
is that it induces an isometry of
q_{GM}
forms.
Thus
\Phi_{*}
induces an isometry of exterior Guillou-Marin forms, concluding the proof of the claim.

We therefore have from (
18
) that
\widehat{q}_{GM_{0}}=q_{KT_{0}}(\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t})
.
From Corollary
6.17
it follows that
f^{*}\mathfrak{s}
, corresponding to the spin structure
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{t}
under the tubular neighbourhood map
\alpha_{0}
, extends to
X_{F_{0}}
. This completes the proof of property (
1
) and therefore of Proposition
6.2
.
∎

7.
The odd-dimensional
\ell
-monoid

We recall the definition and some facts surrounding the
monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
.
We will introduce quasi-formations, define
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
, study elementary quasi-formations and recall how a quadratic form
v
over
R
determines a subset
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v)\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R)
.
At the end of the section, we focus on
\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z})
and its relation to linking forms.

For the remainder of the section we fix an integer
q
and set
\varepsilon:=(-1)^{q}
.
Since we will ultimately work with
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
which are both group rings of groups with vanishing Whitehead group, we work in less generality than in
[
41
,
15
]
in order to avoid technicalities related to bases and Whitehead torsion.
As a consequence, throughout this section we assume that
R=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is a group ring of a group
\pi
with vanishing Whitehead group, referring to
[
15
, Section 3]
for the discussion in full generality.
The involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
on
R
is
\sum n_{g}g\mapsto\sum n_{g}g^{-1}
.

7.1.
Quasi-formations

We introduce quasi-formations and the
\ell
-monoid. The original reference for these monoids is
[
41
]
, but we also refer the reader to
[
15
]
as we will work with the equivalent formulation developed there.
It might help to refer back to Section
3.1
on hermitian and quadratic forms.

Definition 7.1
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic
quasi-formation
is a triple
((P,\psi);F,V)
, where
(P,\psi)
is a nonsingular
\varepsilon
-quadratic form,
F\subseteq P
is a lagrangian, and
V\subseteq P
is a half rank direct summand;
P,F
and
V
are assumed to be stably free.

This definition and terminology are due to Crowley and Sixt
[
15
, Section 3]
but are inspired by work of Kreck
[
41
]
.

Remark 7.2
.

In both
[
41
]
and
[
15
]
, the authors work with stably based modules throughout, as this is needed in the most general applications of the theory. However, we have made the assumption that
R=\mathbb{Z}[G]
where
G
has vanishing Whitehead torsion, meaning we can avoid using based modules, as we now briefly explain. We assume some familiarity with the definition of the first reduced
K
-group
\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)
and refer to e.g.
[
15
, Section 3]
for more details and definitions.
An
R
-module
P
is
stably based
if
P
is stably free and is equipped with an equivalence class of a basis for a free stabilisation
R^{n}\cong P\oplus R^{k}
. Two bases are defined to be equivalent if there is a common free stabilisation in which the change of basis matrix defines an element of
Z:=\{\pm g\mid g\in G\}\subseteq\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)
. Assuming that
\operatorname{Wh}(G)=0
implies that
Z=\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)
, so that all stable bases are equivalent, and so do need not be kept track of. For this reason, we do not work with stably based modules, merely stably free modules.

We recall the relation between quasi-formations and formations.
The latter play an important rôle in classical surgery;
see e.g.
[
47
, Section 9.2.1]
for further background.

Definition 7.3
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic
formation
is an
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,G)
for which the half rank direct summand
G\subseteq P
is a lagrangian.

In some references, e.g. in
[
59
]
, a “formation” refers to a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,G)
with
G\subseteq P
a sublagrangian, and what we refer to as a formation is then called a nonsingular formation.
We do not follow this convention; our formations are always nonsingular.

The next definitions introduce further terminology related to quasi-formations and formations.

Definition 7.4
.

•

Two
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formations
((P,\psi);F,V)
and
((P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}),F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
are
isomorphic
if there is an isometry
f\colon(P,\psi)\to(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
such that
f(F)=F^{\prime}
and
f(V)=V^{\prime}
.

•

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation is
trivial
if for some
F
it is isomorphic to a formation of the type
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,F^{*})
.
Here, and afterwards when it causes no confusion, we use the abbreviation
F,F^{*}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
instead of
F\oplus 0,0\oplus F^{*}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
.

•

The
boundary
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation
\delta(F,\kappa)
of a sesquilinear form
(F,\kappa)
is the quasi-formation
(H_{\varepsilon}(F),F,\Gamma_{\kappa})
, where
\Gamma_{\kappa}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
denotes the graph of
\kappa\colon F\to F^{*}
.
Note that the sesquilinear form
\kappa
need not be hermitian.

•

A quasi-formation is a
boundary
if it is isomorphic to the boundary
\delta(F,\theta)
of some sesquilinear form
(F,\kappa)
.

•

Two
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formations are
stably isomorphic
if they become isomorphic after some number of trivial
\varepsilon
-quadratic formations is added to each.
We use
\cong
to denote isomorphism and
\cong_{s}
to denote stable isomorphism.

Definition 7.5
.

•

If a sesquilinear form
(F,\kappa)
is even (meaning, by definition, that
\kappa
can be written as
\theta-\varepsilon\theta^{*}
for some
\theta
)
and
(-\varepsilon)
-hermitian, then the graph
\Gamma_{\kappa}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
is a lagrangian for
H_{\varepsilon}(F)
.
If
(F,[\theta])
is a
(-\varepsilon)
-quadratic form, then
(H_{\varepsilon}(F),F,\Gamma_{\theta-\varepsilon\theta^{*}})

is called the
boundary
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation
of
(F,[\theta])
.
Note that
\theta-\varepsilon\theta^{*}
is tautologically even and is also
(-\varepsilon)
-hermitian.

•

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation is a
boundary
if it is isomorphic to
the boundary
\delta(F,\theta)
of some
(-\varepsilon)
-quadratic form
(F,[\theta])
.

Every
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
is isomorphic to one of the form
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V^{\prime})
for some half rank direct summand
V^{\prime}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
.
To see this, consider that the Fundamental Lemma of
L
-theory (see e.g.
[
47
, Lemma 8.95]
)
implies that the embedding
(F,0)\hookrightarrow(P,\psi)
extends to an isometry
f\colon H_{\varepsilon}(F)\xrightarrow{\cong}(P,\psi)
, which therefore induces an isomorphism

(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,f^{-1}(V))\cong((P,\psi);F,V).

Definition 7.6
.

The
\ell
-monoid

\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is the unital abelian monoid (under direct sum) of stable isomorphism classes of
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic quasi-formations modulo the relation

(19)

((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus((P,\psi),G,V)\sim((P,\psi);F,V),

where
F,G\subseteq P
are both lagrangians.

Definition 7.7
.

The
L
-group

L_{2q+1}(R)
is the group (under direct sum) of stable isomorphism classes of
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic formations modulo the equivalence relation of cobordism, where
x
and
x^{\prime}
are said to be
cobordant
if there are boundary formations
b
and
b^{\prime}
such that
x\oplus b
and
x^{\prime}\oplus b^{\prime}
are stably isomorphic.

Remark 7.8
.

An alternative definition of the
L
-group
L_{2q+1}(R)
is as the abelian group (under direct sum) of stable isomorphism classes of
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic formations modulo the relation (
19
); see e.g.
[
60
, Remark 9.15]
. Thus
L_{2q+1}(R)\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R)
.

7.2.
Elementary quasi-formations

We recall the definition of elementary quasi-formations and the submonoid of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
that they define.
We continue with the notation
\varepsilon:=(-1)^{q}
and, from now on, write “quasi-formation” (resp. “formation”) instead of “
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation” (resp.“
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation”).
References for the material include
[
41
]
and
[
15
, Section 3]
.

We start by recording some terminology that we will use frequently in the sequel.

Definition 7.9
.

A
direct complement
to a submodule
V\subseteq P
is a submodule
U\subseteq P
such that the inclusions
U,V\subseteq P
extend to an isomorphism
U\oplus V\cong P
. We say that
U
and
V
are
complementary
.
If
(P,\psi)
is a quadratic form, then a
lagrangian complement
to
V\subseteq P
is a direct complement
U
that is moreover a lagrangian.

We introduce a geometrically significant notion of triviality for a quasi-formation.

Definition 7.10
.

A quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
is
elementary
if
F\subseteq P
is a direct complement to
V\subseteq P
.
A class in
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is
elementary
if it admits an elementary representative.

For an elementary quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
, since
F
is by definition a lagrangian, it follows that
F
is a lagrangian complement to
V
.
In particular,
V
admits a lagrangian complement.
The next proposition proves a partial converse by working on the level of the monoid, modulo formations.
This proposition was proved by Kreck
[
41
, Proposition 8 (iii)]
but we translate the argument into the language of quasi-formations.

Proposition 7.11
.

If
\Theta\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is represented by a quasi-formation
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V)
such that
V
admits a lagrangian complement, then there exists a formation
y
such that
\Theta+[y]
is elementary.

Proof.

Let
L\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
be a lagrangian complement for
V\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
.
Note that
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);L,V)
is elementary.
Consider the formation
y:=(H_{\varepsilon}(F);L,F)
. Working in
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
and using (
19
), we have

(20)

[(H_{\varepsilon}(F);L,V)]=[(H_{\varepsilon}(F);L,F)]+[(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V)]=[y]+[x]=[y]+\Theta,

and thus
\Theta+[y]

is elementary as required.
∎

Proposition
7.11
motivates considering the action of
L_{2q+1}(R)
on
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
by direct sum.

Construction 7.12
.

The action of
[y]\in L_{2q+1}(R)
on
[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is by
[y]\cdot[x]:=[x\oplus y]
.
To verify this is well defined, it suffices to prove that trivial formations and boundary formations act trivially.
For trivial formations, this is clear: in
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
stably isomorphic quasi-formations are equal.
To verify that boundary formations act trivially, use that in
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
a boundary formation
y=(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}})
decomposes as
y_{1}\oplus y_{2}:=(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,F^{*})\oplus(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F^{*},\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}})
, which is the sum of two trivial formations. To see that the latter is trivial one shows that
F^{*}
and
\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}}
are complementary.
Here is the proof that
(0\oplus F^{*})\oplus\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}}=F\oplus F^{*}
.
The fact that
(0\oplus F^{*})\cap\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}}=0
is verified by noting that if
(0,\varphi)=(x,(\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*})(x))
, then
x=0
and therefore
\varphi=({\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}})(0)=0
.
The inclusion
(0\oplus F^{*})\oplus\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}}\subseteq F\oplus F^{*}
is clear and the reverse inclusion follows by writing
(x,\varphi)\in F\oplus F^{*}
as
(0,\varphi-(\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*})(x))+(x,({\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}})(x))
.
Now apply
[
47
, Lemma 9.13 i)]
,
which states that a formation
((P,\psi);F,G)
is trivial if
F\oplus G=P
. This completes the proof that
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F^{*},\Gamma_{\theta-(-1)^{q}\theta^{*}})
is a trivial formation.
Thus in
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
we obtain
x\oplus y\sim x\oplus y_{1}\oplus y_{2}\sim x
, as required.

The following proposition describes an equivalent characterisation of
\Theta\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
being elementary when
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
cf.
[
29
, Proposition 2]
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Assume that
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
.
Given
\Theta\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
, the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

\Theta
is elementary, i.e.
\Theta
admits a representative
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V)
such that
F
is a lagrangian complement to
V
.

(2)

\Theta
is represented by a quasi-formation
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V)
such that
V
admits a lagrangian complement.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
is immediate.
Assuming that
(2)
holds, Proposition
7.11
implies that
\Theta
is elementary modulo
L_{2q+1}(R)
.
Since
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
, we deduce that
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
.
∎

If a formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
is elementary then in particular
V
admits a lagrangian complement, but it is unclear in general that for
every

((P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime});F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
in the class
[((P,\psi);F,V)]\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
, the module
V^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\prime}
admits a lagrangian complement.
We will show that this property holds if
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
and one is willing to add a formation to
((P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime});F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
.

For brevity in the next lemma and its proof, given a quasi-formation
x=((P,\psi);F,V)
we will say
x
admits a lagrangian complement
to meant that
V\subseteq P
admits a lagrangian complement.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
R
be a ring with
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
. Let
a
and
b
be quasi-formations over
R
that are either stably isomorphic, or related by the defining relation (
19
) of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
. Then there is a formation
f
such that
a\oplus f
admits a lagrangian complement if and only if there is there is a formation
f^{\prime}
such that
b\oplus f^{\prime}
admits a lagrangian complement.

Proof.

First assume that
a
and
b
are stably isomorphic, so that there are trivial formations
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that
a\oplus t_{1}\cong b\oplus t_{2}
.
If
a\oplus f
admits a lagrangian complement for some formation
f
, then
b\oplus(f\oplus t_{2})\cong a\oplus f\oplus t_{1}
admits a lagrangian complement because both
a\oplus f
and
t_{1}
do.
The proof of the converse is identical.

Next assume that
a=((P,\psi);F,V)
and
b=((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus((P,\psi);G,V)
are as in (
19
).
If
a\oplus f
admits a lagrangian complement for some formation
f
, then
((P,\psi);G,V)\oplus f
admits a lagrangian complement.
Since
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
, every formation is stably isomorphic to a boundary formation
[
60
, Corollary 9.12]
.
In particular, after adding a trivial formation
t
, the formation
((P,\psi);F,G)
admits a lagrangian complement, because every boundary formation does.
It follows that

b\oplus f\oplus t\cong\left(((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus t\right)\oplus(((P,\psi);G,V)\oplus f)

admits a lagrangian complement.

For the converse, assume that
b\oplus f
admits a lagrangian complement for some formation
f
.
More explicitly,
((P,\psi);G,V)\oplus(((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus f)
admits a lagrangian complement.
Since admitting a lagrangian complement is a statement that only involves the half rank direct summand, it follows that
((P,\psi);F,V)\oplus((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus f\ a\oplus((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus f
also admits a lagrangian complement.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
R
be a ring with
L_{2q+1}(R)=0
, let
\Theta\in\ell_{2q+1}(R)
and let
u
be any representative quasi-formation for
\Theta
.
If
\Theta
is elementary, then there is a formation
v
such that
in the quasi-formation
u\oplus v=:((P,\psi);F,V)
, the summand
V\subseteq P
admits a lagrangian complement.

Proof.

The definition of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is as the transitive closure of the set of quasi-formations under the relation of being either stably isomorphic or related as in (
19
). The proposition is now immediate from Lemma
7.14
.
∎

7.3.
Induced forms

Following
[
15
, Section 5]
, we describe some additional facts about the structure of the monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
.

Given a quasi-formation
x=((P,\psi);F,V)
(a representative for) the quadratic form
\psi\colon P\times P\to R
restricts to a quadratic form
\theta:=\psi|_{V\times V}
on
V
leading to a split isometric inclusion
(V,\theta)\hookrightarrow(P,\psi)
.
The same holds for
V^{\perp}
and we formalise this as follows.

Definition 7.16
.

The
induced forms
of a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
are the quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})
, where
\theta:=\psi|_{V\times V}
and
\theta^{\perp}:=\psi|_{V^{\perp}\times V^{\perp}}
.

Example 7.17
.

We describe the induced forms in some simple cases.

(1)

The induced forms of a formation
((P,\psi);F,G)
are both equal to the zero form
(G,0)
. This is immediate since
G
is a lagrangian of
(P,\psi)
so
\psi|_{G\times G}=0
and
G^{\perp}=G
.

(2)

Adding a formation to a quasi-formation has the effect of adding a zero form to its induced forms: if
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K),K,V)
is a quasi-formation and
y=(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,G)
is a formation, then the induced forms of the quasi-formation
x\oplus y=(H_{\varepsilon}(K\oplus F),K\oplus F,V\oplus G)
are the quadratic forms
(V\oplus G,\theta\oplus 0)
and
(V^{\perp}\oplus G,-\theta^{\perp}\oplus 0)
.

Example
7.17
(
2
) motivates the following definition from
[
15
]
.

Definition 7.18
.

Two quadratic forms
(P,\psi)
and
(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
are
0
-stably equivalent
if there are zero forms
(Q,0)
and
(Q^{\prime},0)
and an isometry

(P,\psi)\oplus(Q,0)\cong(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\oplus(Q^{\prime},0).

The
0
-stable equivalence class of a form
(P,\psi)
is denoted
[P,\psi]_{0}
.
Such an equivalence class is called a
0-stabilised quadratic form
.
We write
\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{2q}(R)
for the unital abelian monoid of
0
-stabilised
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic forms over
R
, where addition is given by direct sum.

Definition 7.19
.

The
induced
0
-stabilised forms
of a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
are the
0
-stable equivalence classes
[V,\theta]_{0}
and
[V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp}]_{0}
of its induced forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})
.

The notion of induced
0
-stabilised forms descends to
\ell
-monoids, as first observed in
[
15
]
.

Proposition 7.20
.

Given a ring
R
, taking induced
0
-stabilised forms gives rise to a well defined monoid homomorphism

\displaystyle b\colon\ell_{2q+1}(R)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{2q}(R)\times\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{2q}(R)

\displaystyle[((P,\psi);F,V)]

\displaystyle\mapsto([V,\theta]_{0},[V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp}]_{0}).

Proof.

Adding a trivial formation to a quasi-formation adds a zero form to the induced forms; recall Example
7.17
.
It therefore remains to study the effect of the relation in (
19
):

((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus((P,\psi);G,V)\sim((P,\psi);F,V).

Since
((P,\psi);F,G)
is a formation, its induced forms are zero forms and therefore the induced forms on both sides of this equivalence are
0
-stably equivalent.
Note that the induced forms of a quasi-formation are independent of the data of the first lagrangian.
∎

Given quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
such that
[v]_{0}\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(R^{k})=[v^{\prime}]_{0}\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(R^{k})
,
[
15
, Corollary 5.3]
ensures that
([v],[v^{\prime}])\in\operatorname{\operatorname{Im}}(b)
.
This leads to the following definition, which is due to Crowley-Sixt
[
15
, Subsection 5.2]
.

Definition 7.21
.

Given quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
such that
[v]_{0}\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(R^{k})=[v^{\prime}]_{0}\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(R^{k})
, we define

\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime}):=b^{-1}([v]_{0},[v^{\prime}]_{0})\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R).

This is the set of all (equivalence classes of) quasi-formations whose induced forms are
0
-stably equivalent to
v
and
v^{\prime}
respectively.
If
[v]_{0}=[v^{\prime}]_{0}
, then we write
\ell_{2q+1}(v)
instead of
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v)
.
Note that for any
0
-form
(V,0)
, we have
\ell_{2q+1}(V,0)=L_{2q+1}(R)

[
15
, Lemma 6.2]
.

Crowley and Sixt also show that for every
0
-stable equivalence class of a quadratic form
v=(V,\theta)
, there exists a unique elementary class
e(v)\in\ell_{2q+1}(v)
such that

b\circ e(v)=([v]_{0},[v]_{0})

[
15
, Corollary 5.3]
.
Explicitly,
e(v)
is the class of the boundary quasi-formation
\delta(V,\rho)
, where
\rho
is a sesquilinear form representing
\theta\in Q_{\varepsilon}(V)
.

Definition 7.22
.

Given a quadratic form
v
we say that
\ell_{2q+1}(v)
is
trivial
if it contains a single (necessarily elementary) element.

7.4.
Linking forms and boundary automorphism sets

This section focuses on quasi-formations over
\mathbb{Z}
.
The reason is that in the proof of our main theorem, we convert a question about being elementary in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
into a question about being elementary in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Given a quadratic form
v=(V,\theta)
over
\mathbb{Z}
, we therefore recall Crowley-Sixt’s description of
\ell_{5}(v)
in terms of linking forms
[
15
]
.
We use this to discuss various examples where
\ell_{5}(v)
is trivial.
In this section, the group
T
is a finite abelian group and the group
V
is always understood to be a finitely generated abelian group.

Definition 7.23
.

A (symmetric)
linking form
(over
\mathbb{Z}
) refers to a pair
(T,b)
where
T
is a finite abelian group and
b\colon T\times T\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is a symmetric bilinear form.

Definition 7.24

(Ranicki
[
59
, §3.4]
)
.

A
split quadratic linking form

(T,b,\nu)
(over
\mathbb{Z}
) is a symmetric linking form
(T,b)
together with a map
\nu\colon T\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
such that the following equalities in
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
hold for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in T
and all
r\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(1)

\nu(rx)=r^{2}\nu(x),

(2)

\nu(x_{1}+x_{2})-\nu(x_{1})-\nu(x_{2})=b(x_{1},x_{2})
.

Note that applying the second condition with
x_{1}=x_{2}=x
yields
\nu(2x)-2\nu(x)=b(x,x)
, and then applying the first condition we learn that
2\nu(x)=b(x,x)
.

Definition 7.25
.

A nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form
\lambda\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{Z}
, whose adjoint we denote
\widehat{\lambda}\colon V\to V^{*}
, determines a linking form
\partial\lambda\colon\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})\times\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
via the formula
([x],[y])\mapsto\frac{1}{s}y(z)
, where
sx=\widehat{\lambda}(z)
for some
z\in V
and some
s\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.
We call
\partial\lambda
the
boundary linking form
of the symmetric form
\lambda
.

We recall the corresponding definition for quadratic forms.
Note that if
\theta:=[\rho]\in Q_{\varepsilon}(V)
is a quadratic form, then for
z\in V
, the integer
\rho(z,z)
only depends on the the class of
\rho
in
Q_{\varepsilon}(V)
.
As a consequence, we write
\theta(z,z)
instead of
\rho(z,z)
.

Definition 7.26
.

Let
(V,\theta)\in Q_{\varepsilon}(V)
be a nondegenerate quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}
with symmetrisation
(V,\lambda)
.
The
boundary split quadratic linking form
of
(V,\theta)
is the split quadratic linking form
\partial(V,\theta):=(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}),\partial\lambda,\partial\theta)
where
(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}),\partial\lambda)
is the boundary linking form of the symmetric form
(V,\lambda)
and

\displaystyle\partial\theta\colon\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle([x])

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{s^{2}}\theta(z,z),

where
sx=\widehat{\lambda}(z)
for
z\in V
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.

One verifies that if a nondegenerate symmetric form
(V,\lambda)
is represented by a size
n
matrix
A
, then
\partial\lambda([x],[y])=xA^{-1}y
where
A^{-1}
denotes the inverse of
A
over
\mathbb{Q}
and
[x],[y]\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/A\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

We record the corresponding calculation in the quadratic case.

Remark 7.27
.

If a nondegenerate quadratic form
(V,\theta)
is represented by a size
n
matrix
Q
so that its symmetrisation is represented by
A=Q+Q^{T}
, then

\partial\theta([x])=x^{T}(A^{-1})^{T}QA^{-1}x=x^{T}A^{-1}QA^{-1}x

for every
[x]\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/A\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, where once again we invert
A
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Example 7.28
.

We use Remark
7.27
to calculate the boundary split quadratic linking form of a rank one quadratic form.
When
V=\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, we have
\lambda=2\theta
and the boundary forms are therefore defined on
\mathbb{Z}/2\theta
.
Remark
7.27
implies that
\partial\lambda([x],[y])=\frac{1}{2\theta}xy\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
and

\partial\theta([x])=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{\theta}{4\theta^{2}}$}x^{2}=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\theta}$}x^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

Still building towards the description of
\ell_{5}(v)
in terms of linking forms, we now describe isometries of linking forms.

Definition 7.29
.

An
isometry
of split quadratic linking forms
(T_{0},b_{0},\nu_{0})
and
(T_{1},b_{1},\nu_{1})
is an isomorphism
f\colon T_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}T_{1}
such that
\nu_{1}(f(x))=\nu_{0}(x)
for every
x\in T_{0}
.

Observe that an isometry automatically satisfies
b_{1}(f(x),f(y))=b_{0}(x,y)
because

b_{1}(f(x),f(y))=\nu_{1}(f(x)+f(y))-\nu_{1}(f(x))-\nu_{1}(f(y))=\nu_{0}(x+y)-\nu_{0}(x)-\nu_{0}(y)=b_{0}(x,y).

Given an isometry
h\colon(V_{0},\theta_{0})\to(V_{1},\theta_{1})
of quadratic forms, one verifies that
(h^{*})^{-1}\colon V_{0}^{*}\to V_{1}^{*}
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}_{0})\to\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}_{1})
on the cokernels.
This isomorphism, which we denote by
\partial h:=(h^{*})^{-1}
, is an isometry of the boundary split quadratic linking forms.

Definition 7.30
.

The
boundary
of an isometry
h\colon(V_{0},\theta_{0})\to(V_{1},\theta_{1})
is the isometry

\partial h:=(h^{*})^{-1}\colon\partial(V_{0},\theta_{0})\to\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}).

This determines a homomorphism

\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta)).

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)
of isometries of
(V,\theta)
acts on the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
on the left both via
h\cdot f:=\partial h\circ f
and
g\cdot f:=f\circ\partial g^{-1}
where
f\in\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
and
g,h\in\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)
.
Combining these actions, which clearly commute, leads to a left action of
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\times\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)
on
\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
.

Definition 7.31
.

The
boundary automorphism set
of a nondegenerate quadratic form
(V,\theta)
is the orbit set

\operatorname{bAut}(V,\theta)=\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))/\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\times\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta).

Observe that an isometry
f\in\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
is trivial in the boundary automorphism set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,\theta)
if and only if
f
extends to an isometry of
(V,\theta)
.

Example 7.32
.

Using Example
7.28
, one verifies that for a rank one form
(\mathbb{Z},\theta)
, the automorphisms of the module
\mathbb{Z}/2\theta
are the units of the ring
\mathbb{Z}/2\theta
, while the automorphisms of
\partial(\mathbb{Z},\theta)
consist of the units
x
of
\mathbb{Z}/2\theta
such that
x^{2}=1
mod
4\theta
. The corresponding boundary automorphism set
\operatorname{bAut}(\mathbb{Z},\theta)

consists of the same
x\in\mathbb{Z}/2\theta
, but with
x
and
-x
identified.

•

For
\theta=4
, the units of
\mathbb{Z}/8
are
\pm 1
and
\pm 3
,
but only
1
and
-1
satisfy
x^{2}=1
mod
16
.
It follows that
\operatorname{bAut}(\mathbb{Z},4)
is trivial.

•

For
\theta=6
, the units of
\mathbb{Z}/12
are
\pm 1,\pm 5
all of which satisfy
x^{2}=1
mod
4\theta=24
.
It follows that
\operatorname{bAut}(\mathbb{Z},6)=\{1,5\}
.

Theorem 7.33

(Crowley-Sixt
[
15
, Section 6.3]
)
.

Given a nondegenerate quadratic form
v=(V,\theta)
over
\mathbb{Z}
, there is a bijection between
\ell_{5}(v)
and
\operatorname{bAut}(v)
.

Example 7.34
.

We illustrate Theorem
7.33
with some examples that will be relevant to the study of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of nonorientable genus
h=1,2
.

•

In Example
7.32
we saw
\operatorname{bAut}(\mathbb{Z},4)
is trivial, and thus so is
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z},4)
, by Theorem
7.33
.

•

We argue that
\operatorname{bAut}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
is trivial, thus proving
\ell_{5}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
is trivial.
First observe that the isometries of the quadratic form
4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
are

\operatorname{Aut}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))=\operatorname{Aut}(H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\\
0&\varepsilon_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mid\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}=1\right\}.

We verify that all the isometries of
\partial(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
are induced by
\operatorname{Aut}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
.
This was already observed by Kreck
[
40
, p. 70]
.
First, note that the symmetric linking form is
b\colon(\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4})\times(\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, represented by
B:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\frac{1}{4}\\
\frac{1}{4}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and the quadratic enhancement is
\nu\colon\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4}\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},ke_{1}+\ell e_{2}\mapsto k\ell/4
.
An automorphism
\alpha
of this is represented by a matrix
A:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)
over
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
such that
A^{T}BA=B
over
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
and such that
\nu\circ\alpha=\nu\colon\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4}\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
.
The former condition implies that
ad+bc=1\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, while the conditions that
\nu(e_{1})=\nu(ae_{1}+ce_{2})
and
\nu(e_{2})=\nu(be_{1}+de_{2})
give
ac\equiv 0\equiv bd
. A case analysis yields exactly the same four matrices for
A
as in the description of
\operatorname{Aut}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
. It follows that
\operatorname{Aut}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})))
is surjective, as claimed.

7.5.
Nikulin’s criterion for trivial bAut

Determining whether
\ell_{5}(v)
is trivial is geometrically significant, as we will recall in the next section.
Theorem
7.33
converts this problem into a question about linking forms.
In order to state a criterion due to Nikulin
[
56
]
that describes conditions for
\operatorname{bAut}(v)
to be trivial, we introduce some notation.
Namely, given an abelian group
G
and a prime
p
, we write
n_{p}(G)
for the minimal number of generators for the
p
-primary part of
G
, or equivalently
n_{p}(G):=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(G\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

Theorem 7.35

(Nikulin
[
56
, Theorem 1.14.2]
)
.

Let
v=(V,\theta)
be an indefinite, nondegenerate quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}
with symmetrisation
(V,\lambda)=(V,\theta+\theta^{*})
.
Suppose that

(1)

the inequality
\operatorname{rk}(V)\geq n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))+2
holds for all odd primes
p
, and

(2)

if
\operatorname{rk}(V)=n_{2}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))
, then the split quadratic linking form
\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)])
is a summand of
\partial(V,\theta)
.

Then
\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
is surjective, and so
\operatorname{bAut}(v)
consists of one element.

The statement in Nikulin’s paper is not written this way. The fact that Nikulin’s work could be translated into this language and used in this context was observed in Crowley-Sixt
[
15
, Proposition 6.18 i)]
, where they reported the outcome of that translation. We provide the details of the translation momentarily, but first record the key consequence for our purposes.

Corollary 7.36
.

Under the conditions in the hypotheses of Theorem
7.35
,
\ell_{5}(v)
is trivial.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
7.35
with Theorem
7.33
.
∎

We now translate the result stated in
[
56
, Theorem 1.14.2]
to that stated in Theorem
7.35
. First, Nikulin works with the nonsplit version of quadratic linking forms, whose definition we recall next, in which the quadratic enhancements
\mu
are valued in
\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 7.37

(Ranicki
[
59
, §3.4]
)
.

A
nonsplit quadratic linking form

(T,b,\mu)
(over
\mathbb{Z}
) is a symmetric linking form
(T,b)
together with a map
\mu\colon T\to\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}
such that the following equalities in
\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}
hold for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in T
and all
r\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(1)

\mu(rx)=r^{2}\mu(x),

(2)

\mu(x_{1}+x_{2})-\mu(x_{1})-\mu(x_{2})=2b(x_{1},x_{2})
.

In the more general context where Ranicki defined these notions, there is in general a difference between split and nonsplit quadratic linking forms. For us, because
1/2\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, there is effectively no difference.

Proposition 7.38

(
[
59
, Proposition 3.4.2]
)
.

A split quadratic linking form
(T,b,\nu)
determines a nonsplit quadratic linking form
(T,b,\mu)
by sending
\nu\colon T\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
to
\mu:=2\nu\colon T\to\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}
.
This gives rise to an equivalence of categories

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{split quadratic linking forms over }\mathbb{Z}\\
\text{and morphisms thereof}\end{array}\right\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{nonsplit quadratic linking forms over }\mathbb{Z}\\
\text{and morphisms thereof}\end{array}\right\}

that is the identity on the morphisms.

Definition 7.39
.

The
boundary
of a nondegenerate, even symmetric bilinear form
\lambda\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{Z}
is the nonsplit quadratic linking form
\partial(V,\lambda):=(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}),\partial\lambda,\mu)
, where

(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}),\partial\lambda)
is the boundary linking form of the symmetric form
(V,\lambda)
, and

\mu\colon\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})\to\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}

is defined by
\mu([y])=\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{s^{2}}$}\lambda(z,z)
, where
s\in\mathbb{Z},z\in V
and
y\in V^{*}
satisfy
sy=\widehat{\lambda}(z)
.

Remark 7.40
.

We caution the reader that
\partial(V,\lambda)
does not refer to a symmetric linking form but to a nonsplit
quadratic
linking form.
As illustrated in Example
7.32
, quadratic linking forms typically have fewer isometries than their symmetrisations.

Construction 7.41
.

Write
\mathbf{Quad},\mathbf{EvenSym},\mathbf{SplitQuadLink}
, and
\mathbf{NonSplitQuadLink}
for the categories of nondegenerate quadratic forms over
\mathbb{Z}
, even symmetric forms over
\mathbb{Z}
, split quadratic linking forms over
\mathbb{Z}
and nonsplit quadratic linking forms over
\mathbb{Z}
, together with morphisms thereof.
Consider the following functors:

(21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Quad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SplitQuadLink}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{EvenSym}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \mathbf{NonSplitQuadLink}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Here, the left vertical functor maps a quadratic form to its symmetrisation and is the identity on morphisms. It is an equivalence of categories because every even symmetric form over
\mathbb{Z}
admits a unique quadratic refinement.
The right hand side vertical functor is the equivalence described in Proposition
7.38
, and the horizontal functors take the boundary.

Proposition 7.42
.

The square (
21
) commutes.

Proof.

To see that the diagram of categories and functors commutes on the level of objects, note that if
\theta\in Q_{+}(V)
is a quadratic form with symmetrisation
\lambda
, then

\partial\theta([y])=\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{s^{2}}$}\theta(z,z)=\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{2s^{2}}$}\lambda(z,z)=\mbox{\large$\frac{1}{2}$}\mu([y]).

The commutativity on the level of morphisms holds because a homomorphism
f\colon V\to V
is sent via both routes to
\partial f\colon\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})\to\mathbb{Q}/2\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Corollary 7.43
.

The diagram in (
21
) induces the following commutative diagram of automorphism groups:

(22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\lambda)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

The vertical equalities refer to equalities as subsets of the automorphisms
\operatorname{Aut}(V)
of the group
V
.

Proof.

Restrict (
21
) to the isometries.
∎

Nikulin’s theorem is stated in his paper in terms of the objects on the bottom row of (
22
) and Crowley-Sixt’s Theorem
7.33
is stated in terms the objects on the top row. For our statement in Theorem
7.35
, we translated Nikulin’s statement into the language of quadratic forms and split quadratic linking forms, in order to be able to apply it in the context of Theorem
7.33
.

Summarising, Nikulin’s map
\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\lambda))
is identified with the map
\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
from Definition
7.30
.
Thus for
(V,\lambda)=(V,\theta+\theta^{*})
, Nikulin’s requirement that the nonsplit quadratic form
\partial(V,\lambda)
split off a
\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right))
summand (Nikulin writes
U^{(2)}(2)
for the symmetric form
\lambda
and
u_{+}^{(2)}(2)
for its nonsplit quadratic boundary
\partial(V,\lambda)

[
56
, Proposition 1.8.1]
) is equivalent to requiring that the split quadratic linking form
\partial(V,\theta)
contains a
\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)])
summand.

We conclude this section by illustrating Corollary
7.36
with some examples that will be useful when studying
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces.
Given an integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider the quadratic form

X_{n}=\operatorname{sgn}(n)\left[\begin{pmatrix}2&2&2&\ldots&2\\
0&1&1&\cdots&1\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&1\\
0&\ldots&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})

from Proposition
5.11
.
For nonnegative integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, set
h:=a+b
and
\sigma=a-b
.
Observe that
h-|\sigma|
is nonnegative and even, and note that if
\sigma=0
then
h
is even.
Consider the quadratic form

\theta_{a,b}:=\begin{cases}2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus(\frac{h}{2}-1)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma=0,\\
X_{\sigma}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma\neq 0.\end{cases}

This is a rank
h
, signature
\sigma
, nondegenerate quadratic form. If
h\neq|\sigma|
then
h-|\sigma|>0
so
\theta_{a,b}
is an indefinite form on
V:=\mathbb{Z}^{h}
.

Proposition 7.44
.

Given nonnegative integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, set
h:=a+b
and
\sigma=a-b
.
If
h\leq 3
or if
|\sigma|\neq h
, then the set
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{a,b})
is trivial.

Proof.

For
h=1
we have
2\theta_{a,b}=4
and we already proved in Example
7.34
that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z},4)
is trivial.
For
h=2
and
\sigma=0
we have
2\theta_{a,b}=4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
and again
we already proved in Example
7.34
that
\ell_{5}(4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
is trivial.
The cases where
|\sigma|=h=2,3
involve detailed explicit computations, and are treated in the appendix.

We now assume that
h\geq 3
and
|\sigma|\neq h
. Write
\theta:=\theta_{a,b}
.
If
h\geq 3
then
\theta
has at least one hyperbolic summand, and therefore
2\theta
has a
2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
-summand.
We deduce that
\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
splits off a
\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)])
summand.

Thanks to Corollary
7.36
, the proof therefore reduces to verifying that
\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})
is
2
-primary and satisfies
n_{2}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))=\operatorname{rk}(V)=h
. Thus in our case
n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))=0
for every odd prime, and so
h\geq 3
implies that
h=\operatorname{rk}(V)\geq n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))+2=2
.
We have that

2\theta\cong\begin{cases}4H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus 2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{h}{2}-1}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma=0,\\
2X_{\sigma}\oplus 2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma\neq 0.\end{cases}

When
\sigma=0
, we have that
\operatorname{coker}(4H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus 2H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{h}{2}-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\oplus(h-2)}
.
This is 2-primary and generated by no fewer than
h
elements. Thus
n_{2}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))=h
in this case.
When
\sigma\neq 0
, we have that
\operatorname{coker}(2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)})\cong(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{\oplus h-|\sigma|}
, which is 2-primary and generated by no fewer than
h-|\sigma|
elements.
To complete the
\sigma\neq 0
case, it therefore suffices to show that
\operatorname{coker}(2X_{\sigma}+2X_{\sigma}^{T})
is
2
-primary and generated by
|\sigma|
and no fewer elements.

We consider

2X_{\sigma}+2X_{\sigma}^{T}=\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\begin{pmatrix}8&4&4&\ldots&4\\
4&4&2&\cdots&2\\
4&2&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&2\\
4&2&\ldots&2&4\end{pmatrix}.

By performing row and column operations, we compute that

\operatorname{coker}(2X_{\sigma}+2X_{\sigma}^{T})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{|\sigma|-1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{8}&\quad\text{ if }|\sigma|\text{ is odd,}\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{|\sigma|-2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{4}&\quad\text{ if }|\sigma|\text{ is even.}\end{cases}

Thus
\operatorname{coker}(2X_{\sigma}+2X_{\sigma}^{T})
is indeed
2
-primary and is generated by no fewer than
|\sigma|
elements.
This concludes the verification that the assumptions of Corollary
7.36
hold, from which it follows that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
is trivial.
∎

Remark 7.45
.

We currently know of no pair
(a,b)
where
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{a,b})
is nontrivial; here recall that
h:=a+b
and
\sigma=a-b
.
In particular, it could be that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{a,b})
is trivial for all
a,b
. It would be very interesting to know whether or not this holds.
Proposition
7.44
implies that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{a,b})
could only potentially be nontrivial when
h=|\sigma|\geq 4
.
In the appendix, we verify by direct calculations of isometries that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{a,b})
is trivial in the cases corresponding to
h=|\sigma|\in\{2,3\}
. The computations grow rapidly in complexity with
h
. For
h=|\sigma|=2
, the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},\theta_{a,b}))
has
8
elements, whereas for
h=|\sigma|=3
, it has
48
elements.
This computational complexity explains why, for extremal values of
\sigma
, we only proved Proposition
7.44
for
h\leq 3
.

8.
Modified surgery theory

In this section we recall some elements of Kreck’s modified surgery theory
[
41
]
.
In Subsection
8.1
, we review the concept of a
normal
k
-smoothing
of a manifold
M
.
Restricting to dimension
4
, Subsection
8.2
recalls the definition of the modified surgery obstruction that takes values in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])
.
In Subsection
8.3
we narrow down where in the monoid
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])
the modified surgery obstruction lives.

8.1.
Normal smoothings

We recall the notion of a normal smoothing and of the normal
k
-type from
[
41
]
.
We then consider the concrete case where the manifolds are
4
-dimensional and orientable with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Let
B
be a topological space with the homotopy type of a CW complex and let
\xi\colon B\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
be a fibration.
An
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
consists of a pair

(M,\overline{\nu})
, where
M
is an oriented
n
-manifold
and
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is a lift of the oriented stable (topological) normal bundle
\nu\colon M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
of
M
, meaning that
\nu=\xi\circ\overline{\nu}
.

A
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism
for a closed
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is an
(n{+}1)
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(W,\overline{\nu})
for which
\partial(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
A
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
between two
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifolds
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
consists of:

(i)

a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
;

(ii)

for
i=0,1
, homotopies
\overline{\nu}_{i}\sim\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{i}
, to some maps
\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
, such that

{\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{0}}|_{\partial M_{0}}={\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{1}}|_{\partial M_{1}}\circ f\colon\partial M_{0}\to B;

(iii)

an
(n{+}1)
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism of
(M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})

where
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}|_{M_{0}}=\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{0}
and
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}|_{M_{1}}=-\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{1}
.

We note that the homotopies in condition (
ii
) are there to ensure one can produce a well-defined
B
-structure
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}
on
M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1}
in the scenario that the
B
-structures
\overline{\nu}_{1}\circ f
and
\overline{\nu}_{0}
are isomorphic, but not necessarily equal (equality is required for glueing).

We briefly discuss orientation reversal, to give meaning to the notation
-\overline{\nu}^{\dagger}_{1}
above. Given a
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(M,\overline{\nu})
, the map
\overline{\nu}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon M\times[0,1]\to B
defines a
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(M\times[0,1],\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu})
. We identify
(M,\overline{\nu})
with the restriction to
M\times\{0\}
, and define
-\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
as the restriction to the end
M\times\{1\}
, which then produces a
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(-M,-\overline{\nu})
, i.e.
-\overline{\nu}
lifts the oppositely oriented stable normal bundle
\nu_{-M}\colon-M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
.

Recall that a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
is
m
-connected
if
f_{*}\colon\pi_{i}(X)\to\pi_{i}(Y)
is an isomorphism for
i<m
and is surjective for
i=m
. A map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
is
m
-coconnected
if
f_{*}\colon\pi_{k}(X)\to\pi_{k}(Y)
is an isomorphism for
i>m
and is injective for
i=m
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
B
be a space with the homotopy type of a CW complex with finite
(k+2)
-skeleton, let
\xi\colon B\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
be a fibration, and let
(M,\overline{\nu})
be a
(B,\xi)
-manifold.

(1)

If
\overline{\nu}
is
(k{+}1)
-connected, then
(M,\overline{\nu})
is called a
normal
k
-smoothing
into
(B,\xi)
.

(2)

The pair
(B,\xi)
is a
normal
k
-type
for
M
if
\xi
is
(k+1)
-coconnected and there exists a normal
k
-smoothing
(M,\overline{\nu})
into
(B,\xi)
.

The existence of the Moore-Postnikov factorisation
[
4
]
of the stable normal bundle
\nu\colon M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
ensures that for all
k\geq 0
, there exists a normal
k
-type for
M
. Moreover, this theory implies any two normal
k
-types for
M
are fibre homotopy equivalent.

We now assume that
M
is a
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and equipped with a spin structure. This implies the existence of maps
c\colon M\to B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{s}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOPSpin}
, where
c
classifies the universal cover of
M
, and
\mathfrak{s}
is a lift of the stable normal bundle along the standard principal fibration
\gamma\colon\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
. These maps are unique up to homotopy.
They will be used in the statement of the next lemma.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
M
be a spin
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, equipped with a spin structure. Write

(B,\xi):=(\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2},\gamma\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}).

Then the map

\overline{\nu}:=\mathfrak{s}\times c\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}

determines a normal
1
-smoothing into
(B,\xi)
, and
(B,\xi)
is a normal
1
-type for
M
.

The lemma is well-known to experts, e.g.
[
40
,
41
]
; we provide the details for the convenience of the reader.

Proof.

The space
B
has finite
3
-skeleton because both
B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\simeq\mathbb{R}P^{\infty}
and
\operatorname{\operatorname{BTOPSpin}}
do (for the latter, use
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BTOPSpin})=0
for
i=1,2,3
together with CW approximation).
The map
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
is 2-coconnected because
\operatorname{TOPSpin}\to\operatorname{STOP}
is the universal covering map.
It follows that
\xi
is 2-coconnected, because
\pi_{i}(B\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
for
i\geq 2
.
Next, since
M
is spin, the stable normal bundle

\nu\colon M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}
lifts to
\mathfrak{s}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOPSpin}

and therefore combining with
c
we obtain a smoothing
M\to\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This is 2-connected because
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BTOPSpin})=0
for
i=1,2
.
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

If
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces with the same nonorientable genus and normal Euler number, then there exist normal
1
-smoothings
(X_{F_{i}},\overline{\nu}_{i})
for
i=0,1
, that are bordant over their normal
1
-type, relative to a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
that restricts to a homeomorphism
S(\nu F_{0})\to S(\nu F_{1})
that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary.

Proof.

Write
(B,\xi)
for the normal
1
-type of the
X_{F_{i}}
that was described in Lemma
8.2
.
By Proposition
6.2
, there is a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
such that
M:=X_{F_{0}}\cup_{f}-X_{F_{1}}
is spin and has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Fix a spin structure on
M
. Via Lemma
8.2
, we obtain a normal
1
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
. Denote by
\overline{\nu_{0}}\colon X_{F_{0}}\to B
and
-\overline{\nu_{1}}\colon-X_{F_{1}}\to B
the restriction of
\overline{\nu}
to the respective exteriors.
By Lemma
8.2
,
(X_{F_{i}},\overline{\nu}_{i})
is a normal
1
-smoothing for
i=0,1
.
The proposition now reduces to proving that
[(M,\overline{\nu})]
vanishes in the bordism group
\Omega_{4}(B,\xi)\cong\Omega_{4}^{{\operatorname{TOPSpin}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
A spectral sequence calculation shows that
\Omega_{4}^{\operatorname{TOPSpin}}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\Omega_{4}^{\operatorname{TOPSpin}}\cong\mathbb{Z}
, detected by the signature divided by 8. See e.g.
[
57
, Proof of Proposition 5.1]
for the details.

For
i=0,1
, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence corresponding to the decomposition
D^{4}=X_{F_{i}}\cup-\overline{\nu}F_{i}
implies that the inclusion-induced map
H_{2}(\partial X_{F_{i}};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(X_{F_{i}};\mathbb{Z})
is surjective.
As a result, the ordinary intersection pairing of
X_{F_{i}}
vanishes identically, and so in particular
\sigma(X_{F_{i}})=0
.
Then Novikov additivity of the signature implies that the signature of
M
also vanishes.
It follows that
(M,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-null-bordant, as desired.
∎

8.2.
The surgery obstruction

We recall the definition of the modified surgery obstruction in odd-dimensions.
The main reference is
[
41
]
.
For simplicity,
we restrict our discussion to
4
-manifolds whose fundamental group has vanishing Whitehead group.
Since
\operatorname{Wh}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0

[
68
]
, this will suffice for our purposes.

We say that
(W,\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
is a
modified surgery problem
if
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are
4
-manifolds with normal
1
-type
(B,\xi)
, the
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
are normal
1
-smoothings, and
(W,\overline{\nu})
is a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism between
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
.

Let
(W,\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be a modified surgery problem.
By surgery below the middle dimension
[
41
, Proposition 4]
, we may assume that
\overline{\nu}\colon W\to B

is
2
-connected i.e. that
(W,\overline{\nu})
is a normal
1
-smoothing.
It can then be proved that
K\pi_{2}(W):=\ker(\overline{\nu}_{*})
is finitely generated; see e.g.
[
12
, Lemma 3.4]
.
Choose a finite set
\omega
of generators for
K\pi_{2}(W)
. Choose disjoint embeddings
\varphi_{i}\colon S^{2}\times D^{3}\hookrightarrow W
with basing paths representing these generators, that are compatible with the
(B,\xi)
-structure.
Set

U:=\bigsqcup_{i}\varphi_{i}(S^{2}\times D^{3}).

Since

\partial U\cong\bigsqcup_{i}S^{2}\times S^{2}
,
its quadratic intersection form, denoted
(H_{2}(\partial U;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]),\psi)
, is hyperbolic.
For concision, we refer to the pair
(\omega,\varphi):=(\omega,\sqcup_{i}\varphi_{i})
as a
set of embedded generators for
K\pi_{2}(W)
, we set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
, and consider the
\Lambda
-modules

(23)

F:=H_{3}(U,\partial U;\Lambda)\quad\text{ and }\quad V:=H_{3}(W\setminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U;\Lambda).

Via the boundary maps in the long exact sequences of the appropriate pairs,
F
and
V
can be identified with submodules of
P:=H_{2}(\partial U;\Lambda)
. Under these identifications,
F\subseteq P
is a lagrangian and
V\subseteq P
is a stably free half-rank direct summand
[
41
, p. 734]
.
The
kernel quasi-formation
associated with these data is defined as

\Sigma(W,\overline{\nu},\omega,\varphi):=((P,\psi);F,V).

The class of this quasi-formation in
\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
only depends on the
(B,\xi)
-bordism class rel. boundary of
(W,\overline{\nu})

[
41
, Theorem 4]
.
This leads to the following definition
[
41
, p. 734]
; see also
[
15
, p. 494]
.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
(W,\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be a modified surgery problem.
The
modified surgery obstruction
of
(W,\overline{\nu})
is

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}):=[(\Sigma(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}))]\in\ell_{5}(\Lambda),

where
(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is a normal
1
-smoothing over
(B,\xi)
, which is
(B,\xi)
-bordant
rel. boundary to
(W,\overline{\nu})

and
(\omega^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a set of embedded generators for
K\pi_{2}(W^{\prime})
.

Remark 8.5
.

The class
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
only depends on the
(B,\xi)
-bordism class rel. boundary of the
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
: it is independent of the choice of the
1
-smoothing
(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
and of the subsequent choice of a set of embedded generators for
K\pi_{2}(W^{\prime})

[
41
, Theorem 4]
.

Theorem 8.6

(Kreck
[
41
, Theorem 4]
)
.

Let
(W,\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be a modified surgery problem.
The modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
is elementary if and only if
(W,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism.

8.3.
Wall forms

Let
(W^{5},\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be a modified surgery problem as in the previous section.
We wish to narrow down where in the monoid
\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
the modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
lives. For this we will show how to choose nondegenerate quadratic forms
v
and
v^{\prime}
, on free
\Lambda
-modules, so that the obstruction lies in the subset
\ell_{5}(v,v^{\prime})\subseteq\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
described in Definition
7.21
.

Write
(M^{4}_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
for a normal
1
-smoothing into the normal
1
-type
(B,\xi)
of
M_{0}
and recall that

K\pi_{2}(M_{0}):=\ker((\overline{\nu}_{0})_{*}\colon\pi_{2}(M_{0})\to\pi_{2}(B)).

Intersections and self-intersections in
M_{0}
define a quadratic form
(K\pi_{2}(M_{0}),\psi_{M_{0}})
called the
Wall form
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

[
41
, Section 5]
.
Write
\lambda_{M_{0}}
for the symmetrisation of
\psi_{M_{0}}
(this agrees with the restriction of the equivariant intersection form on
\pi_{2}(M_{0})=H_{2}(M_{0};\Lambda)
) and recall that
the
radical

\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{M_{0}})\subseteq K\pi_{2}(M_{0})
of this pairing consists by definition of those
x\in K\pi_{2}(M_{0})
such that
\lambda_{M_{0}}(x,-)\equiv 0
.
We shorten
K\pi_{2}(M_{0})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{M_{0}})
to
K\pi_{2}(M_{0})/\operatorname{rad}
from now on.
The Hermitian form
\lambda_{M_{0}}
descends to a nondegenerate Hermitian form
\lambda_{M_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
on this quotient and therefore determines a quadratic form
\psi_{M_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
on this quotient.
The form
(K\pi_{2}(M_{0})/\operatorname{rad},\psi_{M_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
is called the
nondegenerate Wall form
.

We would like to use the nondegenerate Wall forms for
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
as the forms
v
and
v^{\prime}
mentioned at the start of this subsection.
However, the modules
K\pi_{2}(M_{i})
need not be free, or even stably free.
This is solved using the following idea from
[
41
,
15
]
.

Definition 8.7
.

Let
(M_{0}^{4},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be a normal
1
-smoothing into the normal
1
-type
(B,\xi)
of
M_{0}
.
Given a free
\Lambda
-module
S
and a surjection
\varpi\colon S\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{2}(M_{0})/\operatorname{rad}
, the pull-back of the nondegenerate Wall form
(K\pi_{2}(M_{0})/\operatorname{rad},\psi_{M_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
by
\varpi
is called a
free Wall form
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and is denoted by

(S,\vartheta):=(S,\varpi^{*}\psi_{M_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}).

When the free module and the quadratic form are not directly relevant to an argument, we will sometimes simply write
v(\overline{\nu}_{0})
for a free Wall form of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.

A consequence of
[
41
, Proposition 8]
is that all free Wall forms of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

are
0
-stably equivalent (Definition
7.18
).
The following proposition is also a consequence of
[
41
, Proposition 8 ii)]
.
Recall the map of monoids
b\colon\ell_{5}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\Lambda)
from Proposition
7.20
, which takes a quasi-formation to its pair of induced forms, considered up to 0-stable equivalence.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
(W,\overline{\nu},M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0},M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be a modified surgery problem with modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\Lambda)
.
For any choice of free Wall forms
v(\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
v(\overline{\nu}_{1})
for
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
respectively,
we have a
0
-stable equivalence:

b(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))=(v(\overline{\nu}_{0})_{0},v(\overline{\nu}_{1})_{0})\in\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\Lambda)

and hence

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(v(\overline{\nu}_{0}),v(\overline{\nu}_{1})).

Proof.

Since the induced form of a formation is the zero form (recall Example
7.17
), it follows from
[
41
, Proposition 8 ii)]
that the induced forms of
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
are zero stably equivalent to the free Wall forms
v(\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
v(\overline{\nu}_{1})
.
The definition of the map
b
from Proposition
7.20
associates to a quasi-formation its induced forms and so the equality
b(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))=(v(\overline{\nu}_{0})_{0},v(\overline{\nu}_{1})_{0})
immediately follows.
∎

9.
Forms and quasi-formations over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]

As before we write
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\langle T\mid T^{2}=1\rangle
.
In this section we specialise to the ring
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]=\mathbb{Z}[T]/\langle T^{2}-1\rangle
and develop some results concerning quasi-formations over this ring.
The eventual goal is to describe a tractable criterion guaranteeing that a class
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary.

In Subsection
9.1
we use
\pm 1
eigenspaces to associate two forms over
\mathbb{Z}
to a form over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, called the
plus and minus forms.

In Subsection
9.2
, we use plus and minus forms to associate two quasi-formations over
\mathbb{Z}
to a quasi-formation over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
.
In Subsection
9.3
, we use this construction to prove the main technical ingredient.
In Subsection
9.4
, we deduce a criterion ensuring that a class
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary; see Proposition
9.14
.

In this section we assume that
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules are finitely generated and free; cf. Lemma
3.9
.

9.1.
Forms over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
and eigenspaces

A
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module can also be thought of as an abelian group.
Given a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module
P
, recall that we write
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\subseteq P
for the abelian subgroup given by the
(\pm 1)
-eigenspaces of
T
.

In this subsection we recall how a hermitian (resp. quadratic) form over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
induces a hermitian (resp. quadratic) form over
\mathbb{Z}
on
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
.

Remark 9.1
.

Recall that
P
is assumed to be free. In this case, we argue that
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
can equivalently be described as
(1\pm T)P
.
Certainly, we always have
(1\pm T)P\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
.
For the reverse inclusion, since
P
is free we can work coordinate by coordinate, thereby reducing to the case
P=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
.
Now if
a+bT\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
satisfies
T(a+bT)=\pm(a+bT)
, then
T(a+bT)=b+Ta
, so
a=\pm b
.

Remark 9.2
.

In the notation of Subsection
3.1
, which concerned
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms over a ring
R
, we have
R=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
and
\varepsilon=1
. Note that
\overline{a+bT}=a+bT
in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
.
It follows that
Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, and so quadratic refinements of hermitian forms take values in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
.

Given a quadratic form
(P,\lambda,\mu)
over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, since
(1\pm T)\overline{(1\pm T)}=(1\pm T)^{2}=2(1\pm T)
, we have that
\mu((1\pm T)x)=(1\pm T)\mu(x)\overline{(1\pm T)}=2(1\pm T)\mu(x)
and
\lambda((1\pm T)x,(1\pm T)y)=(1\pm T)\lambda(x,y)\overline{(1\pm T)}=2(1\pm T)\lambda(x,y)
for every
x,y\in P
.
It follows that the restrictions of
\lambda
and
\mu
to
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
take values in
2\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
. We identify
\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
using the isomorphism
n\mapsto(1\pm T)n
. We refer to the inverse as dividing by
(1\pm T)
.
Under this identification
2\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is identified with
2\mathbb{Z}
.
Dividing by
2
gives rise to an isomorphism
2\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
We will use the composition

\frac{1}{2(1\pm T)}\colon 2\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\xrightarrow{/(1\pm T)}2\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{/2}\mathbb{Z}

in the following construction.

Construction 9.3
.

Given a quadratic form
(P,\lambda,\mu)
over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(1\pm T)}$}\circ\lambda|\colon\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\times\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\to\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(1\pm T)}$}\circ\mu|\colon\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\to\mathbb{Z}.

We write
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\lambda,\mu):=(\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P),\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\lambda),\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mu))
.
Similarly if
\theta=[\rho]\in Q_{\varepsilon}(P)

is a quadratic form, then we write
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\theta)\in Q_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P))

for the quadratic forms determined by this construction, namely the equivalence class represented by the pairing

\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(1\pm T)}$}\circ\rho|_{\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\times\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)}\colon\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\times\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)\to\mathbb{Z}.

Remark 9.4
.

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
is naturally a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module, where
T
acts by multiplication by
\pm 1
. But
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\lambda,\mu)
is not a quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
because
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
is not stably free over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
.

Remark 9.5
.

If
x,y\in\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
, then
\lambda(x,y)=2(1\pm T)\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\lambda)(x,y)
.

The following proposition can be straightforwardly verified from the definitions and the fact that
(P,\lambda,\mu)
is a quadratic form.

Proposition 9.6
.

The data
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\lambda,\mu)
determines a quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}
.
If
(P,\lambda,\mu)
is nonsingular, then so is
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\lambda,\mu)
.

Definition 9.7
.

The
plus form
and the
minus form
of a quadratic form
(P,\lambda,\mu)
over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
are respectively the quadratic forms
\mathcal{E}_{+}(P,\lambda,\mu)
and
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P,\lambda,\mu)
over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 9.8
.

Let
\psi\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{n})
be a quadratic form.
A matrix representing the quadratic form
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\psi)\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
can be obtained by plugging in
T=\pm 1
into a matrix representing
\psi
.

In particular, given a quadratic form
\theta\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
, the plus form of
(1-T)\theta\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{n})
is zero, while its minus form is
2\theta\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
.
To see this, let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be the standard basis for
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{n}
and pick a sesquilinear form
\rho
representing
\psi=[\rho]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{n})
.
Then
\{(1\pm T)e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a basis for
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{n})
.
For
p\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
we write
p(\pm 1)
for the evaluation of
p
at
\pm 1
.
Similarly to above we have

\rho((1\pm T)e_{i},(1\pm T)e_{j})=(1\pm T)\rho(e_{i},e_{j})\overline{(1\pm T)}=2(1\pm T)\rho(e_{i},e_{j})=2(1\pm T)\big(\rho(e_{i},e_{j})(\pm 1)\big).

Therefore the
(i,j)
entry of a matrix representing
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\rho)
is

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\theta)((1\pm T)e_{i},(1\pm T)e_{j})=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(1\pm T)}$}\Big(2(1\pm T)\big(\rho(e_{i},e_{j})(\pm 1)\big)\Big)=\rho(e_{i},e_{j})(\pm 1),

which arises by evaluating as asserted.

9.2.
Plus and minus quasi-formations

Given a quasi-formation over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, we describe how plus and minus forms can be used to produce two quasi-formations over
\mathbb{Z}
.

First of all, observe that if
i\colon V\hookrightarrow P
is a split injection
then the induced map

i_{\pm}\colon\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V)\to\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)

is also a split injection.

Proposition 9.9
.

If
x:=((P,\psi);F,V)
is a quasi-formation over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
,
then the triple

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(x)=(\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\psi);\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(F),\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V))

is a quasi-formation over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

First observe (e.g. using Example
9.8
) that
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\psi)
is hyperbolic over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Next we observe that since
F,V\subseteq P
are summands, we have inclusions of summands
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(F),\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)
.
Finally, one verifies that

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(F)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\psi)

is a lagrangian and

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V)

is a half rank direct summand.
∎

Using
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})
to denote the induced forms of a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
(Definition
7.16
), note that the induced form of

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P,\psi)
on
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V)
and
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V^{\perp})
coincide with
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(-\theta^{\perp})
respectively.

Proposition 9.10
.

The following assignments define monoid maps

f_{\pm}\colon\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\to\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}),[x]\mapsto[\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(x)].

Proof.

Since
f_{\pm}
maps trivial formations to trivial formations, we deduce that it preserves stable isomorphisms.
The reader can then verify that
f_{\pm}
maps quasi-formations of the form
((P,\psi);F,V)
and
((P,\psi);F,G)\oplus((P,\psi);G,V)
(which are equivalent in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
) to equivalent quasi-formations in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Additivity follows as well.
∎

In what follows, we will often write
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\Theta)=[\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(x)]
instead of
f_{\pm}(\Theta)
and refer to
\mathcal{E}_{+}(x)
and
\mathcal{E}_{-}(x)
as the
plus and minus quasi-formations
of
x
.

Remark 9.11
.

Given quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
, the monoid map
f_{\pm}
from Proposition
9.9
restricts to a map
f_{\pm}\colon\ell_{5}(v,v^{\prime})\to\ell_{5}(\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(v),\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(v^{\prime}))
.
This follows from the observation
that taking induced forms commutes with taking
\pm
-forms/formations i.e. the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\times\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]))}
