{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{zs}}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\pm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\pm}}

9.3.
Finding a lagrangian complement

The following lemma is a technical ingredient towards establishing our criterion for showing that a quasi-formation over
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
is elementary.
The lemma will justify that, for this purpose, it suffices to understand the plus and minus quasi-formations.
A sketch was given on
[
40
, p. 70]
.
We follow the same overall strategy, but the proof given below is considerably more detailed, and includes arguments not found in Kreck’s sketch.

Lemma 9.12
.

Suppose
(P,\lambda)
is a nonsingular, even, hermitian form on a free
\Lambda
-module
P
, with a free half-rank direct summand
V\subseteq P
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
is a lagrangian for
(\mathcal{E}_{+}(P),\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda))
and that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
admits a lagrangian complement.
Then
V\subseteq P
admits a lagrangian complement.

Proof.

We will frequently use the following fact.
Let
U
be a complementary free direct summand to
V
in
P
. Let
\{u_{i}\}
be a basis for
U
and let
\{v_{i}\}
be a basis for
V
.
Add linear combinations of basis elements of
V
to the basis elements for
U
, to obtain a set
\{u_{i}+\sum_{j}a_{ij}v_{j}\}
, for some
a_{ij}\in\Lambda
. An elementary linear algebra argument shows that this set is a basis for a new complementary direct summand
U^{\prime}
to
V
in
P
, for any
a_{ij}\in\Lambda
.

Now we begin the proof.
Choose a basis
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
for
V
and complete it to a basis of
P
via
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
.
Since
P
is free we may consider the dual basis elements
x_{i}^{*}\in P^{*}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
that satisfy
x_{i}^{*}(x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
and
x^{*}_{i}(v_{j})=0
for every
i,j
.
Since the form
\lambda
is nonsingular, we can choose
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in P
such that
\widehat{\lambda}(u_{i})=x_{i}^{*}
.
In particular, we have
\lambda(x_{i},u_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for every
i,j
.

We assert that the
x_{j}
and
u_{j}
generate
P
. We will prove this by showing that each
v_{i}
can be expressed as a linear combination of the
u_{j}
and
x_{j}
.
Write
u_{i}=\sum_{k}a_{ik}x_{k}+\sum_{l}b_{il}v_{l}
for some
a_{ik},b_{il}\in\Lambda
.
Arrange the
u_{i},x_{k},v_{l}
as column vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}
and the
a_{ij},b_{il}
as matrices
A,B
so that, formally, we can write
\mathbf{u}=A\mathbf{x}+B\mathbf{v}
.
Since we have
\lambda(u_{i},x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
and since the form
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)
vanishes on
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)\times\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
, we obtain

\delta_{ij}=\lambda(u_{i},x_{j})=\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)((1+T)u_{i},(1+T)x_{j})=\sum_{l=1}^{n}b_{il}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)((1+T)v_{l},(1+T)x_{j}).

The second equality here uses that
\lambda(u_{i},x_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Write
L
for the matrix with coefficients
L_{lj}=\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)((1+T)v_{l},(1+T)x_{j})
and
I_{n}
for the size
n
identity matrix so that
I_{n}=BL
.
A square matrix with coefficients in a commutative ring admits an inverse if and only if its determinant is a unit. That inverse is both a left and a right inverse. The equalities
1=\det(I_{n})=\det(BL)=\det(B)\det(L)
imply that
\det(B)
is a unit.
We deduce that
B
is invertible with inverse
L
.
Therefore
\mathbf{u}=A\mathbf{x}+B\mathbf{v}
can be rewritten as
\mathbf{v}=L(\mathbf{u}-A\mathbf{x})
.
Thus the
v_{i}
can be expressed as linear combinations of the
u_{j}
and
x_{j}
.
This concludes the proof of the assertion the
x_{j}
and
u_{j}
generate
P
.

Since
\{x_{j}\}\cup\{u_{j}\}
form a rank
n
generating set of
P\cong\Lambda^{n}
, they are also linearly independent.
This statement follows from the general fact, which we now prove, that a surjective
\Lambda
-linear map
f\colon\Lambda^{n}\to\Lambda^{n}
is necessarily injective.
Let
A
be a matrix for
f
with respect to the canonical basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
of
\Lambda^{n}
.
Surjectivity implies that there are column vectors
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
such that
f(b_{i})=e_{i}
.
Arrange the column vectors
b_{i}
into a matrix
B
such that
AB=I_{n}
.
Taking determinants, as above we deduce that
f
is also injective, as claimed.

Since
\{x_{j}\}\cup\{u_{j}\}
forms a basis of
P
, it follows that the
u_{i}
span a half rank direct summand
U_{0}\subseteq P
with
P=V\oplus U_{0}
and
\lambda(x_{i},u_{j})=\delta_{ij}
.

Let
U_{-}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
be the hypothesised complementary lagrangian to
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
.
First, we will modify
U_{0}
to obtain a direct summand
U_{1}\subseteq P
that is complementary to
V
and such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{1})=U_{-}
.
Then we will improve
U_{1}
further, to obtain a lagrangian complement
U_{2}
for
V
.

Write
b_{i}:=(1-T)x_{i}
for the basis of
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)
obtained from
\{x_{i}\}
.
Recall that we have a basis
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
for
U_{0}
and that
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\}
is a basis for
P
.
Write
(1-T)u_{i}=w_{i}+z_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{0})
with respect to the decomposition
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)\cong\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus U_{-}
, i.e.
w_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)
and
z_{i}\in U_{-}
for each
i
.
Write
w_{i}=\sum_{j}n_{ij}b_{j}
for some
n_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Define
U_{1}\subseteq P
, a new complementary direct summand to
V
, as the submodule with basis given by the elements

y_{i}:=u_{i}-\sum_{j}n_{ij}x_{j}.

Here we used the fact from the first paragraph of the proof to see that
U_{1}
is a direct complement to V.

To confirm that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{1})=U_{-}
,
first note that
\{(1-T)y_{i}\}
forms a basis for
\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{1})
, because
\{y_{i}\}
forms a basis for
U_{1}
.
Next, note that
\{(1-T)x_{1},\dots,(1-T)x_{n},(1-T)u_{1},\dots,(1-T)u_{n}\}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n},w_{1}+z_{1},\dots,w_{n}+z_{n}\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)=\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{0})
, and therefore so is
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
, because
w_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)
for all
i
.
Therefore
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus U_{-}\cong\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
, and we deduce that
\{z_{i}\}
forms a basis for
U_{-}
. Now we compute that

(1-T)y_{i}=(1-T)u_{i}-\textstyle{\sum_{j}}n_{ij}(1-T)x_{j}=w_{i}+z_{i}-\textstyle{\sum_{j}}n_{ij}b_{j}=z_{i}.

It follows that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{1})=U_{-}
as asserted.
This completes the
U_{1}
step. Note that
U_{1}
need not be a lagrangian, so the proof is not yet complete.

At this point, we consider the following subspaces with their respective bases shown underneath each subspace:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)&&\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)&&U_{+}:=\mathcal{E}_{+}(U_{1})&&U_{-}=\mathcal{E}_{-}(U_{1})\\
\{a_{i}:=(1+T)x_{i}\}&&\{b_{i}:=(1-T)x_{i}\}&&\{t_{i}^{+}:=(1+T)y_{i}\}&&\{t_{i}^{-}:=(1-T)y_{i}\}.\end{array}

Next we modify the bases
t^{\pm}_{i}
to something more favourable. Consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-valued forms

\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\lambda)
on
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(P)=\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(V)\oplus{U}_{\pm}
; this decomposition holds because
P=V\oplus U_{1}
. With respect to this decomposition, these forms are represented by block diagonal matrices over
\mathbb{Z}
,
say
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}(\lambda)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{\pm}&Y_{\pm}\\
Y_{\pm}^{T}&B_{\pm}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Observe for later use that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)|_{U_{+}\times U_{+}}
is even.
To see this note that
\lambda(y_{i},y_{i})\in 2\Lambda
because
\lambda
is even and
p+\overline{p}\in 2\Lambda
for every
p\in\Lambda
.
Therefore

\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)(t_{i}^{+},t_{i}^{+})=\frac{1}{2(1+T)}\lambda((1+T)y_{i},(1+T)y_{i})=\varepsilon(\lambda(y_{i},y_{i}))\in 2\mathbb{Z},

where the last equality uses
\lambda(y_{i},y_{i})\in 2\Lambda
and
\varepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
denotes the augmentation map, defined by
\varepsilon(a+bT)=a+b
.

In the “
+
” case,
A_{+}=0
because
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
is a lagrangian. Thus, since
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)
is nonsingular, we deduce that
Y_{+}
is nonsingular. Using column operations,
Y_{+}
can be made into the identity matrix. This corresponds to performing elementary basis change operations on the basis
\{t_{i}^{+}\}
of
{U}_{+}
(using only linear combinations of the
t_{i}^{+}
).
We denote the resulting basis of
U_{+}
by
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)(a_{i},c_{j})=\delta_{ij}
.
Since
c_{i}\in U_{+}\subseteq P
and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)\subseteq P
, we have that
\lambda(a_{i},c_{j})=2(1+T)\delta_{ij}
(recall Remark
9.5
).
In fact, in the
+
-case, since
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)|_{U_{+}\times U_{+}}
is even, by adding linear combinations of the
a_{i}
, we can assume that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)(c_{i},c_{j})=0
for all
i,j
.
Note that
U_{+}
may not satisfy
\mathcal{E}_{+}(U_{1})=U_{+}
anymore, since we changed the basis for
U_{+}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}(U_{1})
but did not make the corresponding basis change for
U_{1}\subseteq P
, but this does not affect the remainder of the argument.

In the “
-
” case,
B_{-}=0
because
U_{-}
is a lagrangian. Thus, since
\mathcal{E}_{-}(\lambda)
is nonsingular, we deduce that
Y_{-}
is nonsingular. Arguing as in the “
+
” case, we perform elementary basis change operations on
t_{i}^{-}
to obtain a basis
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
of
{U}_{-}
such that
\lambda(b_{i},d_{j})=2(1-T)\delta_{ij}
, again because
d_{j}\in U_{-}\subseteq P
and
b_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\subseteq P
(recall Remark
9.5
).

We summarise the outcome. With respect to the basis
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{E}_{+}(P)
and
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
, matrices for
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{E}_{-}(\lambda)
) are given by:

\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}\ \ \quad\text{and}\quad\ \ \mathcal{E}_{-}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}*&I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}.

We claim that, for all
i,j
, we have:

(24)

\lambda(a_{i},a_{j})=0,\,\,\lambda(a_{i},c_{j})=2(1+T)\delta_{ij},\,\,\lambda(b_{i},d_{j})=2(1-T)\delta_{ij},\text{ and }\lambda(c_{i},c_{j})=0=\lambda(d_{i},d_{j}).

An argument is needed to see that the first and the last two equalities hold. We will just argue for
\lambda(c_{i},c_{j})=0
; the others proceed similarly.
Since
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\lambda)(c_{i},c_{j})=0
, then
\lambda(c_{i},c_{j})\in 2\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Lambda)
maps to zero under the identifications
2\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Lambda)\cong 2\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and therefore
\lambda(c_{i},c_{j})=0
.

Now, using the same elementary basis change operations we just used to change
t_{i}^{-}
into
d_{i}
, transform the basis
\{y_{i}\}
of
U_{1}
into a new basis
\{\rho^{\prime}_{i}\}
of
U_{{1}}
.
For this we take the basis changes over
\mathbb{Z}
and consider them as basis changes over
\Lambda
via the ring homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\to\Lambda
.
By construction we also have
(1-T)\rho^{\prime}_{i}=d_{i}
.
Write
(1+T)\rho^{\prime}_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)\oplus U_{+}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
and
\gamma_{i}\in U_{+}
, and note that

\rho^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+\gamma_{i}+d_{i}}{2}.

Next we claim we can make a further helpful basis change from the
\rho_{i}^{\prime}
to a new basis for
U_{1}
, given by

\rho_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+c_{i}+d_{i}}{2}.

We must prove
\alpha_{i}+c_{i}+d_{i}
is indeed divisible by
2
and that the resulting
\rho_{i}
form a basis, then after that we will modify
U_{1}
to obtain the desired lagrangian complement
U_{2}
to
V
.

To begin proving the claim that
\alpha_{i}+c_{i}+d_{i}
is divisible by
2
and that
\{\rho_{i}\}
forms a basis for
U_{1}
, first observe that
since the
\{c_{i}\}
form a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
U_{+}
, for each
j
we can write
\gamma_{j}=\sum_{k}\gamma_{jk}c_{k}
with
\gamma_{jk}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It is also the case that the
c_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}(P)
can be written as
c_{k}=(1+T)z_{k}
, for some
z_{k}\in P
. We note for later use that

(25)

2\lambda(x_{i},c_{k})=\lambda(x_{i},(1+T)(1+T)z_{k})=\lambda((1+T)x_{i},(1+T)z_{k})=\lambda(a_{i},c_{k}).

Similarly
2\lambda(x_{i},\alpha_{j})=\lambda(a_{i},\alpha_{j})
and
2\lambda(x_{i},(1-T)\rho^{\prime}_{j})=\lambda(b_{i},(1-T)\rho^{\prime}_{j})
.
Using (
25
), we observe that

\displaystyle 4\lambda(x_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=2\lambda(x_{i},\alpha_{j})+2\lambda(x_{i},\gamma_{j})+2\lambda(x_{i},d_{j})

\displaystyle=2\lambda(x_{i},\alpha_{j})+2\sum_{k}\lambda(x_{i},\gamma_{jk}c_{k})+2\lambda(x_{i},(1-T)\rho^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=\lambda(a_{i},\alpha_{j})+\sum_{k}\lambda(a_{i},\gamma_{jk}c_{k})+\lambda(b_{i},(1-T)\rho^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=0+2(1+T)\gamma_{ji}+\lambda(b_{i},d_{j})

\displaystyle=2(1+T)\gamma_{ji}+2(1-T)\delta_{ij}\in\Lambda.

For the last two equalities we applied (
24
).
Since
\Lambda
is torsion-free as an abelian group, we deduce that

(26)

2\lambda(x_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{j})=(1+T)\gamma_{ji}+(1-T)\delta_{ij}.

Write
\lambda(x_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{j})=r_{ij}+s_{ij}(1-T)
with
r_{ij},s_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
; this is possible because
a+bT=a+b-(1-T)b
for every
a+bT\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}].

Plugging in
T=1
into (
26
) gives
r_{ij}=\gamma_{ji}
.
It follows that setting
T=-1
into (
26
), we get
2\delta_{ij}=2\gamma_{ji}+4s_{ij}
, which we write as
2s_{ij}=\delta_{ij}-\gamma_{ji}
.
Now define

\rho_{i}:=\rho^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{k}s_{ki}c_{k}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+d_{i}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}(\delta_{ki}-\gamma_{ik})c_{k}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+d_{i}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\delta_{ki}c_{k}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+c_{i}+d_{i}}{2}.

Since
c_{i}\in U_{+}=\mathcal{E}_{+}(U_{1})\subseteq U_{1}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
and
\{\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{n}\}
forms a basis of
U_{1}
, it follows that
\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}\}
again forms a basis for
U_{1}
.
This concludes the proof of the claim.

Next we observe some symmetries in the coefficients of
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
a_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
.
Write

\alpha_{i}=\sum_{j}\alpha_{ij}a_{j}

for some
\alpha_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Recall that
\lambda(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=0
,
\lambda(c_{i},c_{j})=0
,
\lambda(d_{i},d_{j})=0
, and
\lambda(a_{i},c_{j})=2(1+T)\delta_{ij}
, by (
24
). We use these and the fact that
\lambda|_{\mathcal{E}_{+}(P)\times\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)}=0
, which follows from the equality
(1+T)(1-T)=0
, to
compute that

\displaystyle\lambda(\rho_{i},\rho_{j})

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\frac{1}{4}}\lambda(\alpha_{i}+c_{i}+d_{i},\alpha_{j}+c_{j}+d_{j})

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\lambda(\alpha_{i},c_{j})+\lambda(c_{i},\alpha_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\lambda\left(\sum_{k}\alpha_{ik}a_{k},c_{j}\right)+\lambda\left(c_{i},\sum_{s}\alpha_{js}a_{s}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\frac{1}{4}}\cdot 2(1+T)\cdot\left(\alpha_{ij}+\alpha_{ji}\right)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}(1+T)\cdot\left(\alpha_{ij}+\alpha_{ji}\right).

Because
\lambda(\rho_{i},\rho_{j})\in\Lambda
and
\alpha_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we deduce that
\alpha_{ij}+\alpha_{ji}\in 2\mathbb{Z}.

We now modify the summand
U_{1}\subseteq P
into a lagrangian complement to
V
. As
V_{+}\subseteq V
, so
a_{j}\in V
for all
j
. Therefore if we modify
\rho_{i}
by adding multiples of
a_{j}
we will obtain a new direct summand of
P
that is still complementary to
V
. Of course, the objective is to do this so that the resulting direct summand is self-annihilating. Consider that for
p\in\Lambda
we have

\rho_{i}+pa_{j}=\frac{\alpha_{i}+2pa_{j}+c_{i}+d_{i}}{2}

so the proposed operation modifies the coefficient
\alpha_{ij}
of
a_{j}
in
\alpha_{i}
by the addition of
2p
. Using this operation, and the symmetries in the
\alpha_{ij}
(namely
\alpha_{ij}+\alpha_{ji}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
), we may now modify the coefficients
\alpha_{ij}
to
\alpha_{ij}^{\prime}
, which satisfy that
\alpha^{\prime}_{ij}+\alpha^{\prime}_{ji}=0
for all
i,j
.
We obtain a new direct summand
U_{2}
of
P
, which is still complementary to
V
by the first paragraph of this proof.
We write
\rho_{i}^{\prime}
for the modified basis element coming from
\rho_{i}
.
The same computation as we made for
\lambda(\rho_{i},\rho_{j})
above now shows that
\lambda(\rho^{\prime}_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{j})=0
for all
i,j
(here it is important that
\lambda(a_{i},a_{j})=0
for all
i,j
), and thus
U_{2}
is a complementary sublagrangian to
V
, i.e.
U_{2}\subseteq U_{2}^{\perp}
.
Since
U_{2}\subseteq P
is a half rank sublagrangian and
\lambda
is nonsingular, it must in fact be a lagrangian; see e.g.
[
61
, Proposition 11.53]
.
We have therefore found a complementary lagrangian to
V\subseteq P
.
∎

9.4.
A criterion for being elementary in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])

We now prove the promised criterion, which ensures that a class
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary.

Lemma 9.13
.

If
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(\Theta)
is elementary, then
\Theta
is represented by a quasi-formation
x^{\prime}=((P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime});F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P^{\prime})
admits a lagrangian complement.

Proof.

Let
x=((P,\psi);F,V)
be a representative of
\Theta
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{-}(\Theta)
is elementary, Proposition
7.15
implies that there is a formation
f=(H_{+}(L);L,G)
over
\mathbb{Z}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus G
admits a lagrangian complement in
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)\oplus(L\oplus L^{*})
.
Now consider the formation

f\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]:=(H_{+}(L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]);L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}],G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]).

and set
x^{\prime}:=x\oplus(f\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(f\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])\cong f
as can be seen by reasoning in terms of bases for the Lagrangians of
f
.

Since
L_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=0
, observe that
[x^{\prime}]=[x]=\Theta

and, by construction,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{-}(x)\oplus\mathcal{E}_{-}(f\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}_{-}(P,\psi),\mathcal{E}_{-}(F),\mathcal{E}_{-}(V))\oplus f

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}_{-}(P,\psi)\oplus H_{+}(L),\mathcal{E}_{-}(F)\oplus L,\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus G)

is such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\oplus G
admits a Lagrangian complement in
\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)\oplus(L\oplus L^{*})
.
∎

Proposition 9.14
.

An element
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary if it satisfies both:

(1)

\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Theta)
is represented by a formation, so
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Theta)\in L_{5}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}\subseteq\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
, and

(2)

\mathcal{E}_{-}(\Theta)
is elementary.

Proof.

Let
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
and assume that (1) and (2) hold.
Thanks to Lemma
9.13
we can represent
\Theta
as
\Theta=[x]=[((P,\psi);F,V)]
where the quasi-formation
x=((P,\psi);F,V)
is such that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
admits a lagrangian complement.

We assert that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{+}(P,\psi)
is a lagrangian.
Proposition
7.20
ensures that the induced forms of
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Theta)=[\mathcal{E}_{+}(x)]\in L_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
are independent up to
0
-stabilisation of the quasi-formation representative of
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Theta)
and therefore of
\Theta
.
Since
[\mathcal{E}_{+}(x)]
is represented by a formation, the induced form, which lies in the 0-stabilisation class of
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V,\psi)
, is trivial. It follows that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\psi)
vanishes on
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
.
Since
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
is a half rank direct summand,
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
is a lagrangian as asserted.

By the assertion and the hypothesis that
\mathcal{E}_{-}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{-}(P)
admits a lagrangian complement, the hypotheses of Lemma
9.12
are satisfied for
(P,\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is the symmetrisation of
\psi
. Here we should also remark that since
P
is stably free is it free by Lemma
3.9
, and that
\lambda
is even and nonsingular since it arises as the symmetrisation of the quadratic form
\psi
in a quasi-formation.
Lemma
9.12
therefore implies that
V\subseteq P
admits a lagrangian complement
L
, with respect to
\lambda
.
As
L
is a direct summand of the free group
P
, it is projective. By Corollary
3.8
,
L
is thus also a lagrangian with respect to the quadratic form
\psi
.
Thus by Proposition
7.13
, and since
L_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])=0

[
65
, Theorem 13A.1]
, the class
\Theta=[x]\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
is elementary.
∎

Proposition 9.15
.

Fix an integer
h\geq 0
.
If
(\mathbb{Z}^{h},\theta)
is a nondegenerate quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}
such that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
is trivial, then every
\Theta\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
is elementary.

Proof.

By Example
9.8
, the induced positive and negative forms are given by evaluation
T=\pm 1
respectively.
Thus the induced positive form of
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
is trivial, and the induced negative form of
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
is
(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
.

Write
\Theta=[((P,\psi);F,V)]
.
The fact that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)

is the zero form implies, as in the proof of Proposition
9.14
, that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(V)
is a lagrangian.
It follows that
\mathcal{E}_{+}(\Theta)\in L_{5}(\mathbb{Z})
.
In addition,
\mathcal{E}_{-}(\Theta)\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
, and so is necessarily elementary because
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
is trivial by assumption.
The proposition now follows from Proposition
9.14
.
∎

10.
Analysis of the surgery obstruction

We summarise the outcome of the previous two sections on modified surgery in the next theorem.
We then explain how we are going to apply this theorem to surface exteriors.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be two
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and normal
1
-type
(B,\xi)
.
For
i=0,1
let
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
be normal
1
-smoothings into
(B,\xi)
, and let
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
be a homeomorphism that is compatible with the
1
-smoothings, i.e.
\overline{\nu}_{1}|_{\partial M_{1}}\circ f=\overline{\nu}_{0}|_{\partial M_{0}}
.
Suppose that
(M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{0}\cup-\overline{\nu}_{1})
is null-bordant over
(B,\xi)
.

Let
(\mathbb{Z}^{h},\theta)\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{h})
be a nondegenerate quadratic form such that
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
is trivial, and such that
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
is a free Wall form for
M_{0}
.
Assume that for both
i=0
and
i=1
, the pair
(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i})
admits a free Wall form that is
0
-stably equivalent to
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
.
Then
f
extends to a homeomorphism
M_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}M_{1}
.

Proof.

By assumption
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
are bordant over their normal
1
-type, say via a cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
.
Consider the modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
.
Proposition
8.8
states that
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta)
.
Since
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta)
is trivial, Proposition
9.15
ensures that
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
is elementary.

Theorem
8.6
implies that
(W,\overline{\nu})
is bordant rel. boundary over
(B,\xi)
to an
s
-cobordism.
The
5
-dimensional
s
-cobordism theorem
[
20
, Theorem 7.1A]
, which applies since
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a good group (every finite group is good see e.g.
[
6
, Theorem 19.2]
) with trivial Whitehead torsion, then implies that
f
extends to a homeomorphism
M_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}M_{1}
.
∎

10.1.
The free Wall form of
X_{F}

We will set up some language to describe a specific free Wall form (see Definition
8.7
) for the exterior
X_{F}
of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface
F
, which has the convenient property of being directly computable from the integral intersection form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
of
\widetilde{X}_{F}
.
Since
X_{F}
spin, so is
\widetilde{X}_{F}
, and therefore the integral intersection form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is even
[
22
, Proposition 3.3]
.
By Proposition
5.12

we have

H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}.

The form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
descends to a nondegenerate form
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
on this quotient.
Because
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is even, so is
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
.
Thus
Q_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
determines a unique nondegenerate quadratic form
(\mathbb{Z}^{h},\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}})
over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Now, remembering the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module structure in order to identify
\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, we will think of the integral quadratic form
\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}\penalty\
as an equivalence class of a pairing:

(27)

\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}=\big[\rho\colon(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-})^{h}\times(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-})^{h}\to\mathbb{Z}\big].

Recall this is not a quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
because the module
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-})^{h}
is not stably free.
The next proposition establishes a formula for a free Wall form for
X_{F}
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
F\subseteq D^{4}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface of nonorientable genus
h
, and let
\overline{\nu}\colon X_{F}\to B:=\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
be a normal
1
-smoothing.
Then a free Wall form for
X_{F}
is given by
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},\vartheta)
, where the quadratic form
\vartheta
is represented by the pairing

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\times\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}],\qquad(x,y)\mapsto(1-T)\rho(x\otimes 1,y\otimes 1).

We write
\vartheta=(1-T)\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}.

Proof.

First we describe a specific free Wall form for
X_{F}
, and then we show that it can indeed be expressed as claimed in the proposition.
Since
{\operatorname{TOPSpin}}
is simply-connected,
\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BTOPSpin})=0
, and therefore we deduce that
\pi_{2}(B)\cong\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2})=0
.
Since
K\pi_{2}(X_{F})=\ker(\pi_{2}(X_{F})\to\pi_{2}(B))
and
\pi_{2}(X_{F})\cong H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])
, we deduce that

K\pi_{2}(X_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})=\pi_{2}(X_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})=H_{2}(X_{F};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}])/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h},

where the last isomorphism follows from Proposition
5.5
We have therefore showed that the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module underlying the nondegenerate Wall form
(K\pi_{2}(X_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}}),\psi_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
is defined on
\mathbb{Z}^{h}
.
The tensor product

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-},\qquad x\mapsto x\otimes 1

is a surjection of left
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-modules.
Identifying
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{-}=\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}
, we thus obtain a surjection
\varpi\colon\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h}\to\mathbb{Z}^{h}_{-}
. We use
\varpi
to pull back the quadratic form
(K\pi_{2}(X_{F})/\operatorname{rad}(\lambda_{X_{F}}),\psi_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
, thus obtaining a representative
(x,y)\mapsto\rho(\varpi(x),\varpi(y))
for a free Wall form
\vartheta
, where
\rho
is as defined in (
27
).
Observe that the symmetrisation of this quadratic form is
(x,y)\mapsto\lambda_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}(\varpi(x),\varpi(y))
.

We now establish the expression
\vartheta=(1-T)\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
for this free Wall form. Recall that if a hermitian form on a free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-module admits a quadratic refinement, then it admits a unique quadratic refinement (Corollary
3.8
), and therefore is the symmetrisation of a unique quadratic form.
Observe that the symmetrisation of
\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
is
Q^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
.
Thus it now suffices to show the symmetrisation
\lambda
of
\vartheta
is given by the symmetrisation
(1-T)Q^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
of the claimed expression
(1-T)\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}
. To see this, we compute:

\lambda(x,y)=\lambda_{X_{F}}^{\operatorname{nd}}(\varpi(x),\varpi(y))=\lambda^{\operatorname{nd}}_{X_{F}}(x\otimes 1,y\otimes 1)=(1-T)Q^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{X}_{F}}(x\otimes 1,y\otimes 1).

Here, the first equality comes from the fact that symmetrisation and pull-back commute, and the final equality follows from the third item of Proposition
5.12
. This concludes the proof of Proposition
10.2
.
∎

11.
Proofs of Theorems
B
,
C
, and
E

We now prove Theorem
B
, whose statement we recall for the reader’s convenience. Recall that Theorem
B
implies Theorem
A
, by taking
K
to be the unknot.

Theorem
B
.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
and the same boundary
K
. Assume that
|\det(K)|=1
.
If either
h\leq 3
or
e
is non-extremal, then
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

Proof.

For
i=0,1
set
M_{i}:=X_{F_{i}}
for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surface exteriors. Recall that
B:=\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}\xrightarrow{\xi}\operatorname{BSTOP}
is the normal 1-type of
M_{i}
.
By Proposition
8.3
there are normal 1-smoothings
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
and there is a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}
such that
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
are
B
-bordant relative to
f
. In other words, writing

M:=M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1}
, we have that
M
is spin,
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
(M,\overline{\nu}_{0}\cup-\overline{\nu}_{1})
is null-bordant over
B
.
Moreover
f
restricts to a homeomorphism
S(\nu F_{0})\to S(\nu F_{1})
that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary.
Proposition
10.2
shows that a free Wall form for
M_{i}
is given by the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
-quadratic form
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{h},(1-T)\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
where
\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
denotes the non-degenerate
\mathbb{Z}
-quadratic form determined by the even form
Q_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}}
on
H_{2}(\widetilde{M}_{i})/\operatorname{rad}(Q_{\widetilde{M}_{i}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{h}
.
Set
\sigma:=\sigma(\Sigma_{2}(F_{i}))
, the signature of the
2
-fold cover of
D^{4}
branched over
F_{i}
.
Recall from Theorem
2.5
that
|\sigma|\neq h
if and only if
e
is non-extremal.
By Proposition
5.11
we know that

\theta^{\operatorname{nd}}_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}=\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\cong\begin{cases}2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{h}{2}-1}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma=0,\\
X_{\sigma}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus\frac{1}{2}(h-|\sigma|)}&\quad\text{ if }\sigma\neq 0.\end{cases}

Proposition
7.44
states that when
h\leq 3
or if
|\sigma|\neq h
(i.e. if
e
is non-extremal), then
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
is trivial.
Theorem
10.1
now implies that
f
extends to homeomorphism
X_{F_{0}}\cong X_{F_{1}}
.
The
\nu
-extendability condition implies that
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are equivalent rel. boundary.
That is, there is a homeomorphism
G\colon D^{4}\to D^{4}
with
G|_{S^{3}}=\operatorname{Id}|_{S^{3}}
and
G(F_{0})=F_{1}
. By the Alexander trick,
G
is isotopic rel. boundary to
\operatorname{Id}_{D^{4}}
and therefore
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.
∎

In the proof of Theorem
11
, the assumption
\det(K)=1
was essentially only used to be able to analyse the isometry type of the intersection form
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F)}
.
In general, if
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
have the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
, and the same boundary
K
, we do not know whether
Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{0})}\cong Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{1})}
.
Even if this were the case however, there would still be a further potential obstruction in
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
to surface exteriors being homeomorphic rel. boundary.
Nikulin’s theorem ensures that if
2\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}
splits off a
2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
-summand then
\ell_{5}(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{i}}}^{\operatorname{nd}})
is trivial.
For general
K
, we do not know whether this set is trivial, but
this is always the case after we add a single tube to both
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
, as we now prove.
We recall the statement of Theorem
C
for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem
C
.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}\subseteq D^{4}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces of the same nonorientable genus
h
, the same normal Euler number
e
and the same boundary
K
.
Then
F_{0}\#T^{2}
and
F_{1}\#T^{2}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.

Proof.

Since
F_{0},F_{1}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-surfaces with the same nonorientable genus, the same boundary and the same normal Euler number, a result of Baykur–Sunukjian
[
5
, Theorem 1]
(cf.
[
14
, Appendix]
, where their proof was adapted to the topological category) implies that they become ambiently isotopic rel. boundary after adding
n>0
unknotted tubes to each.
Adding a tube adds an
S^{2}\times S^{2}
connected summand to the
2
-fold branched cover, and therefore adds a
H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
summand to the intersection form of the
2
-fold branched cover.
Thus we have

(28)

Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{0}\#^{n}T^{2})}=Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{0})}\oplus H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus n}\cong Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{1})}\oplus H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus n}=Q_{\Sigma_{2}(F_{1}\#^{n}T^{2})}.

As above we write
M_{i}:=X_{F_{i}}
.
Applying Proposition
5.7
to (
28
) and passing to quadratic forms, we obtain

\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{0}\#^{n}T^{2}}^{\operatorname{nd}}}=\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus n}\cong\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{1}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus n}=\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{1}\#^{n}T^{2}}^{\operatorname{nd}}}.

A cancellation result due to Bass
[
3
, Corollary IV.3.6]
(cf.
[
14
, Proposition 7.3]
for an explanation as to how Bass’s general theory applies to this simple setting) gives an isometry
\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{1}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})

and thus

\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{0}\#T^{2}}^{\operatorname{nd}}}=\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{0}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{1}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})=\theta_{\widetilde{X}_{F_{1}\#T^{2}}^{\operatorname{nd}}}.

We now follow the same steps as in Theorem
11
to verify that
F_{0}\#T^{2}
and
F_{1}\#T^{2}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.
Proposition
10.2
implies that the free Wall form of
F_{i}\#T^{2}
is
(1-T)(\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}}\oplus H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
.
We also know by Proposition
8.3
that we can find normal 1-smoothings
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon X_{F_{i}}\to B:=\operatorname{BTOPSpin}\times B\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and a homeomorphism
f\colon\partial X_{F_{0}\#T^{2}}\cong\partial X_{F_{1}\#T^{2}}
, such that the union along
f
is spin, has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and is null-bordant over
B
.
Moreover
f
restricts to a homeomorphism
S(\nu(F_{0}\#T^{2}))\to S(\nu(F_{1}\#T^{2}))
that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary.
Thanks to the criterion from Theorem
10.1
, it now suffices to prove that
\ell_{5}((\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})\oplus 2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
is trivial.

We verify the assumptions of Nikulin’s Theorem
7.35
for

(V,\theta):=(\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})\oplus 2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}).

Let
\lambda
denote the symmetrisation of
\theta
.
We claim that
\operatorname{rk}(V)=h+2\geq n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))+2
for every odd prime
p
. To see this let

\lambda^{\prime}:=2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}}+(2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})^{*}

and note that
n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda^{\prime}}))\leq h
. Adding
2H^{+}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\lambda^{\prime}
to obtain
\lambda
adds
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
to the cokernel, but does not affect the odd primary part. Therefore
n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))=n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda^{\prime}}))\leq h
, so
n_{p}(\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda}))+2\leq h+2=\operatorname{rk}(V)
, as claimed. The second condition of Theorem
7.35
holds since
\theta
splits off a
2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
summand.
By Corollary
7.36
we therefore have that
\ell_{5}((\mathbb{Z}^{h},2\theta_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}^{\operatorname{nd}})\oplus 2H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}))
is trivial.

Theorem
10.1
now implies that
f
extends to a rel. boundary homeomorphism
X_{F_{0}\#T^{2}}\cong X_{F_{1}\#T^{2}}
.
The
\nu
-extendability condition implies that
F_{0}\#T^{2}
and
F_{1}\#T^{2}
are equivalent rel. boundary, which again using the Alexander trick implies they are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary.
∎

Finally, we give the proof of Theorem
E
from the introduction, whose statement we recall now.

Theorem
E
.

Suppose that for
i=0,1
, the images
F_{i}:=g_{i}(F)

satisfy the hypotheses of one of Theorems
A
,
B
, or
C

(
in the latter case, we assume that
g_{i}(F)
are the stabilised surfaces
)
.
The embeddings
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary if and only if the homeomorphism

\psi:=g_{1}\circ(g_{0})^{-1}\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}

induces an isometry of the Guillou-Marin forms.

Proof.

Suppose that
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary. Then in particular there is a homeomorphism
\Phi\colon N\to N
such that
\Phi\circ g_{0}=g_{1}\colon F\hookrightarrow N
. The Guillou-Marin form is defined geometrically, using immersed discs in the ambient
N
(Definition
6.12
). As
\psi
is the restriction of the homeomorphism
\Phi
, this immediately implies that
f
induces an isometry of forms because the geometric definitions match up.

For the converse, assume that
\psi
induces an isometry of the Guillou-Marin forms.
In the proofs of Theorems
A
,
B
, or
C
, we constructed ambient isotopies
F_{0}\sim F_{1}
. The construction required that we chose a homeomorphism
F_{0}\to F_{1}
that preserves the Guillou-Marin form. This choice was made during the proof of Proposition
6.2
and was made arbitrarily (the existence of such a homeomorphism was shown to follow from the hypothesis that
e(F_{0})=e(F_{1})
). In the present proof, we may choose this homeomorphism to be
\psi\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}
.
As in Proposition
6.2
, this homeomorphism becomes part of the construction of a homeomorphism
\overline{\psi}\colon S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})\xrightarrow{\cong}S(\nu_{F_{0}\subseteq D^{4}})
, that is
\nu
-extendable rel. boundary, and with the property that gluing
X_{F_{0}}
and
X_{F_{1}}
using a homeomorphism
\partial X_{F_{0}}\to\partial X_{F_{1}}
that restricts to
\overline{\psi}
produces a spin union.
The resultant ambient isotopies produced by our proofs of Theorems
A
,
B
, or
C
then induce the chosen
\psi
. Expanding what this means, we have achieved that
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel. boundary as desired. Indeed, we have produced an isotopy
G_{t}\colon N\to N
with
G_{0}=\operatorname{Id}_{N}
,
G_{1}|_{F_{0}}=\psi\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}
. We therefore have that
G_{t}|_{F_{0}}\circ g_{0}\colon F\hookrightarrow N
is an isotopy rel. boundary from
G_{0}|_{F_{0}}\circ g_{0}=\operatorname{Id}_{N}\circ g_{0}=g_{0}
to
G_{1}|_{F_{0}}\circ g_{0}=g_{1}\circ g_{0}^{-1}\circ g_{0}=g_{1}
.
Since
G_{t}
is an isotopy of
N
,
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are ambiently isotopic, as desired.
∎

Appendix A
Quadratic linking forms for
h=2,3
and extremal normal Euler number

During our proof of Theorem
11
for nonorientable surfaces with nonorientable genus
h=2,3
and extremal normal Euler number we used that for the quadratic forms
(V,\theta)=(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\pm\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right])\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
and
(V,\theta)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\left[\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\right)\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
, the set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,2\theta)
is trivial.
More specifically, this was asserted in the proof of Proposition
7.44
.
This section proves these facts.
As we mentioned above Corollary
7.36
(see specifically the diagram in (
22
)), for a quadratic form
(V,\theta)\in Q_{+}(V)
over
\mathbb{Z}
with symmetrisation
(V,\lambda)
we have
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)=\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)
.
We will use Sage to calculate
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)
with respect to a particular basis and then show that
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(V,\theta))
is surjective.

A.1.
Extremal normal Euler number calculation for
h=2
.

We prove that for the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right])\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})=:Q
, the set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,2\theta)
is trivial.

Lemma A.1
.

The quadratic form
\psi:=[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
is represented by

\widetilde{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}.

The cokernel of the adjoint
\widehat{\lambda}
of the symmetrisation
\lambda
of
\psi
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
and, for
[x],[y]\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
the boundary symmetric form
\partial\lambda\colon(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is given by

\partial\lambda([x],[y])=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{4}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{4}\end{pmatrix}y.

The boundary split quadratic form
\partial\psi\colon(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is given by

\partial\psi\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{8}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

We claim that
2\theta\cong\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&0\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}).

Use
e_{1},e_{2}
to denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Perform the basis change which replaces
e_{1},e_{2}
by
e_{1}-e_{2},e_{2}
.
With respect to this basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the quadratic form
\psi:=2\theta\cong\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
now becomes
\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
-2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
.
The claim now follows because
\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
-2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&0\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.

We now calculate the boundary split quadratic linking form.
The symmetrisation of
\psi
is represented by
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&0\\
0&4\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
It follows that the boundary linking form
\partial\lambda
is defined on
\operatorname{coker}(\widehat{\lambda})=(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
, given by
\partial\lambda([x],[y])=x^{T}A^{-1}y
where
A^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{1}{4}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
As in Remark
7.27
, the boundary split quadratic linking form is
\partial\psi([x])=x^{T}A^{-1}\widetilde{Q}A^{-1}x\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
where
[x]\in(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
.
In particular, evaluating on the canonical basis elements of
(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
, we see that
\partial\psi
is determined by the pair of elements
(\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{8})
of
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

In the following proposition, we write automorphisms of
(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
as matrices with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.

Lemma A.2
.

The automorphism group of the quadratic form
\psi:=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
contains
8
elements.
More precisely, in the basis of Lemma
A.1
, we have a bijective correspondence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{1:1}\bigg\{\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\\
0&\varepsilon_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,\bigg|\,\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}\bigg\}\subseteq GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

With respect to this same basis, the image of
\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi))
is

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}(\partial)=\bigg\{\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\\
0&\varepsilon_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,\bigg|\,\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}\bigg\}\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}((\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}).

Proof.

The calculation of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi)
follows from the fact, proved in Lemma
A.1
, that
\psi
is represented by
\widetilde{Q}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&0\\
0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The second statement is a consequence of the definition of
\partial
as
\partial F:=(F^{*})^{-1}
.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

For
(V,\theta)=(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left[\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right])\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})=:Q
, the set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,2\theta)
is trivial.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for
\theta=[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)]
because
\operatorname{bAut}(v)=\operatorname{bAut}(-v)
for every quadratic form
v
.
An automorphism of
(\mathbb{Z}_{4})^{2}
is determined by its values on
e_{1}:=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
and
e_{2}:=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
.
We immediately rule out some automorphisms that do not preserve
\partial\psi
, where
\psi:=2\theta
.
Given
f\in\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi))
, write
f(e_{i})=a_{i}e_{1}+b_{i}e_{2}
with
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, note that since
\partial\lambda(e_{1},e_{2})=0
, Lemma
A.1
implies that

\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}$}=\partial\psi(e_{i})=\partial\psi(f(e_{i}))=\partial\psi(a_{i}e_{1}+b_{i}e_{2})=a_{i}^{2}\partial\psi(e_{1})+b_{i}^{2}\partial\psi(e_{2})+\partial\lambda(e_{1},e_{2})=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{a_{i}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}}{8}$}\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

This implies that
a_{i}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}\equiv 1\text{ mod }8\text{ with }a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
for
i=1,2
and thus
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}=\{0,\pm 1\}.

Using Lemma
A.2
, this implies that
\operatorname{Im}(\partial)=\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi))
.
We deduce that
\operatorname{bAut}(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\psi)
is trivial, as claimed.
∎

A.2.
Extremal normal Euler number calculation for
h=3
.

We prove that for the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\left[\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&2&2\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\right)\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
, the set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,2\theta)
is trivial.

Lemma A.4
.

Set
\psi:=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}).

The form
\psi
is represented by

\widetilde{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}12&32&-16\\
0&22&-22\\
0&0&6\end{pmatrix}.

The cokernel of the adjoint
\widehat{\lambda}
of the symmetrisation
\lambda
of
\psi
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2}
and, for
[x],[y]\in\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2}
the boundary symmetric form
\partial\lambda\colon(\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2})\times(\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2})\mapsto\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is given by

([x],[y])\mapsto x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}y.

The boundary split quadratic form
\partial\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2}\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is given on the generators by

\partial\psi\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)=\frac{11}{16},\ \ \ \ \ \partial\psi\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{2},\ \ \ \ \ \partial\psi\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Write
Q:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
A:=Q+Q^{T}
.
Performing row and column operations on
A
, one gets
\operatorname{coker}(A)=\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2}
with generators given by the classes of
(1,0,0),(0,1,1)
and
(2,0,1)
.
With respect to this generating set, the boundary symmetric linking form
\partial\lambda([x],[y])=xA^{-1}y
is isometric to the pairing

([x],[y])\mapsto x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&-\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2}&0&-\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}y\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

We now simplify this form by performing isometries that lift to
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
Namely, we set
B:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&2\\
0&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and
C:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
-1&1&0\\
-1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Write
D:=BC
.
The linking form
\partial\lambda
is isometric to the pairing

([x],[y])\mapsto x^{T}D^{T}A^{-1}Dy=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}y.

The matrix
\widetilde{Q}
is obtained by using the defining relation of
Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
:

M:=D^{-1}QD^{-T}=\begin{pmatrix}12&20&-12\\
12&22&-14\\
-4&-8&6\end{pmatrix}\sim\begin{pmatrix}12&32&-16\\
0&22&-22\\
0&0&6\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{Q}.

The statement about the boundary symmetric linking form now follows by construction, so we conclude by studying the boundary quadratic linking form.
The symmetrisation of
\widetilde{Q}
is
G:=D^{-1}AD^{-T}
.
As noted in Remark
7.27
, the values for the boundary quadratic linking form on the generators follow by looking at the diagonal entries of the matrix
G^{-1}MG^{-1}=D^{T}(A^{-1}QA^{-1})D
.
These diagonal entries are precisely
\frac{11}{16},\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

As explained in
[
32
, Theorem 3.6]
, there is a canonical isomorphism

(29)

\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong\frac{\bigg\{A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&4b&4c\\
d&e&f\\
g&h&i\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{3}(\mathbb{Z})\,\bigg|\,A\text{ mod }2\in GL_{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\bigg\}}{\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}8a&8b&8c\\
2d&2e&2f\\
2g&2h&2i\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\bigg|\,a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h,i\in\mathbb{Z}\bigg\}}.

From now on we use this isomorphism implicitly.

Lemma A.5
.

Set
\psi:=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}).

The group
\operatorname{Aut}(\psi)
has
48
elements.
More precisely, using the basis from Lemma
A.4
, the image of
\partial\colon\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi)\to\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi))
decomposes as
\operatorname{Im}(\partial)=X_{0}\sqcup Y_{0}
where

X_{0}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&1&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}\\

has
12
elements and
Y_{0}
has
36=12\cdot 3
elements:

\displaystyle Y_{0}=\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
0&1&1\\
1&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
0&0&1\\
1&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
0&0&1\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
0&1&1\\
1&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
0&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
1&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
1&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
1&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
1&0&1\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
1&1&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
1&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
1&1&0\\
1&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
1&1&1\\
1&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&4\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&4&0\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
1&1&1\\
1&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&0&4\\
1&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Biggr\}.

Proof.

Write
Q:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
so that the symmetrisation
\lambda
of
\psi
is represented by
A=Q+Q^{T}
.
We used Sage
5
5

5

Specifically we entered
Q=\text{QuadraticForm}(\text{ZZ},3,[12,32,-16,22,-22,6])
and then
Q.\text{automorphisms}()
to list the automorphisms.
The relevant documentation can be found here
https://doc.sagemath.org/html/en/reference/quadratic_forms/sage/quadratic_forms/quadratic_form.html
.

to list all the elements of
\{M\mid M^{T}AM=A\}
, which is in bijective correspondence with

\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi)=\operatorname{Aut}(V,\lambda)
.
Taking the inverse transpose of these matrices and reducing the coefficients according to (
29
) leads to the claimed determination of
\operatorname{Im}(\partial)
.
∎

Proposition A.6
.

For
(V,\theta)=\left(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\left[\pm\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\right)\in Q_{+}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
, the set
\operatorname{bAut}(V,2\theta)
is trivial.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for
\theta=\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&4&4\\
0&2&2\\
0&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]
because
\operatorname{bAut}(v)=\operatorname{bAut}(-v)
for every quadratic form
v
.
Write
\psi:=2\theta
and
\lambda
for the symmetrisation of
\psi
.
Recall that
\partial\psi
(resp.
\partial\lambda
) denotes the boundary quadratic linking form (resp. boundary symmetric linking form) of
\psi
and
\lambda
.
Recall also that
\partial\psi(x+y)=\partial\psi(x)+\partial\psi(y)+\partial\lambda(x,y)
and
\partial\psi(rx)=r^{2}\partial\psi(x)
.
Using these facts as well as the calculation of
\partial\lambda
from Lemma
A.4
, we see that for
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
we have

(30)

\partial\psi(a,b,c)=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{11}{16}$}a^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(b^{2}+c^{2})+\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}bc=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{11a^{2}+8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)}{16}$}.

Now we begin our study of the automorphisms of
\partial\psi
.
Such an automorphism
f
is determined by its images on the canonical generators of
\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

•

For
(a,b,c):=f(1,0,0)
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{8},b,c\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the calculation in (
30
) gives

\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{11}{16}$}=\partial\psi(1,0,0)=\partial\psi(f(1,0,0))=\partial\psi(a,b,c)=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{11a^{2}+8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)}{16}$}\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

In other words we have

11\equiv 11a^{2}+8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)\text{ mod }16.

We also know that
f(1,0,0)
must have order
8
.
It follows that
a=\pm 1
or
\pm 3
.
If
a=\pm 1
then we have the equation
8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)\equiv 0
mod
16
which implies
b=c=0
.
If
a=\pm 3
then we have the equation
8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)\equiv 8
mod
16
which implies
(b,c)\neq(0,0)
.
Putting this all together, we deduce that

f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\bigg\}\sqcup\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3\\
x\\
y\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\Big|\,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
y\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Big\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Big\}\bigg\}.

•

For
(a,b,c):=f(0,1,0)
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{8},b,c\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the same reasoning as above (but this time using
\partial\psi(0,1,0)=\frac{1}{2}
) leads to the equation

8\equiv 11a^{2}+8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)\text{ mod }16.

Since
f(0,1,0)
must have order
2
we obtain that
a\in\{0,4\}
.
Since
a^{2}\equiv 0
mod
16
for both
a=0
and
a=4
, we obtain the equation

8\equiv 8(b^{2}+c^{2}+bc)\text{ mod }16.

This implies that
(b,c)\neq(0,0)
.
Putting this all together, we deduce that

f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\bigg|\,x\in\{0,4\}\bigg\}.

•

The exact same reasoning shows that

f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\bigg|\,x\in\{0,4\}\bigg\}.

Combining these calculations, we have proved that

\operatorname{Im}(\partial)\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi))\subseteq X\sqcup Y\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2})

where
X
is the following set with
(2\cdot 2\cdot 2)\cdot 6=48
elements

X=\bigg\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&1&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&b&c\\
0&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\bigg|\,b,c\in\{0,4\}\bigg\},\\

and
Y
is the following set with
(3\cdot 2\cdot 2\cdot 2)\cdot 6=144
elements

(31)

Y=\Biggl\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&1&0\\
g&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&1&1\\
g&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&0&1\\
g&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\\
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&0&1\\
g&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&1&1\\
g&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 3&b&c\\
d&1&0\\
g&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\bigg|\,b,c\in\{0,4\},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d\\
g\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Big\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\Big\}\Biggr\}.

We are justified in writing these automorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}_{8}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as matrices because the entries
b
and
c
are divisible by
4
and because the reductions mod
2
have nonzero determinant; see (
29
).

Using the basis from Lemma
A.4
, elements of
\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi))
must additionally preserve the linking form

([x],[y])\mapsto x^{T}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)y.

Tedious but explicit matrix calculations (available upon request) show that of the
192
elements of
X\sqcup Y
only those in
\operatorname{Im}(\partial)
(as listed in Lemma
A.5
) preserve the linking form.
For example

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&4&0\\
1&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{T}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&4&0\\
1&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{3}{8}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{3}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}).

We conclude that
\operatorname{Im}(\partial)=\operatorname{Aut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}^{3},\psi))
and this concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎
